## 182_united_states_v_butler

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Striking down the Agricultural Adjustment Act's processing tax 6--3, the Court adopted Hamilton's view that the Spending Clause confers an independent power to tax and spend for the general welfare, yet held that Congress may not use that power to coerce or purchase compliance with regulation of local agricultural production reserved to the states (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/297/1/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the First Circuit by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued on December 9--10, 1935, and decided on January 6, 1936 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/297/1/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/297/1}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Roberts delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Hughes and Justices Van Devanter, McReynolds, Sutherland, and Butler.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Stone filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Brandeis and Cardozo.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Condition Invalid): Clawing Back Existing Funds to Force a Runoff Cap.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Standing to Challenge the Whole Scheme): An Earmarked Fee That Funds Production Payments.} Congress imposes a per-ton fee on dairy processors, deposits the proceeds in a special fund, and directs the Secretary of Agriculture to use the fund only to pay dairy farmers who agree to cut milk output. A processor refuses to pay and argues that the payment program is unconstitutional. The Government responds that, under \textit{Massachusetts v.\ Mellon}, a payer has no standing to object to how federal money is spent. Under \textit{Butler}'s standing holding, that defense fails: the fee is earmarked, rises and falls with the payment program, and is an integral part of a single regulatory plan, so the processor may challenge the validity of the entire scheme. (The merits would now be judged under modern Commerce and Spending Clause doctrine rather than \textit{Butler}'s reserved-powers bar.)

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Condition Valid): A New, Optional, Germane Grant.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Spending Upheld): Direct Payments for a National Purpose Outside Other Enumerated Powers.} Congress appropriates money from the general Treasury to pay monthly stipends to individuals who complete apprenticeship training, finding that a national shortage of skilled workers depresses the economy as a whole. A taxpayer objects that job training is not within any enumerated power. \textit{Butler} itself defeats the objection: the Court adopted the Hamiltonian view that the power to spend for the general welfare is not limited by the other direct grants of legislative power. Because the payments impose no regulation on states and the revenue is not earmarked from a particular group, the program falls within the line of \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/301/619/}{\textit{Helvering v.\ Davis}, 301 U.S.\ 619 (1937)}, which defers to Congress's judgment of the general welfare unless it is plainly arbitrary.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Withholding a Larger Share of Existing Highway Funds.} Congress provides that any state that does not adopt a federally specified distracted-driving law will lose 25 percent of its existing federal highway funds, an amount equal to about 2 percent of a typical state's budget. The condition is unambiguous and related to the purpose of highway spending, so it satisfies \textit{Dole}'s notice and relatedness requirements. The hard question is coercion, the principle \textit{Butler} first announced. The penalty is far larger than the 5 percent of highway funds upheld in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/483/203/}{\textit{South Dakota v.\ Dole}, 483 U.S.\ 203 (1987)}, but far smaller than the loss of all Medicaid funds held coercive in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/567/519/}{\textit{NFIB v.\ Sebelius}, 567 U.S.\ 519 (2012)}. Because \textit{NFIB} declined to fix where pressure turns into compulsion, the result is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 183_south_dakota_v_dole

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Upholding 7--2 a federal law withholding 5\% of highway funds from States with a drinking age under 21, the Court set the test for conditional spending: conditions must serve the general welfare, be unambiguous, relate to the federal interest, violate no independent constitutional bar, and not coerce (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/483/203/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Eighth Circuit by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued on April 28, 1987, and decided on June 23, 1987 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/483/203/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Rehnquist delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices White, Marshall, Blackmun, Powell, Stevens, and Scalia.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Brennan filed a dissenting opinion (483 U.S. at 212). Justice O'Connor filed a dissenting opinion (483 U.S. at 212--218).
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Condition Valid): New EV-Corridor Grants with a Building-Code Condition.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Condition Valid): A Blood-Alcohol Limit Tied to Highway Funds.} Suppose Congress directs the Secretary of Transportation to withhold 5\% of specified federal highway funds from any State that does not adopt a 0.08 blood-alcohol limit for drunk-driving offenses. The statute states the condition plainly, and it is even more tightly related to the federal interest in safe interstate travel than the drinking-age condition in \textit{Dole}, because it regulates driving itself. A State may constitutionally adopt such a limit, so there is no independent constitutional bar, and the amount at stake is the same ``relatively mild encouragement'' the Court approved. Under \textit{Dole}'s test the condition is valid even if Congress could not impose the limit directly (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/483/203/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Condition Invalid): Threatening All Existing Education Funds.}

Relatedness is also doubtful:

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Condition Invalid): Inducing Racial Discrimination.} Congress offers a modest new grant to state university systems on the condition that each participating university reserve a fixed number of admission seats for applicants of a single race. The grant is small and optional, so there is no coercion problem, and the condition is related to the federal interest in higher education. It fails anyway under \textit{Dole}'s fourth prong: the independent-constitutional-bar limit forbids Congress from using federal funds ``to induce the States to engage in activities that would themselves be unconstitutional,'' and the Court named invidious racial discrimination as its example. Because a State could not itself adopt a racial quota consistent with the Equal Protection Clause, Congress cannot pay it to do so.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): A 25\% Education-Fund Condition.} Congress amends an existing federal K--12 grant program to withhold 25\% of each State's existing funds unless the State bans student cell phones during the school day in all public schools. The condition is clear, plausibly serves the general welfare, and relates to the program's educational purpose, and a phone ban violates no one's constitutional rights. The difficulty is coercion. The threatened amount is far larger than the 5\% highway cut in \textit{Dole} but smaller than the loss of all Medicaid funds in \textit{NFIB}, and it attaches to pre-existing funds rather than a new program. Neither decision supplies a metric for the space between them, so whether this is ``relatively mild encouragement'' or ``a gun to the head'' is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 184_haaland_v_brackeen

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Rejecting a multi-front attack on the Indian Child Welfare Act, the Court held 7--2 that ICWA falls within Congress's Article~I power over Indian affairs, that its custody rules do not commandeer the States, and that the individual challengers and Texas lacked standing to press equal protection claims against federal officials (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/599/21-376/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court decided the case by a \textbf{7--2} vote. It was argued on November 9, 2022, and decided on June 15, 2023 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/599/21-376/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/21-376}{Cornell LII}). Disposition:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Barrett delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Sotomayor, Kagan, Gorsuch, Kavanaugh, and Jackson.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Gorsuch filed a concurring opinion, joined by Justices Sotomayor and Jackson as to Parts I and III. Justice Kavanaugh filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Thomas and Justice Alito each filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Gorsuch

Justice Kavanaugh

Justice Thomas

Justice Alito
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Statute Upheld): A Federal Rule for Tribal Members' Estates.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Standing): An Adoption Agency Sues the Secretary.} A private adoption agency that places children with non-Indian foster families sues the Secretary of the Interior in federal district court, alleging that ICWA's foster-care preferences in \S~1915(b) disadvantage its families on the basis of race, and seeks a declaratory judgment and an injunction against the Secretary. Under \textit{Brackeen}, the suit must be dismissed for lack of redressability: state courts and state agencies, not the Secretary, apply the placement preferences, so a judgment against the Secretary would not bind the state judges who decide each placement. That a favorable federal opinion might persuade state courts does not count, because it is the judgment, not the opinion, that redresses an injury. The agency's families must raise the claim as parties in a state-court custody proceeding, as Justice Kavanaugh's concurrence noted.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Statute Invalid): A Nationwide Child-Welfare Mandate Under the Interstate Commerce Clause.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Provision Likely Invalid): A Search Duty Imposed Only on State Agencies.} Amending ICWA itself, Congress requires each State's child-welfare agency, and no other party, to complete a documented nationwide search for extended-family and tribal placements before any Indian child may enter foster care, and to file monthly search reports with the Secretary of the Interior. Congress's Article~I power over Indian affairs still covers the subject, so the challenge turns on the Tenth Amendment. \textit{Brackeen} upheld \S~1912 because it binds ``any party'' evenhandedly and upheld \S~1915 because, under \textit{Adoptive Couple v. Baby Girl}, it imposes no search duty on state agencies. A duty addressed only to state executive agencies, with reporting by agency officials rather than ancillary court recordkeeping, is the kind of direct command to state officers that \textit{Printz} and \textit{Murphy} forbid, so the State would likely prevail.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Undecided): An Equal Protection Challenge Raised in State Court.} Non-Indian foster parents seek to adopt a child who is eligible for membership in one tribe; a family belonging to a different tribe asks for the placement under \S~1915(a)'s ``other Indian families'' preference. In the state adoption proceeding, where the preference is actually applied, the foster parents argue that it is a racial classification barred by \textit{Palmore v. Sidoti}, 466 U.S. 429 (1984). Their standing is secure, so a court must reach the merits \textit{Brackeen} left open. The tribe answers that, under \textit{Morton v. Mancari}, 417 U.S. 535 (1974), classifications tied to tribal membership are political, not racial. The foster parents reply that a preference for families of any tribe looks less like a political tie to the child's own tribe and more like ancestry. Justice Kavanaugh called the issue ``serious,'' and the Court has not resolved it.
```

## 185_chisholm_v_georgia

### Memory Jogger

```latex
In the Court's first major constitutional decision, a South Carolina executor sued Georgia on a Revolutionary War debt; the Justices held 4--1, in seriatim opinions, that Article III lets a citizen of one State sue another State without its consent---a ruling the Eleventh Amendment promptly overturned (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/2/419/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court, exercising original jurisdiction, ruled for the plaintiff by a \textbf{4--1} vote and ordered that default judgment be entered unless Georgia appeared or showed cause. The case was argued on February 5, 1793, and decided on February 18, 1793 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/2/419/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority (Seriatim Opinions):} Chief Justice Jay and Justices Cushing, Wilson, and Blair each delivered a separate opinion; there was no single opinion of the Court.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None apart from the seriatim majority opinions.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Iredell dissented alone.
\end{itemize}

Five Justices participated, and the seriatim opinions were delivered in reverse order of seniority.
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{The Eleventh Amendment:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Immunity Bar): The State as Plaintiff.} A Texas supplier sells generators to Louisiana and then refuses to honor its warranty. Flip the posture of a typical immunity case:

. The Eleventh Amendment reaches only suits ``commenced or prosecuted against'' a State, so Article III's grant over controversies between a State and citizens of another State still operates when the State is the one suing, just as Georgia was suing South Carolina citizens in \emph{Georgia v.\ Brailsford} while claiming immunity in \emph{Chisholm}.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Relief Available): Prospective Relief Against a State Officer.} After Louisiana's legislature passes a statute cancelling its outstanding contracts with out-of-state suppliers, a Texas supplier sues the state treasurer in federal court, seeking an injunction against enforcement of the statute as a violation of the Contracts Clause. The suit may proceed: under \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/209/123/}{\emph{Ex parte Young}, 209 U.S.\ 123 (1908)}, a federal court may order a state officer to stop violating federal law prospectively, and the officer cannot shelter behind the State's immunity. This preserves part of the \emph{Chisholm} majority's concern that a State not escape its contracts without judicial accountability. The supplier cannot, however, obtain an order requiring payment of the past debt from the state treasury.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Suit Barred): An Unpaid Contract Debt in Federal Court.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Suit Barred): Federal Wage Claims in the State's Own Courts.} State probation officers in Maine sue the State in a Maine state court, seeking back overtime pay owed under the federal Fair Labor Standards Act. They argue that the Eleventh Amendment, which speaks only to ``the Judicial power of the United States,'' says nothing about state courts. Under \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/527/706/}{\emph{Alden v.\ Maine}, 527 U.S.\ 706 (1999)}, the suit is barred: Congress may not use its Article I powers to subject a nonconsenting State to private damages suits in its own courts. The result rests on the structural view of state sovereignty traced to Justice Iredell's dissent, not on the Amendment's text that the \emph{Chisholm} majority's textualism would have emphasized.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Is a State-Created Hospital Authority an ``Arm of the State''?} A State creates a public hospital authority as a separate corporation. The Governor appoints its board, and most of its budget comes from annual state appropriations, but state law says the State is not legally liable for the authority's debts. An out-of-state vendor sues the authority in federal court for unpaid invoices. If the authority is an arm of the State, the Eleventh Amendment bars the suit; if it is more like a city or county, it is not immune. State control and funding point toward immunity, but the State treasury's freedom from legal liability for its judgments---the factor the Court stressed in \emph{Hess v.\ Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp.}, 513 U.S.\ 30 (1994)---points the other way, so the outcome is uncertain.
```


## 186_pike_v_bruce_church

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Arizona ordered a grower to pack its premium Parker cantaloupes in Arizona rather than in its California sheds 31 miles away, so the melons would carry Arizona's name; a unanimous Court struck the order under the \textit{Pike} balancing test, which invalidates evenhanded laws whose burden on interstate commerce is clearly excessive in relation to their putative local benefits (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/397/137/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{affirmed} the three-judge District Court's permanent injunction, \textbf{8--0}, on direct appeal. The case was argued on January 13, 1970, and decided on March 2, 1970 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/397/137/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/397/137}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Stewart delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justices Black, Douglas, Harlan, Brennan, White, and Marshall.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Threshold Rejection of the ``Intrastate Preparation'' Defense}

\subsubsection{The \textit{Pike} Balancing Framework}

\subsubsection{Minimal Local Interest vs. Forced In-State Operations}

\item \textbf{\textit{National Pork Producers Council v.\ Ross} (2023):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Pike Violated): Forced In-State Data Servers.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Pike Violated): Requiring Shrimp to Be Processed In-State.} A coastal State requires that shrimp caught in its waters be peeled and the heads and hulls removed at in-state plants before the shrimp may be shipped out of state, asserting an interest in conserving the shell waste as fertilizer. A packing company with efficient processing plants just across the border challenges the law. Like Arizona's order, the law requires an operation that could be performed more efficiently elsewhere to be performed at home---the burden \textit{Pike} called virtually per se illegal. \textit{Pike} relied on \textit{Foster-Fountain Packing Co.\ v.\ Haydel}, 278 U.S.\ 1 (1928), which struck down a materially identical Louisiana shrimp law, and the State's interest could be served with far less impact on interstate activity.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (State Prevails): A Neutral Packaging Ban.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (State Prevails): A City Smoke-Abatement Ordinance Applied to Ships.} A port city's air-pollution ordinance limits the density of smoke that any furnace or boiler within city limits may emit, and the city applies it to the boilers of a cargo vessel docked there while loading goods for interstate voyages. The shipowner must refit its boilers and argues that the ordinance burdens interstate commerce. The ordinance regulates evenhandedly, serves a legitimate local health interest, and has only incidental effects on commerce; it requires no operation to be relocated in-state. That is the pattern \textit{Pike} cited from \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/362/440/}{\textit{Huron Portland Cement Co.\ v.\ Detroit}, 362 U.S.\ 440 (1960)}, which upheld exactly such an ordinance, and the burden is not clearly excessive in relation to the benefit.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call After \textit{Ross}): A Sales Ban on Milk From Hormone-Treated Cows.} State D bans the in-state sale of milk from cows treated with a federally approved growth hormone, citing contested health concerns and consumer preference. The rule applies equally to in-state dairies, but because most of the State's milk comes from out-of-state cooperatives, they must build segregated supply chains at a cost of hundreds of millions of dollars. No forced in-state processing is involved, so the case turns on balancing. Under \textit{Ross}, three Justices would refuse to weigh economic costs against such noneconomic interests, four found that comparable compliance costs did not plausibly allege a substantial burden on the interstate market, and others would apply \textit{Pike} balancing to an industry-wide burden. With the Court so divided, the outcome is uncertain.
```

## 187_south_central_timber_v_wunnicke

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Alaska sold state timber on condition that buyers process it in Alaska before export; the Court held that a parallel federal-lands policy was not ``unmistakably clear'' congressional authorization, and a plurality held that the market-participant exception does not let a State impose conditions that regulate a downstream market it does not participate in (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/467/82/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} the Ninth Circuit by a \textbf{6--2} vote, with one Justice not participating. The case was argued on February 29, 1984, and decided on May 22, 1984 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/467/82/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/467/82}{Cornell LII}):
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Part II (Opinion of the Court): ``Unmistakably Clear'' Congressional Authorization}

\subsubsection{Part III (Plurality Opinion): The Downstream Market-Participant Boundary}

\subsubsection{Part IV (Plurality Opinion): Per Se Invalidity and Foreign Commerce}

Justice Brennan

Justice Powell, joined by Chief Justice Burger,

Justice Rehnquist, joined by Justice O'Connor,

\item \textbf{The ``Unmistakably Clear'' Canon Applied:}

\item \textbf{The Downstream Limit in Later Market-Participant Cases:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Invalid Downstream Mandate): In-State Refining of State-Land Lithium.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Invalid Downstream Mandate): Resale Restrictions on State-Made Cement.} A State that operates its own cement plant sells cement to private distributors on condition that they resell it only to customers within the State, even after title and payment have passed. Out-of-state builders who would buy from the distributors challenge the condition. The State may choose its own customers, as \textit{Reeves} allowed, but here it reaches past its sale to control the buyers' later dealings in a resale market where the State is not a participant. The plurality identified exactly this kind of post-sale restriction as one of the features absent from \textit{Reeves}, and a resale ban that walls off the State's market for local benefit is the kind of downstream regulation \textit{Wunnicke} forbids.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Valid Market Participation): A State-Owned Refinery Choosing Its Customers.} Return to the lithium hypothetical, but suppose Nevada instead

. Nevada is now acting as a seller in the very market it has entered and is simply choosing its customers, the conduct \textit{Reeves} protected when South Dakota sold cement from its own plant to residents first. No condition follows the lithium after sale into a market Nevada does not participate in, so the market-participant exception applies and the Dormant Commerce Clause does not bar the preference.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Valid Market Participation): Cash Subsidies Instead of Export Conditions.} Alternatively, suppose Nevada

. Lessees remain free to ship raw ore anywhere, so the State has not stripped buyers of their choice, the feature that distinguished \textit{Alexandria Scrap} from Alaska's contract clause. The subsidy may favor local refiners, but a direct payment from the State's own funds is not a regulation of the downstream market, and the plurality noted that such alternatives remain open to a State.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Requiring Resident Logging Crews on State Land.} Alaska sells standing timber on state land and requires that the buyer use crews made up of Alaska residents to cut and haul the timber off state land; once the logs leave state land, the buyer may process and ship them anywhere. Alaska argues that, as in \textit{White}, the workers are effectively working on a state project before the sale is complete. The challenger replies that the condition regulates the separate labor market for logging services. Because the plurality defined the market by intuition without an objective test, and because Part~III never commanded a majority, it is unclear whether harvesting on state land falls inside the timber-sales market or downstream of it.
```

## 188_garcia_v_samta

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Overruling \textit{National League of Cities v. Usery}, the Court held 5--4 that a city transit authority must obey the Fair Labor Standards Act's wage and overtime rules: ``traditional governmental functions'' immunity is unworkable, and the States are protected mainly by the political process, not by judicially defined enclaves of sovereignty (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/469/528/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{5--4} vote, expressly overruling \textit{National League of Cities v. Usery}. The case was argued March 19, 1984, reargued October 1, 1984, and decided February 19, 1985 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/469/528/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Blackmun delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Brennan, White, Marshall, and Stevens.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Powell filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justices Rehnquist and O'Connor (469 U.S.\ at 557--579). Justice Rehnquist filed a dissenting opinion (469 U.S.\ at 579--580). Justice O'Connor filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Powell and Rehnquist (469 U.S.\ at 580--589).
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Unworkability and Democratic Flaws of ``Traditional Governmental Functions''}

\subsubsection{The Political Safeguards of Federalism}

\item \textbf{Reaffirmed for Generally Applicable Rules:}

\item \textbf{Limits on Enforcing the FLSA Against States:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Generally Applicable Rule Valid): Bus-Fleet Electrification.}

As to Section~1:

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Generally Applicable Rule Valid): Federal Shift Caps for Police and Firefighters.}

Under the overruled \textit{National League of Cities} framework and the \textit{Garcia} dissents, by contrast:

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Commandeering): State DMVs as Federal Enforcers.} Same statute, but a State challenges Section~2.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Sovereign Immunity): Private Overtime Suits Against a State in Its Own Courts.} Congress amends the FLSA to authorize state employees to sue their nonconsenting State for back overtime and liquidated damages in the State's own courts. A group of state prison guards sues, and the State moves to dismiss. Under \textit{Garcia}, the guards are covered: the FLSA's wage and overtime rules validly bind the State as an employer. But \textit{Garcia} answers only whether Congress may impose the obligation, not how it may be enforced. Under \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/527/706/}{\textit{Alden v. Maine}}, 527 U.S. 706 (1999), Congress's Article~I powers cannot subject a nonconsenting State to private damages suits in its own courts, so the suit must be dismissed. The guards must look to a suit by the Secretary of Labor, injunctive relief, or the State's consent.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Rules Aimed Only at States): Federal Pay and Hours Rules for State Judicial Staff.} Invoking the Commerce Clause, Congress sets minimum salaries and maximum weekly hours for state-court law clerks and legislative staff, positions that exist only in state government; no private employer is covered. \textit{Garcia} points toward validity: it rejected judicial enclaves of state immunity and left the States to protect themselves in Congress, and the statute regulates the States as employers rather than ordering them to legislate or enforce federal law, so it does not commandeer. But the statute is not generally applicable, which is the question \textit{Reno} expressly reserved, and it reaches the internal staffing of state courts and legislatures, the kind of core state structure for which \textit{Gregory v. Ashcroft}, 501 U.S. 452 (1991), demands an unmistakably clear statement. Whether a regulation aimed solely at States' own governing institutions survives is unsettled.
```

## 189_crosby_v_nftc

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A unanimous Court held that Massachusetts's law barring state purchases from companies doing business with Burma was preempted under the Supremacy Clause because it obstructed the Federal Burma Act's grant of flexible presidential discretion, its calibrated sanctions, and its call for multilateral diplomacy (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/363/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{affirmed} the First Circuit, \textbf{9--0}, solely on statutory preemption grounds. The case was argued on March 22, 2000, and decided on June 19, 2000 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/363/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-474.ZO.html}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Souter delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Scalia filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justice Thomas.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Holding

```latex
\textbf{Question Presented:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Preempted): A Broader, Unwaivable State Contracting Ban.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Preempted): A State Law Matching Federal Scope but Lacking the Waiver.} Congress bars U.S.\ persons from making new investments in a foreign country's mining sector and authorizes the President to waive the ban whenever he certifies that doing so serves national security. A State enacts a law imposing the same ban on the same class of persons and the same conduct, but with no waiver and no termination clause. The President later waives the federal ban as part of a negotiated agreement. Even though the state law tracks Congress's calibrated scope, it still conflicts with the first pillar of \textit{Crosby}: because it operates with no mechanism to yield to presidential waivers, it strips the President of control over the economic pressure Congress placed in his hands and reduces the value of his bargaining chips. The state law is preempted to the extent it outlasts the waiver.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Survives): Congress Expressly Authorizes Matching State Divestment.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Crosby Inapplicable): A State Boycott With No Federal Statute in the Field.} A State bars its agencies from buying goods from companies that do business with a foreign country accused of human-rights abuses. Congress has enacted no sanctions statute concerning that country, and the President has issued no executive order or agreement addressing it. A trade association sues, relying on \textit{Crosby}. Obstacle preemption under \textit{Crosby} requires a federal statute whose purposes and objectives the state law frustrates; here there is no federal statute to obstruct, so \textit{Crosby}'s holding does not invalidate the law. The challengers must instead press the theories \textit{Crosby} expressly reserved, the dormant foreign affairs power under \textit{Zschernig v.\ Miller} and the dormant Foreign Commerce Clause, each of which raises open questions the Court did not decide.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): A State Pension Divestment Law That Tracks the Federal List Without Congressional Authorization.} Congress imposes sanctions on a foreign country's defense firms and lets the President add or remove firms from a Treasury list, but says nothing about state measures. A State requires its pension funds to divest from firms on the Treasury list and to reinvest automatically when a firm is delisted. For the State: unlike Massachusetts's law, its statute adds no new targets, follows every federal designation, and expires when federal sanctions do, preserving the President's bargaining chips and Congress's calibration. For the challengers: the State still adds economic pressure Congress did not impose, foreign governments may protest, and \textit{Crosby} held that congressional silence is ambiguous rather than permission. Whether a court treats close federal tracking as eliminating any obstacle is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 190_marsh_v_alabama

### Memory Jogger

```latex
When a private corporation owns and runs an entire company town open to the public, it performs a public function and is bound by the First and Fourteenth Amendments; Alabama therefore could not convict a Jehovah's Witness of trespass for leafletting on the town's business-block sidewalk (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/326/501/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{5--3} vote. The case was argued on December 6, 1945, and decided on January 7, 1946 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/326/501/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Opinion of the Court:} Justice Black delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Douglas, Murphy, and Rutledge; Justice Frankfurter joined except as to its Commerce Clause analogy.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Frankfurter filed a concurring opinion (326 U.S. at 510--11).
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Reed filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Chief Justice Stone and Justice Burton (326 U.S. at 511--17).
    \item \textbf{Not Participating:} Justice Jackson took no part in the consideration or decision of the case.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Shopping Centers: Expansion and Overruling (1968--1980):}

\item \textbf{The ``Traditionally Exclusively'' Requirement (1974--2001):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (State Action Found): The Corporate ``Tech Town.''}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (State Action Found): A Private Company That Runs the County's Elections.} A county contracts with a private corporation to conduct its municipal elections from start to finish: the company qualifies candidates for the ballot, staffs the polls, counts the votes, and certifies the winners. The company refuses to place on the ballot any candidate who has criticized its parent firm. A disqualified candidate sues under the First and Fourteenth Amendments. Running public elections is one of the very few functions the post-\textit{Marsh} cases treat as traditionally and exclusively reserved to the State, as the white-primary decision in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/345/461/}{\textit{Terry v.\ Adams}, 345 U.S.\ 461 (1953)}, illustrates. As in \textit{Marsh}, a State cannot hand a governmental function to a private entity and thereby free it from constitutional limits, so the company is a state actor.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No State Action): The Dominant Social-Media Platform.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Federal State Action): The Open-Air Lifestyle Shopping Center.}

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): The Developer-Owned New Town.} A developer builds and continues to own an unincorporated, ungated community of 20,000 residents in which it holds title to every street, sidewalk, park, and the central shopping district, and it employs private security officers who enforce its rules; but the county supplies police, fire protection, water, and public schools. The developer bars petition circulators from its town-center sidewalks. The streets are open to the public and the town looks like the Chickasaw business block, which favors applying \textit{Marsh}. Yet \textit{Hudgens} confined \textit{Marsh} to private property with all the attributes of a town, and here the core municipal services remain public. Because the Court has never said how many municipal attributes are enough, a court could reasonably go either way.
```

```latex
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Vinson delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Black, Frankfurter, Douglas, Murphy, and Burton.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Not Participating:} Justices Reed, Jackson, and Rutledge took no part in the consideration or decision of the cases.
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Enforcement Barred): A Religious Covenant Used to Divest Title.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Enforcement Barred): Damages Against the Selling Owner.} A recorded subdivision covenant bars sale to persons of a particular national origin. An owner sells to a willing buyer of that origin, and the neighbors, rather than seeking to evict the buyer, sue the seller in state court for breach of the covenant and ask for money damages. Under \textit{Shelley}, a state court that lends its coercive power to a discriminatory private agreement is itself acting for the State, and \textit{Barrows v. Jackson}, 346 U.S. 249 (1953), applied that reasoning to damages actions because the threat of liability would pressure owners to refuse willing buyers. The court must deny relief; the Equal Protection Clause reaches national-origin classifications as well as race (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/334/1/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Constitutional Violation): Excluding a Guest From One's Own Home.}

Unlike in \textit{Shelley}, no willing owner is being overridden: the State's trespass law enforces the owner's own choice about her own home, and

. Reading \textit{Shelley} to reach this case would turn every invocation of neutral property law into constitutional state action.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No State Action): Enforcing a Non-Disparagement Clause.}

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Testamentary Condition Based on a Beneficiary's Spouse.} A will leaves a large bequest to the testator's grandson on the condition that he marry a person of the testator's own faith within seven years; otherwise the money passes to a charity. The grandson marries outside the faith and asks the state probate court to strike the condition, arguing that judicial enforcement is state action under \textit{Shelley}. He can point out that the court's decree would give legal force to a private religious exclusion. But no willing seller and willing buyer are being overridden: the court would simply carry out the testator's own disposition of his own property under neutral probate rules, the posture that \textit{Evans v. Abney} treated as permissible. Because \textit{Shelley}'s reach beyond restrictive covenants has never been defined, the outcome is uncertain.
```

## 192_jackson_v_metropolitan_edison

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A privately owned, heavily regulated electric monopoly that cut off a customer's power without notice or a hearing was not a state actor: the Court required a close nexus between the State and the specific challenged act, and confined the public-function doctrine to powers traditionally exclusively reserved to the State (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/419/345/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Third Circuit by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued on October 15, 1974, and decided on December 23, 1974 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/419/345/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Rehnquist delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justices Stewart, White, Blackmun, and Powell.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Douglas, Justice Brennan, and Justice Marshall each filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Consolidation (1978--1999):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No State Action): A Medicaid-Funded Nursing Home Transfers a Resident.} A privately owned nursing home, licensed and closely regulated by the State and receiving most of its revenue from Medicaid, decides through its own physicians that a resident needs only a lower level of care and transfers her to another facility without a hearing. The State then reduces her Medicaid benefits to match the new placement. Under \textit{Jackson}, licensing, heavy regulation, and public funding do not make the home's decision the State's; the question is whether the State compelled or put its own weight behind the specific transfer, and here private medical judgment made it. \textit{Blum v. Yaretsky}, 457 U.S. 991 (1982), applied \textit{Jackson} to these facts and found no state action, so the resident has no Fourteenth Amendment due process claim against the home.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No State Action): A Franchised Internet Provider Cancels Service.} A privately owned broadband company holds an exclusive municipal franchise, strings its fiber along public rights-of-way, and is regulated by the state utility commission. It terminates a subscriber's service for disputed late payments without notice, under a termination clause in its filed terms of service that the commission never reviewed. Each of the subscriber's arguments fails as it did in \textit{Jackson}: regulation and a franchise do not supply a nexus to the specific termination; supplying internet service, like supplying electricity, has never been a power traditionally and exclusively reserved to the State; and the commission's passive acceptance of the filed terms does not put the State's weight behind the shutoff. Without state action, the subscriber's remedies lie in contract and state utility law.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (State Action): A Commission-Ordered Shutoff.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Likely State Action): A Utility Condemns an Easement.} The same kind of private electric company, using a power of eminent domain that state law delegates to certificated utilities, files a condemnation proceeding and takes a permanent easement across a homeowner's backyard for a new transmission line. The homeowner sues, claiming the taking was not for public use and that the procedures denied her due process. \textit{Jackson} itself marks this as the contrasting case: the Court said that if Metropolitan were exercising ``some power delegated to it by the State which is traditionally associated with sovereignty, such as eminent domain,'' the case ``would be quite a different one.'' Condemnation is a sovereign power the State has lent to the utility, so the utility's use of it is fairly attributable to the State.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Charter School Unresolved): Charter School vs.\ Prison Clinic.}

The school is the hard case. On one side, education has never been an exclusive state prerogative, and \textit{Rendell-Baker} held that a privately run school funded almost entirely by public money was not a state actor when it fired employees. On the other, a State that labels charter schools as public schools makes them part of its public education system, and controls their charters looks more like the entwined State in \textit{Brentwood}. Because the Court divided evenly in \textit{Drummond}, the outcome is open.
```

### Critique

```latex

\textbf{The Formalist Cliff (\textit{Memphis Light} Contrast):}
```

## 193_shelby_county_v_holder

### Memory Jogger

```latex
An Alabama county challenged the Voting Rights Act's preclearance regime; the Court held 5--4 that the \S~4(b) coverage formula, still based on decades-old data, could no longer justify subjecting some States to \S~5 preclearance, because current burdens must be justified by current needs and the equal sovereignty of the States (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/570/529/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the D.C. Circuit by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on February 27, 2013, and decided on June 25, 2013 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/570/529/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/12-96}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Roberts delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Alito.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Thomas filed a concurring opinion, joining the Court's opinion in full.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Ginsburg filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Breyer, Sotomayor, and Kagan.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Preclearance Inoperable:}

\item \textbf{\emph{Louisiana v. Callais} (2026):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Formula Invalid): A Coverage Formula Pegged to Old History.} Congress enacts a statute requiring every State that used a poll tax in federal elections before 1964 to obtain federal approval before changing its voter-identification rules. The statute rests on that history alone, and the legislative record contains no current data comparing those States with others. Under \emph{Shelby County}, the formula fails: ``history did not end in 1965,'' the Fifteenth Amendment is not designed to punish for the past, and a law that treats States differently must justify its current burdens by current needs. Selecting States by practices long since abolished nationwide is exactly the kind of outdated criterion the Court held could no longer support preclearance.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Formula Invalid): Current Data That Does Not Track the Problem.} Congress responds to \emph{Shelby County} with a formula that uses only current data: any State in which overall turnout fell below 50\% in the most recent presidential election must preclear changes to its voting laws. The data are fresh, but the trigger measures low turnout of all voters rather than racial discrimination in voting. \emph{Shelby County}, applying \emph{Northwest Austin}, requires that a statute's ``disparate geographic coverage is sufficiently related to the problem that it targets.'' Because a turnout trigger could sweep in States with no record of discrimination and omit States with serious problems, a court would likely hold it insufficiently related to the Fifteenth Amendment harm that justifies so drastic a departure from equal sovereignty.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Formula Likely Valid): A Rolling Trigger Based on Adjudicated Violations.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Preclearance by Court Order): Judicial ``Bail-In'' After Intentional Discrimination.} After \emph{Shelby County}, voters sue a formerly covered county, and a federal court finds after trial that the county intentionally moved polling places to make voting harder for Black residents. The court invokes \S~3(c) of the Voting Rights Act, 52 U.S.C.\ \S~10302(c), and orders the county to obtain approval before making further voting changes for a period of years. \emph{Shelby County} does not bar the order: the Court invalidated only the \S~4(b) coverage formula and expressly left \S~5 and \S~2 in place. A bail-in order rests on a present-day judicial finding of a constitutional violation in that jurisdiction, not on decades-old data, so it answers the Court's demand that burdens be justified by current needs.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unresolved): Equal Sovereignty Outside Voting.} A federal statute bars States from authorizing new forms of sports wagering but allows two States that already licensed it before a set date to continue. A State excluded by the cutoff sues, arguing that the law violates the equal sovereignty of the States. \emph{Shelby County} said equal sovereignty ``remains highly pertinent in assessing subsequent disparate treatment of States,'' which supports the challenge. But the Court applied the principle to a law requiring some States to seek federal permission before legislating, and Justice Ginsburg's dissent, relying on \emph{Katzenbach}, read the equal-sovereignty doctrine as limited to the terms on which new States are admitted to the Union. Whether the principle reaches ordinary federal regulation that treats States differently, and what level of justification it demands, is genuinely unsettled.
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Law Upheld): A Temporary, Compensated Pandemic Foreclosure Pause.} During a declared public-health emergency that causes mass layoffs, State W authorizes its courts, on a homeowner's showing of pandemic-related job loss, to postpone a foreclosure sale for up to twelve months, on condition that the homeowner pay the property's court-determined fair rental value toward taxes, insurance, and interest. The law expires when the emergency ends, and the debt, interest, and lender's lien remain intact. Every \textit{Blaisdell} factor favors the State: a genuine emergency, a public rather than private purpose, individualized judicial relief on reasonable conditions, protection of the lender's core investment, and temporary operation. Under the \textit{Energy Reserves} framework that grew out of \textit{Blaisdell}, the measure is a reasonable and appropriate means to a significant public purpose (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/290/398/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Law Upheld): A Notice Rule in a Regulated Industry.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Law Struck Down): Permanent Commercial Rent Forgiveness.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Law Struck Down): An Unconditional Five-Year Redemption Extension.} Facing falling farm prices but no acute crisis, State V extends the redemption period on every existing mortgage foreclosure by five years. Mortgagors need not show hardship or pay rent, no court supervises the extension, and the law has no expiration date. The law lacks each of the guardrails that saved Minnesota's statute: it is not tailored to an emergency, offers no individualized relief, leaves the mortgagee-purchaser uncompensated for lost possession, and is not temporary. That is the pattern struck down in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/295/56/}{\emph{W.\ B.\ Worthen Co.\ v.\ Kavanaugh}, 295 U.S.\ 56 (1935)}, and in the nineteenth-century cases \textit{Blaisdell} distinguished, such as \emph{Bronson v.\ Kinzie}.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): Repealing a Retirees' Cost-of-Living Adjustment.}

The result is genuinely uncertain, however, because a court must first decide whether the COLA statute created a contract at all: statutes are presumed not to create contractual rights unless the legislature clearly intended to bind itself, \textit{National Railroad Passenger Corp.\ v.\ Atchison, Topeka \& Santa Fe Railway Co.}, 470 U.S.\ 451 (1985). If no contract exists, the Contracts Clause challenge fails at the threshold.
```

## 195_mathews_v_eldridge

### Memory Jogger

```latex
The Social Security Administration may cut off disability benefits before holding an evidentiary hearing: procedural due process is flexible, and courts weigh (1) the private interest, (2) the risk of error and value of added safeguards, and (3) the government's interest, here favoring written pre-termination procedures plus a full post-termination hearing (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/424/319/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the Fourth Circuit by a \textbf{6--2} vote. The case was argued on October 6, 1975, and decided on February 24, 1976 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/424/319/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Powell delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justices Stewart, White, Blackmun, and Rehnquist.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Brennan filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Marshall.
    \item \textbf{Not Participating:}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Threshold Property Interest and the Flexible Nature of Due Process}

\subsubsection{Factor One: The Private Interest Affected (SSDI vs. \emph{Goldberg} Welfare)}

\subsubsection{Factor Two: Risk of Erroneous Deprivation and Probable Value of Additional Safeguards}

\subsubsection{Factor Three: The Government's Interest and Institutional Deference}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Post-Deprivation Hearing Suffices): Reducing a Veteran's Disability Rating.} A federal agency reviews a veteran's service-connected disability rating, obtains new reports from his physicians and an agency-ordered examination, and sends him a notice summarizing the medical evidence, giving him sixty days to submit contrary evidence in writing. It then reduces his monthly payment, with a later evidentiary hearing and full retroactive relief if he wins. As in \textit{Eldridge}, the payments are not means-tested, the dispute turns on documented medical findings rather than credibility, and the written notice lets him respond to the agency's reasons. Under the three factors, written pre-reduction procedures plus a prompt post-reduction hearing satisfy due process.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Pre-Suspension Hearing): Suspending an Officer Charged With a Felony.} A state university suspends a campus police officer without pay immediately after he is arrested and formally charged with a drug felony, and gives him a hearing on his status only afterward. The officer's interest in his paycheck is real but temporary. The independent arrest and formal charge by third parties sharply reduce the risk that the suspension is baseless, and the State has a strong interest in not keeping a charged officer in a position of public trust. Applying \textit{Mathews}, the Court held on these facts in \textit{Gilbert v.\ Homar}, 520 U.S.\ 924 (1997), that no pre-suspension hearing was required, leaving for remand whether the later hearing was sufficiently prompt.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Pre-Termination Hearing Required): Cutting Off Subsistence Welfare on a Caseworker's Suspicion.} A state agency terminates a family's means-tested cash assistance after a caseworker concludes, based on a neighbor's report, that an unreported adult is living in the home and contributing income. The family denies it, and the issue turns on whom to believe. This is the \textit{Goldberg} pole that \textit{Eldridge} distinguished: the recipients are at the margin of subsistence, the question turns on lay facts and credibility that written submissions handle poorly, and wrongful interruption may leave them unable to survive while they wait. \textit{Goldberg v.\ Kelly} requires an oral pre-termination evidentiary hearing before benefits stop.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Pre-Termination Opportunity Required): Firing a Tenured Employee Without Notice.} A school board fires a tenured security guard on the day it discovers that he did not disclose a prior conviction on his job application, without telling him the charge or letting him explain. Applying the \textit{Mathews} factors, the Court held in \textit{Cleveland Board of Education v.\ Loudermill}, 470 U.S.\ 532 (1985), that a tenured public employee is entitled before termination to notice of the charges, an explanation of the employer's evidence, and an opportunity to respond. The interest in continued employment is weighty, a short pre-termination exchange may prevent a mistaken firing, and the burden on the employer is slight. A full evidentiary hearing may come afterward.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Algorithmic Medicaid Cuts.}

The case is close because the first factor points toward \textit{Goldberg} while the second and third can be argued either way: whether a generic notice of an algorithmic score lets claimants respond meaningfully before the cut is exactly the question \textit{Eldridge}'s reliance on detailed written notice leaves open.
```

```latex
---

---
```

## 196_yick_wo_v_hopkins

### Memory Jogger

```latex
San Francisco denied laundry permits to every Chinese applicant while granting nearly all non-Chinese applicants; a unanimous Court held that a facially neutral law administered ``with an evil eye and an unequal hand'' violates equal protection, and that the Fourteenth Amendment protects noncitizens as ``persons'' (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/118/356/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{reversed} both the Supreme Court of California (\textit{Yick Wo}) and the U.S.\ Circuit Court (\textit{Wo Lee}), 9--0, remanding with directions to discharge the petitioners. The cases were submitted April 14, 1886, and decided May 10, 1886 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/118/356/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/118/356}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Matthews delivered the opinion of a unanimous Court, joined by Chief Justice Waite and Justices Miller, Field, Bradley, Harlan, Woods, Gray, and Blatchford.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Independent Federal Construction of the Ordinances}

\subsubsection{Distinguishing \emph{Barbier v. Connolly} and \emph{Soon Hing v. Crowley}}

\subsubsection{Universal Textual Scope of the Fourteenth Amendment (Noncitizens as ``Persons'')}

\subsubsection{Constitutional Antipathy to Arbitrary Personal Power}

\subsubsection{Unconstitutional Administration: ``An Evil Eye and an Unequal Hand''}

\item \textbf{Discriminatory Purpose Required (\textit{Washington v. Davis}):}

\item \textbf{Proving Intent (\textit{Arlington Heights}):}

\item \textbf{Selective Prosecution (\textit{Armstrong}):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Discriminatory Administration): Pacifica's ``Fire Resilience Permits.''}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Selective Enforcement of a Neutral Code): Sidewalk-Vending Citations.} A city ordinance bars sidewalk vending within 50 feet of a transit stop. Police records show that over three years officers issued 212 citations under the ordinance, 205 of them to vendors of Somali origin, although city inspectors' own photo surveys document dozens of other vendors at the same stops on the same days who were never cited. The police department offers no explanation for the pattern. As in \textit{Yick Wo}, the law is neutral on its face, but its administration draws a line between similarly situated persons by national origin, and the comparator evidence is concrete and unrebutted. That record is the kind of stark, unexplained pattern from which \textit{Davis} and \textit{Arlington Heights} allow a court to infer purposeful discrimination, so the citations violate equal protection.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Disparate Impact Without Purpose): Pacifica's Uniform Safety Code.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Comparator Proof): A Gambling Prosecution.} A defendant of Chinese descent is prosecuted under a facially neutral ordinance barring gambling rooms. He moves to dismiss on the ground that the ordinance is enforced only against Chinese-owned establishments, but he offers only general assertions and newspaper accounts of anti-Chinese sentiment; he identifies no non-Chinese gambling operators whom the police knew of and declined to charge. \textit{Yick Wo} does not help him. Its inference of hostility rested on an admitted record showing that similarly situated non-Chinese operators were treated differently. Without evidence that comparable violators of another race or nationality went unprosecuted, the claim fails, as it did in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/198/500/}{\emph{Ah Sin v. Wittman}}, 198 U.S. 500 (1905), and under \textit{Armstrong} he would not even obtain discovery.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Standardless Discretion, Moderate Disparity): Food-Truck Permits.} A city's permit board may grant or deny food-truck permits in the public interest, with no written criteria. Over two years it denies 45 percent of applications from noncitizen operators and 20 percent from citizen operators. The board points to some neutral reasons in its files (incomplete applications, parking conflicts), but the reasons are applied inconsistently, and one board member once remarked that the city had too many immigrant vendors. The standardless grant of power echoes the ordinances in \textit{Yick Wo}, and the remark and irregular reasons are \textit{Arlington Heights} evidence. But the disparity is far from \textit{Yick Wo}'s 200-to-0 record, the board has offered partial neutral explanations, and later cases such as \textit{Kotch} largely abandoned \textit{Yick Wo}'s arbitrary-power strand for economic regulation. Whether a court infers purposeful discrimination is genuinely uncertain.
```

```latex
--- Justice Matthews,

--- Justice Matthews,

--- Justice Matthews,
```

## 197_plyler_v_doe

### Memory Jogger

```latex
By a 5--4 vote, the Court held that Texas could not deny a free public education to undocumented children: they are ``persons within its jurisdiction'' under the Equal Protection Clause, and a law imposing a lifetime hardship on innocent children must further some substantial goal of the State (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/457/202/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Fifth Circuit in both consolidated cases by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The cases were argued on December 1, 1981, and decided on June 15, 1982 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/457/202/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/457/202}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Brennan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Marshall, Blackmun, Powell, and Stevens.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Marshall, Justice Blackmun, and Justice Powell each filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Chief Justice Burger filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices White, Rehnquist, and O'Connor.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Marshall

\item \textbf{Residency Requirements:}

\item \textbf{Refusal to Extend Heightened Review:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Unconstitutional): Proof of Lawful Status as a Condition of Enrollment.} A school district adopts a rule that no child may enroll in its elementary or secondary schools without presenting a birth certificate, passport, or federal immigration document showing lawful presence. Undocumented children who cannot produce such papers are turned away. Although the rule is framed as a documentation requirement rather than a ban, its effect is the absolute, status-based deprivation of basic education that \textit{Plyler} condemned. The children are not accountable for their status, the denial imposes a lifetime hardship, and the district cannot show that excluding them furthers a substantial state goal. As in \textit{Plyler}, saving money by excluding a disfavored group is not enough, and the rule violates the Equal Protection Clause.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Unconstitutional): Tuition for Citizen Children of Undocumented Parents.} A State requires families to pay tuition for any child, including a U.S.\ citizen child, whose parents cannot document lawful presence. The State argues that the rule targets only the parents' unlawful conduct. \textit{Plyler}'s reasoning applies with even greater force: the Court held that visiting condemnation on children for their parents' misconduct is contrary to basic principles of individual responsibility, and these children are citizens with every claim to equal treatment. The rule penalizes innocent children for a status they did not choose, imposes the same lifetime hardship of lost education, and serves no substantial interest the State could not pursue by other means. It is invalid under \textit{Plyler}, and likely under any standard of review.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Likely Upheld): Higher-Education Tuition, With a K--12 Twist.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Upheld): A Bona Fide Residency Requirement.} A child whose parents live in another country moves in with an aunt in a Texas school district for the main purpose of attending its public schools, and the district denies him tuition-free admission because he is not a bona fide resident of the district. The family argues that \textit{Plyler} forbids denying a free public education to any child. The rule is valid. \textit{Plyler} invalidated a classification based on immigration status that imposed a total deprivation on innocent children; a neutral residency rule applies to every child alike, whatever his status. In \textit{Martinez v.\ Bynum}, 461 U.S. 321 (1983), the Court upheld exactly such a requirement under a separate portion of \S~21.031. A State may not, however, relabel undocumented children as nonresidents to evade \textit{Plyler}.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unsettled): Collecting Students' Immigration Status.}

\textit{Issue:}

The answer likely turns on proof: \textit{Plyler} rested on a total exclusion, so a court would ask whether the reporting rule in fact deters enrollment or merely collects data, and the Court has never decided whether an indirect burden of this kind triggers \textit{Plyler}'s heightened review.
```

## 198_everson_v_board_of_education

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Incorporating the Establishment Clause against the states and invoking Jefferson's ``wall of separation between church and State,'' the Court nonetheless upheld 5--4 New Jersey's reimbursement of bus fares for children attending parochial schools as a neutral public-welfare benefit, like police and fire protection (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/330/1/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the New Jersey Court of Errors and Appeals by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on November 20, 1946, and decided on February 10, 1947 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/330/1/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/330/1}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Black delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Vinson and Justices Reed, Douglas, and Murphy.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Jackson filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Frankfurter. Justice Rutledge filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Frankfurter, Jackson, and Burton.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Early Applications of the Wall and the Child-Benefit Theory (1948--1971):}

\item \textbf{Neutrality Becomes a Mandate (2002--2022):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Aid Upheld): Free Transit Passes for All Students.} A city gives every enrolled K--12 student a free pass for its public buses and subways, whether the student attends a public, private secular, or religious school. A taxpayer sues, arguing that passes used to reach Catholic and Jewish day schools are tax support for religious institutions. Under \textit{Everson}, the program is a general public-welfare benefit paid to students and their families, not to the schools, and it is indistinguishable from the bus-fare reimbursements the Court upheld. Excluding religious-school students would make the city an adversary of religion rather than a neutral, so the passes survive the Establishment Clause challenge (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/330/1/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Aid Upheld): Loaning Secular Textbooks to All Students.} A state lends approved secular textbooks in mathematics, science, and history to every student in grades 7--12, including students at parochial schools; the books remain state property and the school never receives money. Taxpayers object that the books free up parochial-school budgets for religious instruction. Extending \textit{Everson}'s child-benefit reasoning, \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/392/236/}{\textit{Board of Education v.\ Allen}, 392 U.S.\ 236 (1968)}, upheld exactly this kind of program: the benefit runs to students and parents, the materials are secular, and the State contributes nothing to the schools' religious mission. Rutledge's fungibility objection did not carry the day in \textit{Everson}, and it does not here.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Aid Invalid): Tax Grants to Pay Clergy Salaries.} To help congregations hit hard by an economic downturn, a county appropriates tax revenue for grants to churches, synagogues, and mosques to pay the salaries of their clergy, distributing the money equally among all faiths that apply. Equal treatment of all religions does not save the program. \textit{Everson} declared that government may not pass laws that aid all religions and that no tax in any amount may be levied to support religious activities or institutions, and the grants pay for the core religious function itself rather than a service ``separate'' from it like police or fire protection. Madison's objection to the nondiscriminatory Virginia assessment, which both the majority and Rutledge embraced, applies directly.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Practice Invalid): Religious Classes Inside Public Schools.} A public school district lets clergy of several faiths come into its classrooms for an hour each week during the school day to teach religion to students whose parents consent, while other students move to another room for secular study. The program uses the public school building and the State's compulsory-attendance machinery to help religious groups spread their faith. Applying \textit{Everson}'s incorporated Establishment Clause, \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/333/203/}{\textit{McCollum v.\ Board of Education}, 333 U.S.\ 203 (1948)}, held such a program unconstitutional; unlike \textit{Everson}'s bus fares, the aid here goes to religious instruction itself.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unresolved): Private Aid Versus Charter Status.}
```

## 199_wisconsin_v_yoder

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Applying strict scrutiny under the Free Exercise Clause, the Court held that Wisconsin could not criminally punish Amish parents for refusing to send their children to formal high school after the eighth grade, because the State's interest in one or two more years of schooling did not outweigh the grave threat to the Amish religious way of life (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/406/205/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Wisconsin Supreme Court (reversing respondents' convictions) by a \textbf{6--1} vote, with seven Justices participating. The case was argued on December 8, 1971, and decided on May 15, 1972 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/406/205/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/406/205}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Burger delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Brennan, Stewart, White, Marshall, and Blackmun.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Stewart filed a concurring opinion, joined by Justice Brennan. Justice White filed a concurring opinion, joined by Justices Brennan and Stewart.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Douglas filed an opinion dissenting in part (concurring in the judgment as to Jonas Yoder and dissenting as to Adin Yutzy and Wallace Miller).
    \item \textbf{Not Participating:} Justices Powell and Rehnquist took no part in the consideration or decision of the case.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Early Narrowing:}

\item \textbf{\emph{Smith} and the ``Hybrid Situation'':}

\item \textbf{Revitalization in \emph{Mahmoud v. Taylor}:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Exemption Required): Raising the Attendance Age to 18.} A State raises its compulsory-attendance age from 16 to 18 and prosecutes Old Order Amish parents whose 16- and 17-year-olds are learning farming and carpentry at home under parental supervision. The burden is the one the Court found in \textit{Yoder}: formal high school removes adolescents from the community during the formative years before adult baptism. The State's interests in citizenship and self-sufficiency are already served by eight years of schooling plus the Amish vocational system, and it cannot show with particularity how two more years of classroom attendance for this small group would harm those interests. Under \textit{Yoder}'s ``interests of the highest order'' standard, the prosecutions fail (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/406/205/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Strict Scrutiny Triggered): Normative Classroom Instruction.} Devout parents seek advance notice and the chance to excuse their children from

. The parents are not challenging what the school teaches other children; they seek to keep their own children out of instruction designed to change the religious views the parents are trying to instill. After \textit{Mahmoud v. Taylor} read \textit{Yoder} as a general principle rather than an Amish-specific exception, instruction of this kind is treated as a burden on parents' right to direct their children's religious upbringing that triggers strict scrutiny even though the curriculum applies to everyone. The school district must show a compelling reason for refusing an opt-out.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Exemption): Secular Communitarians.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Exemption): A Faith That Forbids All Schooling.} Members of a small religious community refuse to send their children to any school, or to provide any home instruction, because their faith forbids formal learning of any kind; their children reach adolescence unable to read. The State prosecutes the parents under its compulsory-attendance law. \textit{Yoder} rested heavily on the Amish acceptance of eight years of elementary schooling and on the ``relatively slight'' deviation at issue, and Justice White's concurrence, joined by two members of the majority, stressed that it would be ``a very different case'' if a religion forbade all formal schooling. The State's interest in basic literacy and the child's future is of the highest order and cannot be served by a less restrictive means, so the prosecutions likely succeed.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Vaccine Requirement With Medical Exemptions.} Devout parents seek a religious exemption from

as a condition of school attendance. The State can rely on \textit{Prince v. Massachusetts}, which \textit{Yoder} distinguished as involving a threat to public health and safety, and argue that a communicable-disease rule does not interfere with religious formation the way high school did for the Amish. But the parents can argue that allowing medical exemptions while refusing religious ones means the rule is not generally applicable under \textit{Fulton v. City of Philadelphia}, 593 U.S. 522 (2021), which would trigger strict scrutiny regardless of \textit{Yoder}. Whether the State's interest in preventing outbreaks survives that scrutiny is genuinely contested.
```
