## 200_abrams_v_united_states

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Affirming the Espionage Act convictions of five Russian-born radicals who scattered leaflets urging a munitions strike, the Court held 7--2 that intent to hinder the war could be inferred from the likely effects of their speech; Justice Holmes's dissent, demanding a clear and imminent danger, became a foundation of modern free-speech law (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/250/616/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the convictions by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued on October 21--22, 1919, and decided on November 10, 1919 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/250/616/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/250/616}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Clarke delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice White and Justices McKenna, Day, Van Devanter, Pitney, and McReynolds.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Holmes filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Brandeis.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Holmes Vindicated (\textit{Brandenburg}):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Conviction Upheld): Speech Coordinated With a Foreign Terrorist Organization.} During an overseas conflict, members of a U.S. advocacy group, working at the request of and in coordination with a group the Secretary of State has designated a foreign terrorist organization, produce recruiting videos and train its members to petition international bodies. They are prosecuted under the federal ban on providing material support to such organizations. Like the \textit{Abrams} majority, the Court in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/561/1/}{\textit{Holder v. Humanitarian Law Project}}, 561 U.S. 1 (2010), deferred to the political branches' judgment about speech that could aid an enemy in wartime, upholding the ban as applied to speech coordinated with such groups. Because the speech is directed by the foreign organization rather than independent advocacy, the defendants cannot rely on Holmes's imminence requirement, and the conviction likely stands.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Conviction Upheld Even Under Holmes's Test): A Call to Storm a Recruiting Station.} At an antiwar rally outside a military recruiting office, a speaker points to the building and tells an angry crowd to break in now and burn the draft files before the police arrive; several listeners immediately smash the windows. The speaker is convicted of incitement. Here even Justice Holmes would affirm. Unlike the ``silly leaflet'' in \textit{Abrams}, the speech was intended to produce an immediate evil and created a present danger of it. It also satisfies \textit{Brandenburg}'s test, since it was directed to inciting imminent lawless action and was likely to produce it. The case shows that the dissent's rule protects advocacy, not a speaker who directs an immediate crime.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Speech Protected): Urging a Strike Next Month.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Speech Protected): Calling the President a Coward.} During a war, a pamphleteer distributes leaflets calling the President a coward and the government a fraud, and is prosecuted under a modern statute that, like Count One in \textit{Abrams}, punishes ``disloyal, scurrilous and abusive language'' about the government. The pamphlet urges no action at all. Holmes rejected the claim that the First Amendment left the common law of seditious libel in force, and the Court later adopted that view, explaining in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/376/254/}{\textit{New York Times Co. v. Sullivan}}, 376 U.S. 254 (1964), that the Sedition Act of 1798 had been condemned in the court of history. Criticism of officials, however harsh, is core political speech; because it threatens no imminent lawless action, the conviction cannot stand under \textit{Brandenburg}.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Borderline Imminence): A Viral Call to Shut Down a Plant.}

The case is close because the government must also prove that the anonymous poster intended imminent lawless action, and ``shut down'' could mean a lawful protest or picket; whether the words and the crowd's rapid response establish that intent is a question on which reasonable judges could differ.
```

## 201_new_york_times_co_v_sullivan

### Memory Jogger

```latex
An Alabama jury awarded a Montgomery city commissioner \$500,000 against the \textit{New York Times} over a civil-rights advertisement with minor errors; the Court unanimously reversed, holding that a public official may recover for a defamatory falsehood about his official conduct only by proving ``actual malice''---knowing or reckless falsity (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/376/254/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{reversed and remanded} the judgment of the Supreme Court of Alabama, 9--0. The case was argued on January 6--7, 1964, and decided on March 9, 1964, together with \textit{Abernathy v.\ Sullivan} (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/376/254/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/376/254}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Brennan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Warren and Justices Clark, Harlan, Stewart, and White.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Black filed a concurring opinion, joined by Justice Douglas. Justice Goldberg filed an opinion concurring in the result, joined by Justice Douglas.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Black

Justice Goldberg

\item \textbf{Extensions and Limits of Actual Malice:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Liability): A Careless Op-Ed About a State Attorney General.} A newspaper publishes a guest op-ed accusing the State Attorney General of dropping a corruption investigation to protect a donor. The op-ed misstates the dates and wrongly says the investigation was closed rather than suspended. The editors relied on the author's reputation and did not check the court docket, which would have revealed the errors. The Attorney General sues for libel. Under \textit{Sullivan}, he must prove with convincing clarity that the paper knew the statements were false or recklessly disregarded their falsity. Failing to check available records, like the \textit{Times}'s failure to consult its own news files, is at most negligence, so the claim fails.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Liability): Impersonal Criticism of a Government Office.} A civil-liberties group's website falsely states that guards at the county jail routinely beat inmates who file grievances. It names no individual. The elected sheriff, who oversees the jail, sues, arguing that readers will understand any criticism of the jail as an attack on him. \textit{Sullivan} forecloses that theory: Alabama's inference that criticism of the police necessarily referred to the commissioner who supervised them impermissibly transformed criticism of government into personal libel of an official. Without evidence that the statement was ``of and concerning'' the sheriff personally, and without proof of actual malice, the sheriff cannot recover.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Actual Malice Shown): Purposeful Avoidance of the Truth.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Actual Malice Not Required): A Private-Figure Plaintiff.}

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): A Citizen Who Spoke Once at a Council Meeting.} A local business owner testifies at a single city council hearing against a proposed zoning change, and a short news clip of her testimony circulates online. A blogger then falsely reports that she bribed a council member to block the change; the blogger was careless but did not know the claim was false. Whether she must prove actual malice depends on whether she is a limited-purpose public figure under \textit{Gertz}---someone who voluntarily thrust herself into a particular public controversy to influence its outcome. One appearance at a public hearing points toward public-figure status on the zoning dispute, but her minimal role, lack of media access, and the blogger's own creation of the controversy point the other way, so the result is uncertain.
```

Kept: Curtis/Gertz/Hustler/Bose/Harte-Hanks sentence.

## 202_brandenburg_v_ohio

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A Ku Klux Klan leader convicted under Ohio's criminal syndicalism law for a filmed rally speech hinting at ``revengeance'' won reversal: a unanimous per curiam Court held that the State may punish advocacy of force or law violation only when it is directed to inciting imminent lawless action and is likely to produce it, and overruled \textit{Whitney v.\ California} (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/395/444/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{reversed} the Supreme Court of Ohio, \textbf{8--0}, in a \emph{per curiam} opinion. The case was argued on February 27, 1969, and decided on June 9, 1969 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/395/444/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.oyez.org/cases/1968/492}{Oyez}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Per Curiam Opinion:} Joined by Chief Justice Warren and Justices Black, Douglas, Harlan, Brennan, Stewart, White, and Marshall (one seat vacant following Justice Fortas's resignation).
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Black filed a concurring opinion. Justice Douglas filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{\textit{Hess v.\ Indiana} (1973):}

\item \textbf{\textit{NAACP v.\ Claiborne Hardware Co.} (1982):}

\item \textbf{\textit{Counterman v.\ Colorado} (2023):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Protected Advocacy): A Pamphlet Calling Revolution Morally Necessary.} A small political group distributes a pamphlet arguing that the federal government is illegitimate and that armed revolution will someday be a moral necessity. The pamphlet names no target or date and urges readers to study and organize. The State prosecutes the authors under a statute punishing anyone who advocates the propriety of violence as a means of political reform. Under \textit{Brandenburg}, this is the ``mere abstract teaching'' of the moral necessity of force that the Constitution protects: the speech is not directed to producing imminent lawless action and is not likely to do so. A statute that punishes such advocacy without an imminence requirement is invalid on its face, as Ohio's was (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/395/444/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Civil Liability): A Boycott Leader's Heated Warning.} At a rally supporting a consumer boycott of local stores, an organizer warns that anyone caught shopping at the boycotted stores will be disciplined by the community and will regret it. Weeks later, unidentified persons vandalize the car of a shopper. The store owners sue the organizer for their lost profits and the damage. Because the speech was not directed to, and did not produce, imminent lawless action, and the organizer did not authorize or ratify the later vandalism, \textit{Brandenburg} bars liability. \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/458/886/}{\emph{NAACP v.\ Claiborne Hardware Co.}, 458 U.S.\ 886 (1982)}, applied exactly this reasoning to bar civil damages for emotionally charged boycott speeches.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Unprotected Incitement): A Bullhorn Command to an Armed Crowd.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Outside \textit{Brandenburg}'s Protection): A Private Solicitation to Commit Arson.} In a direct message to one acquaintance, a defendant offers \$5,000 if the acquaintance will set fire to a rival's warehouse that night, supplying the address and the location of an unlocked side door. The defendant is charged with criminal solicitation and argues that, under \textit{Brandenburg}, he engaged only in advocacy. \textit{Brandenburg} protects advocacy of ideas, not speech that is itself the means of committing a crime: a targeted request to a particular person to commit a specific offense is solicitation, a category the First Amendment has never protected, as the Court recognized in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/553/285/}{\emph{United States v.\ Williams}, 553 U.S.\ 285 (2008)}. Even under \textit{Brandenburg} itself, the request is directed to imminent lawless action.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Imminence Unclear): The Scheduled Livestream Raid.}
```

## 203_cohen_v_california

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A 19-year-old wore a jacket reading ``Fuck the Draft'' in a Los Angeles courthouse corridor; the Court held 5--4 that a State may not criminalize the public display of a single expletive conveying a political message, because it was not obscenity or fighting words and unwilling viewers could avert their eyes (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/403/15/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the California Court of Appeal by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on February 22, 1971, and decided on June 7, 1971 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/403/15/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/403/15}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Harlan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Douglas, Brennan, Stewart, and Marshall.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Blackmun filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justice Black, and joined as to its second paragraph by Justice White.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Speech Protected): A Profane Protest Sign Outside City Hall.} During a rally on the plaza outside city hall, a resident holds a sign using the same four-letter word to condemn the mayor's housing policy. Families walking to a nearby park see it, and police arrest the resident under an ordinance banning offensive language in public places. As in \textit{Cohen}, the sign targets a policy and a public official rather than any individual present, so it is not fighting words, it is not erotic and so not obscene, and passersby in a public square can look away. The State may not punish the word merely because it offends, and the arrest violates the First Amendment.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Speech Protected): A Ban on Profane Bumper Stickers.} A State makes it a misdemeanor to display on any vehicle a sticker containing profanity, and police cite a driver whose sticker uses an expletive to protest a war. Other motorists and pedestrians who see the sticker are outside the home and can avert their eyes, so they are not a captive audience. The law reaches emotive political expression that \textit{Cohen} protects, gives officials the boundless power over vocabulary Harlan rejected because one man's vulgarity is another's lyric, and is not tied to any setting that would justify special decorum rules. The citation cannot stand.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Speech Punishable): A Face-to-Face Epithet Inviting a Fight.} During a heated argument on a sidewalk, a man steps within inches of a rival, uses the same expletive together with personal slurs aimed at him, and dares him to throw a punch. He is convicted under a statute narrowly construed to reach only words likely to provoke the average listener to immediate violence. Unlike Cohen's jacket, this speech is a personally abusive epithet directed to the person of the hearer, the core of \textit{Chaplinsky}'s fighting-words category that \textit{Cohen} preserved. The conviction is consistent with the First Amendment.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Restriction Upheld): Profanity Broadcast Into the Home.} A radio station airs, in the early afternoon, a comedy routine that repeats several vulgar words, and a parent driving with a young child complains. The federal regulator sanctions the station under its indecency rules. Unlike the corridor in \textit{Cohen}, broadcast speech reaches listeners in the privacy of the home and is uniquely accessible to children, so a warning or averting one's eyes cannot fully protect them. In \textit{FCC v.\ Pacifica Foundation}, 438 U.S.\ 726 (1978), the Court upheld a sanction on just these facts, showing that \textit{Cohen}'s rule depends on the medium and setting.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): A Courtroom Ban on Profane Apparel.}

The case is close because \textit{Cohen} expressly left open location-specific decorum rules but condemned giving officials power to police offensive words.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Source comment lines and Case Metadata: Chapter/Topic line and Cornell/Justia list (links kept in Judicial Votes).
- Legacy section: introductory paragraph and 'Contemporary Digital and Public-Forum Disputes (Through 2026)' item (general commentary, no decision). Kept: Iancu v. Brunetti and Mahanoy items.
- Exam Hypothetical 'Opposite Side Contrast' (Pacifica) - an exam tip contrasting a real case; Pacifica now appears as new Example 4.

In the Pentagon Papers Case, the Nixon Administration sought to stop the \textit{New York Times} and \textit{Washington Post} from publishing a classified Vietnam War study; a 6--3 \textit{per curiam} held that the Government had not carried the ``heavy burden'' required to justify a prior restraint, with nine Justices writing separately (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/403/713/}{Justia}).

The cases were argued June 26, 1971, and decided June 30, 1971 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/403/713/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Black (joined by Justice Douglas); Justice Douglas (joined by Justice Black); Justice Brennan; Justice Stewart (joined by Justice White); Justice White (joined by Justice Stewart); and Justice Marshall.

### Holding

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Gag Orders on Trial Coverage:} The Court applied the same high barrier when

\item \textbf{Fiduciary Prepublication Review:}

\item \textbf{Protective Orders on Discovery:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Injunction Barred): Leaked Memo Exposing Official Deception.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Injunction Barred): A State's Leaked Emergency Plan.} A newspaper obtains a leaked copy of a State's confidential hurricane-evacuation plan, which shows that officials knew the plan's shelter capacity was badly inadequate. The Governor asks a federal court to enjoin publication, arguing that disclosure could cause public panic and help criminals predict where police will be deployed during a storm. Those predicted harms are speculative and depend on the reactions of third parties. Under the \textit{per curiam}, the restraint comes to court with a ``heavy presumption against its constitutional validity,'' and the Governor's ``surmise or conjecture'' (in Justice Brennan's phrase) about possible harm falls far short of Justice Stewart's showing of direct, immediate, and irreparable damage. The injunction must be denied (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/403/713/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Narrow Injunction Likely Upheld): Live Convoy Coordinates and Cryptographic Keys.} Same facts, as to Document 2.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Restraint Permitted): A Former Analyst's Memoir.} A former intelligence analyst signed an employment agreement promising to submit any writing about her work for prepublication review. She completes a memoir describing classified operations and announces that she will publish it without review. The Government sues to enjoin publication of the unreviewed manuscript and to impose a constructive trust on any profits. \textit{New York Times} does not control, because the analyst is not an outside newspaper publishing what it received; she voluntarily assumed a duty of confidentiality as a condition of access to secrets. Under \textit{Snepp v.\ United States}, 444 U.S. 507 (1980), the agreement is enforceable, and the Government may require review before publication and recover her profits if she breaches it.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Statutory Authorization): A Narrow Congressional Injunction Statute.} Congress enacts a statute authorizing federal courts, after an \textit{in camera} hearing, to enjoin publication of the names of covert intelligence officers currently serving abroad when publication would create a substantial risk of physical harm to them. A news site announces that it will publish a list of such names it has obtained. The statute answers the objection pressed by Justices White and Marshall, who stressed the absence of congressional authorization, and naming a deployed officer may resemble Brennan's troop-ship example. But Justices Black and Douglas would bar any prior restraint, and Stewart's standard demands that harm be direct, immediate, and irreparable, not merely a risk. Because \textit{New York Times} produced no majority rationale, whether such a statute could be applied to a publisher is unresolved.
```

## 205_virginia_state_board_of_pharmacy_v_vccc

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Striking down Virginia's ban on advertising prescription-drug prices, the Court held 7--1 that purely commercial speech is protected by the First Amendment and that consumers have a reciprocal right to receive truthful information about lawful products (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/425/748/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the District Court by a \textbf{7--1} vote; Justice Stevens took no part in the consideration or decision of the case. The case was argued on November 11, 1975, and decided on May 24, 1976 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/425/748/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Blackmun delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justices Brennan, Stewart, White, Marshall, and Powell.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Chief Justice Burger and Justice Stewart each filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Rehnquist filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Chief Justice Burger

Justice Stewart

Justice Rehnquist
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Ban Invalid): Optometrists Barred From Advertising Eyeglass Prices.} A State's optometry board disciplines any licensed optometrist who advertises the price of eye examinations or eyeglasses, explaining that price competition will lead consumers to choose cheap, careless providers. A consumer group and an optometrist who wants to advertise sue. Under \textit{Virginia Pharmacy}, the consumers have standing to assert their reciprocal right to receive the information, and the advertisements are truthful statements about a lawful transaction. The State's rationale is the same paternalistic one the Court rejected: it seeks to protect professional standards by keeping the public in ignorance rather than by regulating the quality of care directly. The ban is unconstitutional, even though \textit{Williamson v.\ Lee Optical Co.} once upheld a similar rule as economic regulation.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Ban Invalid): Lawyers Barred From Advertising Fees for Routine Services.} A state bar rule forbids attorneys from advertising in newspapers that they will handle uncontested divorces, simple wills, or name changes for stated flat fees. Two lawyers who run a low-cost legal clinic publish such an advertisement and are disciplined. Footnote~25 of \textit{Virginia Pharmacy} reserved the question of lawyer advertising, but the Court answered it a year later in \textit{Bates v.\ State Bar of Arizona}, 433 U.S.\ 350 (1977), applying \textit{Virginia Pharmacy}'s reasoning. Truthful price information about routine services helps consumers find affordable counsel, and the bar's fears that advertising will undermine professionalism are the kind of paternalism the First Amendment forbids. The State may still prohibit false or misleading claims.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Regulation Valid): In-Person Solicitation of Accident Victims.} A lawyer visits a hospitalized accident victim and her family, urging them to sign a contingent-fee agreement on the spot, and is disciplined under a rule banning in-person solicitation for profit. \textit{Virginia Pharmacy} protected impersonal, truthful price information that consumers can evaluate at their own pace. Face-to-face solicitation is different: it pressures a vulnerable listener for an immediate decision, invites overreaching, and occurs outside public view, where false or misleading statements are hard to detect. Under \textit{Ohralik v.\ Ohio State Bar Ass'n}, 436 U.S.\ 447 (1978), the State may ban it as a prophylactic measure, consistent with \textit{Virginia Pharmacy}'s own statement that commercial speech remains subject to regulation.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Regulation Valid): Advertising Prescription Drugs for Sale Without a Prescription.} An online seller advertises that it will ship prescription-only medications to Virginia customers without requiring a prescription, listing prices for each drug. The State fines the seller under a law prohibiting such advertisements. The seller invokes \textit{Virginia Pharmacy}, which protected price advertising for prescription drugs. The claim fails. \textit{Virginia Pharmacy} protected truthful information about entirely lawful commercial activity, and the Court expressly preserved the State's power to bar advertisements proposing unlawful transactions. Because selling prescription drugs without a prescription is itself illegal, the advertisement proposes an unlawful transaction and receives no First Amendment protection, so the State may prohibit it outright.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Restricting Sportsbook Promotions.}

\textit{For the challengers:}

\textit{For the State:}

The case is close because the law partly suppresses truthful information to reduce demand, which \textit{Virginia Pharmacy} and \textit{44 Liquormart} condemn, yet leaves the information available to account holders and targets inducements to a harmful activity.
```

## 206_central_hudson_v_public_service_commission

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Striking down New York's total ban on promotional advertising by electric utilities 8--1, the Court announced the four-part intermediate-scrutiny test for restrictions on truthful commercial speech about lawful activity: a substantial interest, direct advancement, and a restriction no more extensive than necessary (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/447/557/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the New York Court of Appeals by an \textbf{8--1} vote. The case was argued on March 17, 1980, and decided on June 20, 1980 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/447/557/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/447/557}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Powell delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justices Stewart, White, and Marshall.
    \item \textbf{Concurring in the Judgment:} Justice Brennan filed an opinion concurring in the judgment (447 U.S. at 572). Justice Blackmun filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justice Brennan (447 U.S. at 573). Justice Stevens filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justice Brennan (447 U.S. at 579).
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Rehnquist filed a dissenting opinion (447 U.S. at 583).
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Blackmun

Justice Stevens

Justice Brennan

Justice Rehnquist
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Restriction Invalid): Banning Truthful E-Cigarette Ads.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Restriction Invalid): A Ban on Advertising Liquor Prices.} To promote temperance, a state forbids liquor retailers from advertising their prices anywhere except inside the store. A retailer that wants to advertise discounts sues. The ads are truthful and concern a lawful product, and reducing alcohol consumption is a substantial interest. But the State offers little evidence that hiding prices meaningfully reduces drinking, and it could pursue its goal directly through higher taxes, minimum prices, or education campaigns. As in \textit{Central Hudson}, the State seeks to influence conduct by keeping consumers uninformed, and the ban is more extensive than necessary; \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/484/}{\textit{44 Liquormart, Inc.\ v.\ Rhode Island}, 517 U.S.\ 484 (1996)}, struck down a similar ban.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Restriction Valid): Advertising an Unlawful Transaction.} A state in which online sports betting remains a crime fines a website operator for running ads urging state residents to place bets on its offshore platform. The operator claims the ads are truthful commercial speech. \textit{Central Hudson}'s first prong ends the inquiry: commercial speech is protected only if it concerns lawful activity and is not misleading. An advertisement proposing a transaction that is illegal where the audience would complete it receives no First Amendment protection, so the State need not show a substantial interest, direct advancement, or tailoring (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/447/557/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Restriction Valid): A Short Waiting Period for Lawyer Solicitation Letters.} A state bar forbids personal-injury lawyers from sending targeted direct-mail solicitations to accident victims or their families within 30 days of the accident. The rule restricts truthful speech about lawful services, but it serves substantial interests in protecting victims' privacy and the reputation of the legal profession, the bar supports it with survey evidence showing that such letters are widely seen as intrusive, and it is limited in time and leaves other forms of advertising open. Applying \textit{Central Hudson} as refined by \textit{Fox}, \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/618/}{\textit{Florida Bar v.\ Went For It, Inc.}, 515 U.S.\ 618 (1995)}, upheld such a rule as a reasonable fit.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): Billboard Ban for State-Legal Marijuana.} A state that has legalized recreational marijuana bans billboard and television advertising by licensed dispensaries in order to reduce youth use. A dispensary challenges the ban. The first prong is the problem: the product is lawful under state law but remains illegal under federal law, and \textit{Central Hudson} protects only speech concerning lawful activity. If a court treats the activity as lawful because the State permits it, the ban faces the same direct-advancement and fit problems as the e-cigarette ban in Example 1. If it treats federal illegality as controlling, the ads may receive no protection at all. The Supreme Court has not resolved which sovereign's law defines lawful activity in this setting.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Case Metadata: docket number, lower-court citation, Chapter/Topic line (Justia and Cornell links kept in Judicial Votes).
- Legacy section: introductory sentence and Cornell LII citation; 'Compelled Disclosures (Zauderer) vs. Restrictions (Central Hudson) Through 2026' item (Zauderer/NIFLA disclosure doctrine, DoorDash v. City of New York and U.S. Chamber of Commerce v. Sanchez lower-court cases) - concerns compelled disclosure and unverifiable 2025--2026 lower-court rulings rather than Central Hudson's holding. Kept: Fox/44 Liquormart and Sorrell items.
- Exam Hypothetical: 'Opposite Side' Contrast (Central Hudson vs. Zauderer and Consolidated Edison) - an exam tip, not a fact pattern.

## 207_ward_v_rock_against_racism

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Upholding New York City's rule that Central Park bandshell performers use city sound equipment and a city technician, the Court held that a content-neutral time, place, and manner regulation is narrowly tailored if it serves a substantial interest that would be achieved less effectively without it; it need not be the least restrictive means (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/491/781/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the Second Circuit by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued on February 27, 1989, and decided on June 22, 1989 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/491/781/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Kennedy delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices White, O'Connor, and Scalia.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Blackmun concurred in the result, without opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Marshall filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Brennan and Stevens.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Blackmun

Justice Marshall
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Regulation Valid): Volume Control at an Amphitheater Next to a Hospital.} A city requires every event at a public waterfront amphitheater beside a hospital to run its amplification through a city-owned sound-limiting system that keeps volume below a set level at the hospital's property line. Performers and speakers choose their own content and sound mix. The rule targets noise, not message, so it is content-neutral under \textit{Ward}'s purpose inquiry. It directly advances the city's substantial interest in protecting patients from unwelcome noise, that interest would be served less effectively without it, and speakers remain free to say anything at the amphitheater at a moderated volume, leaving ample alternative channels (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/491/781/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Regulation Valid): A Less Restrictive Alternative Does Not Defeat the Rule.} To prevent disruption of trials, a county bars amplified sound on the plaza in front of its courthouse during court hours on weekdays, while allowing unamplified demonstrations at any time and amplified rallies on evenings and weekends. Protesters show that a decibel limit enforced by sheriff's deputies would protect the courtrooms nearly as well. Under \textit{Ward}, that showing is not enough: a time, place, and manner rule ``need not be the least restrictive or least intrusive means'' of serving the government's interest. Because the ban is confined to the hours and place where the problem arises, it does not burden substantially more speech than necessary, and it is valid.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Regulation Likely Invalid): Standardless Power to Mute Political Speech.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Strict Scrutiny Applies): A Volume Rule Keyed to Content.} A city's park guideline lets the city's sound technician lower the volume of any performance that contains ``offensive or hateful lyrics,'' while leaving other performances at full volume. The rule fails at \textit{Ward}'s first step. \textit{Ward} upheld New York's guideline because it was adopted to control noise and not ``because of disagreement with the message'' conveyed, and because the city's technician deferred to sponsors on everything but volume. A rule that turns the knob based on what the lyrics say is content-based, so it falls outside \textit{Ward}'s intermediate scrutiny and must survive strict scrutiny, which it almost certainly cannot.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Total Ban on Amplification on a Park's Main Lawn.} Citing noise complaints from new apartment towers, a city bans all amplified sound on the main lawn of its largest park, the traditional site of mass rallies, and permits amplification only at a small stage in a remote corner. The rule is content-neutral, and under \textit{Ward} the city need not prove that a decibel limit would fail. But \textit{Ward} upheld a rule that merely moderated volume and banned no type of expression; here, a rally of thousands on the lawn cannot be heard without amplification, so the alternative channels may be inadequate, and \textit{McCullen} suggests the city must show it seriously tried less intrusive tools. Whether the ban burdens substantially more speech than necessary is a close question.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Case Metadata: full case name, docket number, Chapter/Topic line, duplicate source link (Justia link kept in Judicial Votes).
- Legacy section: introductory paragraph (Turner Broadcasting framing); '2025--2026 Applications' item (TikTok v. Garland citation and West Virginia v. B.P.J. (June 30, 2026) - passing citations, and the latter cannot be verified here). Kept: McCullen and Reed / City of Austin items.
- Exam Hypothetical: the 'City's Defense' bullet (restates Ward's test) and 'Opposite Side Contrast' label; the fact pattern and challenger analysis are reused in Example 3.

## 208_rav_v_city_of_st_paul

### Memory Jogger

```latex
After a teenager burned a cross in a Black family's yard, the Court unanimously struck down St.~Paul's bias-motivated crime ordinance; Justice Scalia's five-Justice majority held that even within an unprotected category like fighting words, government may not single out disfavored topics or viewpoints for punishment (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/505/377/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} the Minnesota Supreme Court's judgment. The judgment was unanimous (9--0), but the Justices divided 5--4 on the rationale. The case was argued on December~4, 1991, and decided on June~22, 1992 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/505/377/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/90-7675}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Scalia delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice White concurred in the judgment, joined by Justices Blackmun and O'Connor, and by Justice Stevens except as to Part~I-A. Justice Blackmun concurred in the judgment. Justice Stevens concurred in the judgment, joined by Justices White and Blackmun as to Part~I.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice White

Justice Blackmun

Justice Stevens
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Law Invalid): Fighting Words That Disparage the Police.} After tense street protests, a city makes it a crime to direct at a police officer any fighting words that disparage law enforcement, while leaving fighting words praising the police, or insulting the officer on other grounds, unregulated. A protester convicted for a face-to-face epithet challenges the ordinance. Under \textit{R.A.V.}, the city may ban fighting words because of their abusive mode, but it may not pick out only those that carry a disfavored message. This ordinance is worse than St.~Paul's: it discriminates by viewpoint on its face, licensing one side of the debate over policing to fight freestyle. The city's interest in protecting officers could be served by a neutral ban on all fighting words directed at anyone, so the law fails strict scrutiny.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Law Invalid): Obscenity Banned Only When It Mocks Officials.} A state already bars the sale of obscene material under the governing test for obscenity, then adds a felony enhancement that applies only to obscene videos that ridicule elected officials. A distributor of such a video challenges the enhancement. Obscenity is a proscribable category, but \textit{R.A.V.} holds that categories of unprotected speech are not ``entirely invisible to the Constitution.'' The enhancement does not target what makes obscenity unprotected, such as especially graphic prurient content; it targets political criticism, a message the State could never suppress directly. Like Justice Scalia's example of banning only libel critical of the government, the distinction suggests that official suppression of ideas is afoot, and none of the three exceptions saves it.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Law Likely Valid): Threats Against Election Workers.} A state law imposes heavier penalties for true threats of violence made against election officials and poll workers than for other true threats. A defendant convicted of threatening a county clerk argues that, under \textit{R.A.V.}, the law singles out threats on a particular subject. The State answers with \textit{R.A.V.}'s first exception. Justice Scalia explained that Congress may criminalize only threats against the President because the reasons threats are proscribable---fear, disruption, and the risk of violence---have special force there. The same reasoning applies to the officials who run elections: the law selects the subset of threats in which the very harms that make threats unprotected are most acute, and it does not favor any viewpoint on the election itself. The law is likely valid.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Prosecution Valid): Bias Enhancement for Conduct.} Suppose St.~Paul had charged R.A.V. under Minnesota's content-neutral laws against terroristic threats and criminal damage to property, and the State then sought a longer sentence under a statute that enhances penalties when the defendant intentionally selected the victim because of race. That prosecution would raise no \textit{R.A.V.} problem. The underlying crimes punish conduct without regard to any message, and the enhancement punishes the discriminatory selection of a victim, not the expression of an idea. As the Court later held in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/508/476/}{\textit{Wisconsin v. Mitchell}, 508~U.S.~476 (1993)}, bias-motivated conduct may be punished more severely, which is why Justice Scalia observed that St.~Paul had sufficient means to punish cross burning without the ordinance.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Contested): A Campus Harassment Act.}

\textbf{Challenger:}

\textbf{State:}

The outcome turns on whether a court sees a demeaning-by-topic rule as a virulent subset of fighting words or as the same topic-based selection \textit{R.A.V.} condemned; the word ``demean'' itself may lead a court to find viewpoint discrimination.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Case Metadata: docket number, Chapter/Topic line, comment lines (Justia and Cornell links kept in Judicial Votes).
- Legacy section: introductory paragraph (characterization of R.A.V.'s significance); Matal v. Tam item (viewpoint discrimination generally); 'Recent Roberts Court Refinement' item (Counterman, Vidal v. Elster, Chiles v. Salazar) - tangential. Kept: Wisconsin v. Mitchell and Virginia v. Black items.
- Exam Hypothetical: final sentence of the State's 'Opposite Side Contrast' (Mitchell-style alternative), now developed in Example 4; 'Challenger's Argument' and 'Opposite Side Contrast' labels replaced by short labels in the Fence-Sitter example.

## 209_virginia_v_black

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Reviewing Virginia's cross-burning statute, the Court held that a State may ban cross burning carried out with intent to intimidate, a form of true threat, without violating \textit{R.A.V.}; but a plurality struck down the provision treating any cross burning as prima facie evidence of that intent (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/538/343/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded}. The case was argued on December 11, 2002, and decided on April 7, 2003 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/538/343/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/538/343}{Cornell LII}). Disposition:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion (Parts I--III):} Justice O'Connor delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices Stevens, Scalia, and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Plurality Opinion (Parts IV--V):} Justice O'Connor, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices Stevens and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Stevens filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Opinions Concurring in Part and Dissenting in Part:} Justice Scalia filed an opinion concurring in part, concurring in the judgment in part, and dissenting in part, joined by Justice Thomas as to Parts I and II. Justice Souter filed an opinion concurring in the judgment in part and dissenting in part, joined by Justices Kennedy and Ginsburg.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Thomas filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Stevens

Justice Scalia

Justice Souter

Justice Thomas

\item \textbf{Subjective Mens Rea Required (\textit{Counterman v.\ Colorado}):}

(\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/600/22-138/}{\textit{Counterman v.\ Colorado}, 600 U.S.\ 66 (2023)}.)
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Ban Valid): A Noose on an Election Clerk's Porch.}

Defendant A hangs a noose on a Black election clerk's porch at night.

If the jury finds intent from the context---a nighttime display at a targeted official's home---rather than from the prima facie clause, Defendant A's conviction stands.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Ban Valid): An Effigy Burned in a Neighbor's Driveway.} A State makes it a crime to burn any object on another person's property with intent to place that person in fear of bodily harm. After a dispute, a man burns an effigy of his neighbor in the neighbor's driveway while shouting that the neighbor will be next. The statute does not single out any message or symbol; it targets intimidation, which \textit{Black} identifies as a type of true threat directed at a person with intent to place the victim in fear of bodily harm or death. Because the State must prove that intent from the facts, rather than presuming it from the act, the prosecution fits within the unprotected category and raises none of the \textit{R.A.V.} concerns that a content-selective ban would.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Prima Facie Clause Invalid): A Noose in a Protest Sculpture.} Under the same noose statute, Defendant B, a political artist, hangs a noose on a public plaza sculpture to protest capital punishment, and the prosecution relies on the prima facie clause rather than proof of intent.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Statute or Prosecution Invalid): A Viewpoint-Selective Ban and an Objective Standard.}

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): A Burning-Cross Image Posted Online.} During a local election, a man posts an image of a burning cross on social media and tags the account of a Black candidate, adding no words. He is prosecuted under a statute banning communications made with intent to intimidate. He says the post expressed his political views to his followers; the candidate says she understood it as a threat against her. Under \textit{Black}, a burning cross directed at a person can be a true threat, but the same symbol can also be political expression, and context decides which. Tagging the candidate points toward targeting her; the absence of any physical presence or explicit statement points the other way. Whether the State can prove intent to intimidate is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 210_snyder_v_phelps

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Westboro Baptist Church picketed, on public land 1,000 feet away, the funeral of a Marine killed in Iraq; an 8--1 Court held that the First Amendment bars tort liability, including for intentional infliction of emotional distress, for peaceful speech on matters of public concern in a public forum, however hurtful (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/562/443/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Fourth Circuit by an \textbf{8--1} vote. The case was argued on October 6, 2010, and decided on March 2, 2011 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/562/443/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/09-751.ZO.html}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Roberts delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer, Sotomayor, and Kagan.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Breyer filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Alito filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Speech Protected): Protest Near a Police Officer's Funeral.} Activists protesting police violence stand on a public sidewalk several hundred feet from a church where a police officer killed on duty is being mourned. Following police instructions, they hold signs condemning the department's use-of-force record and declaring that police deaths are the price of brutal tactics. The officer's widow, who glimpses the signs from the procession, sues for intentional infliction of emotional distress. As in \textit{Snyder}, the signs' content addresses matters of public concern, the protesters chose a traditional public forum, they stayed at a distance and did not disrupt the service, and they had no prior private dispute with the family. A jury's finding that the protest was outrageous cannot overcome the First Amendment's protection (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/562/443/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Speech Protected): A Columnist's Vicious Attack on a Private Landlord.} During a city scandal over unsafe rental housing, a newspaper columnist writes a savage column calling a private landlord, who owns several of the cited buildings, a heartless slumlord who should be ashamed to show his face. The landlord, a private figure, sues for intentional infliction of emotional distress. \textit{Snyder} extended \textit{Hustler}'s protection to private-figure plaintiffs whenever the speech, judged by its content, form, and context, addresses a matter of public concern. Housing safety is a public issue, and the column was published to a broad audience as part of an ongoing public debate, so the landlord cannot recover for emotional distress on the ground that the commentary was outrageous.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Liability Permitted): A Private Feud Dressed Up With Slogans.} After a dispute over a property line, a man repeatedly posts signs in his front yard, facing his neighbors' home, mocking their daughter's recent miscarriage, and adds one generic sign criticizing local taxes. The neighbors sue for intentional infliction of emotional distress. \textit{Snyder}'s protection depends on speech addressing a matter of public concern; the Court distinguished speech of purely private significance, which receives less protection under \textit{Dun \& Bradstreet}, and warned that a speaker cannot immunize a personal attack arising from a preexisting private relationship by sprinkling in public themes. Here the content, form, and context all reveal a private vendetta, so the tort claim may proceed.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Regulation Likely Valid): A Content-Neutral Funeral Buffer Law.} A State makes it a misdemeanor to picket within 300 feet of a funeral from one hour before until one hour after the service, regardless of the message. A church group that pickets funerals is cited and challenges the law. \textit{Snyder} expressly distinguished such content-neutral time, place, and manner rules from tort liability that turns on the content of the speaker's message, noting that Maryland and many other States had adopted them. Because the law applies to all picketing regardless of viewpoint, it would be judged by the standard for time, place, and manner restrictions; a modest, time-limited buffer that leaves ample alternative channels is likely to survive, though \textit{McCullen v.\ Coakley} shows the tailoring must be real.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Residential Picketing and Doxxing by Anti-Drunk-Driving Activists.}

Because \textit{Snyder} left both residential targeting and online campaigns unresolved, either side could prevail.
```

### Key Quotations

---

---

---
```

## 211_reed_v_town_of_gilbert

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A town sign code gave a church's temporary directional signs harsher size and time limits than ideological or political signs; the Court held that a law distinguishing speech on its face by subject matter, function, or purpose is content based and triggers strict scrutiny regardless of benign motive, and struck the code (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/576/155/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{reversed and remanded} the Ninth Circuit, \textbf{9--0} in the judgment, with a \textbf{6--3} majority opinion. The case was argued on January 12, 2015, and decided on June 18, 2015 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/576/155/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Thomas delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Scalia, Kennedy, Alito, and Sotomayor.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Alito filed a concurring opinion, joined by Justices Kennedy and Sotomayor. Justice Breyer filed an opinion concurring in the judgment. Justice Kagan filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justices Ginsburg and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Alito

Justice Breyer

Justice Kagan
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Content Based; Fails Strict Scrutiny): Oakridge's Topic-Based Lawn-Sign Exemptions.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Content Based; Fails Strict Scrutiny): A School-Zone Picketing Ban Exempting Labor Disputes.} A city bans picketing on public sidewalks within 150 feet of a school during school hours, but exempts peaceful picketing of a school involved in a labor dispute. A student protesting the school's discipline policy is arrested. Whether picketing is allowed turns entirely on its subject matter, so the ordinance is content based on its face even though it favors no viewpoint and the city's motive is to prevent disruption. Labor picketing is no less disruptive than other picketing, so the exemption is fatally underinclusive. The Court struck down an essentially identical ordinance in \textit{Police Department of Chicago v.\ Mosley}, 408 U.S.\ 92 (1972), and \textit{Reed} confirms that result.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Content Neutral; Survives): Oakridge's Revised Ordinance.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Outside \textit{Reed}): The Town's Own Holiday Banners.} A town hangs its own banners on downtown light poles promoting a town-sponsored holiday festival and civic slogans, and has never opened the poles to private messages. A resident whose request to hang a banner criticizing the town council was refused argues that the town is favoring its own subject matter. \textit{Reed}'s strict-scrutiny rule governs regulations of private speech; the Free Speech Clause does not restrict the government's own expression, a point Justice Alito's concurrence expressly preserved. Because the banners are the town's speech on property it controls, choosing their content is not content discrimination against private speakers, and the refusal stands unless the town has opened the poles to private messages.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): A Uniform Time Limit on Event Signs.} Responding to \textit{Reed}, a city allows temporary signs advertising any one-time event, whatever its subject, to be posted no more than seven days before the event and removed within a day after it. A church argues that officials must still read a sign to know whether it advertises an event, the very feature for which Gilbert's rule was struck. The city answers that Justice Alito's concurrence, joined by two members of the six-Justice majority, listed time limits on signs advertising a one-time event as permissible, and \textit{City of Austin} rejected the view that a need to read a sign makes a rule content based. Because Justice Kagan identified this tension and the Court has not resolved it, the outcome is uncertain.
```

## 212_counterman_v_colorado

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A man convicted of stalking a singer through hundreds of Facebook messages won a new trial: the Court held 7--2 that a true-threats prosecution requires proof that the speaker was at least reckless---that he consciously disregarded a substantial risk his words would be viewed as threatening violence---not merely that a reasonable person would see a threat (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/600/22-138/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{vacated and remanded} by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued April 19, 2023, and decided June 27, 2023 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/600/22-138/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Kagan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Alito, Kavanaugh, and Jackson.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Sotomayor filed an opinion concurring in part and concurring in the judgment, joined by Justice Gorsuch as to Parts I, II, III-A, and III-B.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Thomas filed a dissenting opinion. Justice Barrett filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Thomas.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Why a Subjective Mental State Is Required: ``Strategic Protection'' Against Chill}

\subsubsection{Why Recklessness---Not Purpose or Knowledge---Strikes the Right Balance}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Objective Instruction Invalid): A Heated Zoning Comment.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Subjective Recklessness): A Delusional Reader's Single Email.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Recklessness Provable): Persistent Messages After Explicit Notice.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Higher Mental State Satisfies the Floor): A Threat Statute Requiring Intent.} After a dispute with his former business partner, a man texts her a photo of her house with the message that she will not live to see the lawsuit through. He is prosecuted under a statute requiring proof that he sent the message with the purpose of placing the recipient in fear of death, and the jury, so instructed, convicts. He argues on appeal that the message was a joke. \textit{Counterman} does not help him. It sets recklessness as the constitutional \emph{minimum}; a State may require more, and a jury finding of purpose necessarily includes conscious disregard of the risk that the message would be seen as threatening. Whether the message was a jest or a true threat was a question for the jury, decided in context under \textit{Watts} and \textit{Virginia v. Black}, and the conviction stands.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Civil Protective Orders): An Objective Standard Outside Criminal Law.} A woman seeks a civil protective order against a former coworker who has sent her a series of hostile messages, including one saying she would regret reporting him. The state statute authorizes an order on a finding that a reasonable person would regard the messages as threatening; it requires no proof of the respondent's state of mind. The coworker argues that, after \textit{Counterman}, the order punishes his speech without proof of recklessness. \textit{Counterman}'s rationale---that an objective standard chills protected speech---applies to any state-imposed sanction for speech. But the Court decided a criminal case, the order carries no criminal penalty and only restricts future contact, and Justice Barrett's dissent flagged exactly this civil setting as unresolved. Whether the recklessness floor applies to civil protective orders remains open.
```

```

## 213_moody_v_netchoice

### Memory Jogger

```latex
The Court unanimously vacated both lower-court judgments because neither court applied the demanding test for facial First Amendment challenges; a majority (Kagan, J.) added that curating third-party posts in a feed is protected editorial expression that a State may not override to rebalance the marketplace of ideas (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/603/22-277/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{vacated and remanded} the judgments of the Eleventh and Fifth Circuits by a \textbf{9--0} vote on the judgment. The case was argued on February 26, 2024, and decided on July 1, 2024 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/603/22-277/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/22-277}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Kagan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Sotomayor, Kavanaugh, and Barrett, and by Justice Jackson as to Parts I, II, and III--A.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Barrett filed a concurring opinion. Justice Jackson filed an opinion concurring in part and concurring in the judgment. Justice Thomas filed an opinion concurring in the judgment. Justice Alito filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justices Thomas and Gorsuch.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Barrett

Justice Jackson

Justice Thomas

Justice Alito
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Facial Challenge Fails; As-Applied Claim Succeeds): A Viewpoint-Neutrality Mandate for ``All Large Online Platforms.''}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Unconstitutional As Applied): A Must-Carry Rule for Candidates' Posts.} A State requires video-sharing platforms to host every post by a candidate for state office and forbids them to remove, demote, or label any such post. A video platform whose published guidelines bar election misinformation brings an \emph{as-applied} challenge after the State fines it for labeling a candidate's false claim about voting dates. Under \textit{Moody}, the platform's decisions about what to include, exclude, and label in its curated feed are protected editorial judgment, just as the newspaper's choices were in \textit{Tornillo}. Compelling it to carry and present candidates' posts unaltered changes its expressive product. The State's interest in giving candidates an equal voice is the kind of rebalancing interest \textit{Moody} held cannot justify overriding private editorial choices.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Likely Valid): Political-Affiliation Protection for Payment Apps and Messaging Conduits.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Likely Valid): Protecting Ride-Share Drivers From Deactivation for Political Views.} A State law forbids ride-share companies to deactivate drivers because of political opinions they express off the app. A ride-share company sues, arguing under \textit{Moody} that the law interferes with its editorial judgment. The argument is weak. \textit{Moody}'s protection attaches to compiling and presenting third-party speech as a curated expressive product, such as a news feed. A ride-share service matches riders with drivers; deciding which drivers may use the app does not express any message, and the law does not require the company to display or endorse anyone's speech. As in \textit{PruneYard} and \textit{FAIR}, the regulated party is not engaged in its own expression, so the law is ordinary economic regulation outside \textit{Moody}'s editorial-discretion holding.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Open Question): A Feed Ranked Only by an Engagement Algorithm.} A State forbids platforms to rank posts on their main feeds based on the viewpoint expressed. A platform challenges the law as applied to a feed ranked entirely by an algorithm that maximizes predicted user engagement and applies no content standards or community guidelines of its own. For the platform: designing the algorithm is an editorial choice about how to present speech, and \textit{Moody} protects compiling and organizing third-party posts into a distinctive product. For the State: the Court expressly reserved feeds driven solely by user-engagement algorithms without independent content standards, and Justice Barrett questioned whether such automated sorting reflects any human expressive judgment. Because \textit{Moody} left the question open, the result is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 214_zelman_v_simmons_harris

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Upholding Cleveland's school-voucher program 5--4, the Court held that a religion-neutral aid program that gives assistance to a broad class of families, who then direct it to religious schools through their own genuine and independent private choice, does not violate the Establishment Clause (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/536/639/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the Sixth Circuit by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on February 20, 2002, and decided on June 27, 2002 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/536/639/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Rehnquist delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice O'Connor and Justice Thomas each filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Stevens filed a dissenting opinion. Justice Souter filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Justice Breyer filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Stevens and Souter.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice O'Connor

Justice Thomas

Justice Stevens

Justice Souter

Justice Breyer
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Aid Upheld): An Education Savings Account in a County With Only Religious Schools.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Establishment Clause Excuse): Excluding Religious Schools From the ESA.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Aid Invalid): Vouchers Redeemable Only at One Denomination's Schools.} A State offers tuition vouchers to low-income families but allows them to be redeemed only at schools affiliated with a single named church, on the theory that its schools have the best record in the district. Parents still choose whether to use the voucher, but the program is not neutral with respect to religion: the State itself, not independent private choice, has selected which religion's schools may receive the money. \textit{Zelman} protected Ohio's program because it was neutral and open to religious and secular schools alike; a program that prefers one faith fails that threshold requirement and violates the Establishment Clause (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/536/639/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Private Choice): Direct Grants Earmarked for Religious Instruction.} A State appropriates funds paid directly to religious schools, in amounts set by the State, to pay for daily religion classes and chapel services, without any family deciding where the money goes. \textit{Zelman} drew a sharp line between such direct aid and programs of true private choice. Here the circuit between government and religion is never broken: the State itself sends tax money to finance religious teaching and worship, so the advancement of religion is attributable to the government rather than to parents, and the program cannot claim \textit{Zelman}'s protection.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Choice of Rehabilitation Programs With a Long Secular Waitlist.} A State lets nonviolent offenders avoid jail by completing a funded residential rehabilitation program of their choice. Most participating providers are faith-based programs that include worship, while the only secular provider has a six-month waitlist. The program is neutral on its face and the money follows each participant's choice, which points toward \textit{Zelman}. But \textit{Zelman} stressed that the choice must be genuine and that adequate secular options must be available, and an offender facing jail in the meantime may have what Justice Souter called a Hobson's choice. Whether a court counts the waitlisted secular option as a real alternative, and whether the coercive criminal setting matters, could decide the case.
```

## 215_mahmoud_v_taylor

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Holding 6--3 that a Maryland school board burdened parents' free exercise by requiring young children to receive normative LGBTQ+-inclusive storybook instruction without notice or opt-outs, the Court applied \textit{Wisconsin v. Yoder} to trigger strict scrutiny without asking whether the policy was neutral and generally applicable (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/606/24-297/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued on April 22, 2025, and decided on June 27, 2025 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/606/24-297/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/24-297}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Alito delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Thomas, Gorsuch, Kavanaugh, and Barrett.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Thomas filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Sotomayor filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Kagan and Jackson.
\end{itemize}
```

### Holding

```latex
\textbf{Question Presented:}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Extension to Parental Notification of Gender Transition:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Parents Prevail): A Workbook Hostile to Religion.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Parents Prevail): Lessons Designed to Change Children's Religious Practices.} A district adopts a mandatory kindergarten-through-second-grade ``fairness'' unit in which teachers read stories portraying religious dietary and modesty rules as unfair to others and follow scripts telling children who mention such rules that they should rethink them. The district refuses notice or opt-outs, although it excuses students from other units for medical and language reasons. As in \textit{Mahmoud}, the instruction is aimed at very young children, is normative rather than informational, and pressures children to abandon practices their parents are trying to instill---a burden ``of the same character as that in \textit{Yoder}.'' Strict scrutiny applies, and the district's existing opt-out mechanisms undercut any claim that excusing these students is unworkable (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/606/24-297/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (School District Prevails): Neutral High-School Science.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Right to the Relief Sought): Removing Books for Every Student.} Parents who object to an elementary reading unit do not ask for notice or an opt-out for their own children; instead they sue to require the district to remove the books from the curriculum for all students. \textit{Mahmoud} protects parents' right to direct the religious upbringing of \textit{their own} children, and the relief it ordered was advance notice and the opportunity to excuse those children. It does not give parents a veto over what a public school teaches other families' children. The parents' claim fails, although they may still seek notice and an opt-out under \textit{Mahmoud}.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Middle-School Health Lesson on Gender Identity.} A district's mandatory seventh-grade health unit explains, in factual terms, that some people identify with a gender different from their sex at birth, and teachers are told to require students to treat classmates respectfully but not to correct students' religious views. Religious parents demand notice and an opt-out, which the district refuses. The parents can argue that the lesson conveys a message contrary to their faith during formative years. The district can respond that twelve-year-olds are less impressionable than \textit{Mahmoud}'s five-to-eleven-year-olds and that the unit is informational ``exposure'' rather than the ``unmistakably normative'' instruction the Court condemned. Where the line falls is unsettled.
```

```latex
\item \textbf{Narrowed (\textit{Nixon v. Administrator of General Services}):}

\item \textbf{Applied (\textit{Selective Service System v. MPIRG}):}

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Attainder): Barring Members of a Named Protest Group From Utility Boards.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Attainder): A Rider Cutting Off Named Officials' Salaries.} After a committee hearing in which members accuse three named career employees of an executive agency of disloyalty, Congress attaches a rider to an appropriations bill providing that no funds may be used to pay their salaries unless they are reappointed by the President with Senate confirmation. The employees sue for their pay. This is the pattern of \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/328/303/}{\textit{United States v. Lovett}}, 328 U.S. 303 (1946), on which \textit{Brown} relied: Congress identified specific people, found them blameworthy without a trial, and imposed a permanent bar from government employment, a vocational disqualification that \textit{Brown} recognized as classic attainder punishment. That Congress acted through its spending power, or claimed only to protect the agency in the future, does not help; \textit{Brown} held that preventive aims are still punishment.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Valid General Rule): Disqualification After an Individualized Hearing.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Likely Valid): A Procurement Ban Naming a Foreign-Controlled Vendor.} A statute

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Defunding a Named Domestic Nonprofit.} After news reports that some employees of a national community-organizing nonprofit gave improper advice to clients, Congress enacts a law barring that organization, by name, and its affiliates from receiving any federal grants or contracts. No court or agency has found the organization liable for anything. Under \textit{Brown}, the law looks like an attainder: Congress named a specific group, made its own findings of wrongdoing, and imposed a sanction without a trial, and congressional statements condemning the group may show punitive motive. Under \textit{Nixon} and \textit{Selective Service}, however, denying discretionary federal funding is not a traditional attainder penalty like imprisonment or a bar from a profession, and Congress can point to a nonpunitive purpose of protecting public funds. Which test dominates, and how heavily the legislative record weighs, makes the outcome uncertain.
```

## 217_nixon_v_administrator_of_general_services

### Memory Jogger

```latex
After Watergate, Congress seized former President Nixon's papers and tapes for archival screening; the Court held 7--2 that the Act was not a bill of attainder (Nixon was a legitimate ``class of one'' and was not punished) and did not violate separation of powers, presidential privilege, privacy, or association rights (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/433/425/}{Justia}).
```

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Brennan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Justices Stewart, Marshall, and Stevens; by Justice White in all but Part VII; by Justice Powell in all but Parts IV and V; and by Justice Blackmun as to Part VII.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Stevens filed a concurring opinion. Justices White, Blackmun, and Powell each filed an opinion concurring in part and concurring in the judgment.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Chief Justice Burger and Justice Rehnquist each filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```
