### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Bill of Attainder): A Procurement Ban Naming One Company.}

. Under \textit{Nixon},

: declining to buy from a vendor is not a traditional attainder sanction, the ban reasonably serves the nonpunitive goal of protecting federal systems, and a record of documented security risks shows regulatory concern rather than legislative condemnation. Like Nixon, Apex may be a legitimate class of one if it alone presents the identified risk.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Bill of Attainder): Securing One Official's Records.} After a former Cabinet secretary leaves office, Congress learns that she conducted official business on a private email server and that her personal attorney plans to delete messages. Congress passes a statute naming her and directing the National Archives to take custody of the server, have career archivists separate official from personal messages, return personal messages to her, and pay just compensation for any property taken. As in \textit{Nixon}, she is a legitimate class of one because only her records face imminent destruction; custody and screening are not traditional punishments; the statute furthers the nonpunitive goal of preserving public records; and the compensation and return provisions negate any inference of punitive confiscation.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Bill of Attainder): Uncompensated Forfeiture Amid Condemnation.} Suppose the same statute also contains a provision

Under \textit{Nixon},

, much as the Elizabeth Morgan Act failed in \textit{Foretich}. Unlike the PRMPA, which provided just compensation and served evidence-preservation goals, the forfeiture is a severe deprivation that does nothing to address the security risk and rests on legislative condemnation.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Separation-of-Powers Problem): Congress Takes the Records Itself.} Congress passes a statute transferring custody of a departing President's records to a House committee, which may review and publish any record it chooses, including privileged national-security advice. The incumbent President, who supports the outgoing President's privilege claims, objects. \textit{Nixon} upheld the PRMPA because the records stayed under Executive Branch control, screening was done by career archivists, Congress received no privileged access, and the incumbent supported the law. Each of those features is missing here. A court applying \textit{Nixon}'s disruption test would likely find that the statute prevents the Executive Branch from protecting confidential communications without an overriding need to justify it.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unresolved): Waiver by a Hostile Successor.}

The incumbent's support favors disclosure under \textit{Nixon}, but unlike in \textit{Nixon}, publication rather than archival screening is at stake and no evidentiary need is present, so the outcome is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 218_sveen_v_melin

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Minnesota's revocation-on-divorce statute, applied retroactively, cut an ex-wife out as beneficiary of a life-insurance policy bought before the law passed; an 8--1 Court held there was no Contracts Clause violation because the law did not substantially impair the contract---it tracks likely intent, does what a divorce court could do, and can be undone with a simple form (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/584/16-1432/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} the Eighth Circuit by an \textbf{8--1} vote. The case was argued on March 19, 2018, and decided on June 11, 2018 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/584/16-1432/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/16-1432}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Kagan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer, Alito, and Sotomayor.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Gorsuch filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Substantial Impairment): Revocation on Divorce for a Payable-on-Death Account.} In 2010 a depositor opens a bank account payable on his death to his then-wife. In 2015 the State extends its revocation-on-divorce statute to payable-on-death accounts, applying it to existing accounts. The couple divorces in 2018, the depositor never changes the designation, and on his death in 2023 his children and his ex-wife both claim the funds. All three of \textit{Sveen}'s factors favor the State: the statute approximates what most divorced depositors would want, a divorce court could always have reallocated the account, and the depositor could have restored his ex-wife as beneficiary with a simple form. The law does not substantially impair the contract, so the inquiry ends at step one (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/584/16-1432/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Substantial Impairment): A Filing Requirement for Old Mineral Interests.} A State enacts a law providing that a severed mineral interest unused for twenty years lapses and reverts to the surface owner unless the owner files a short statement of claim in the county records within two years after the law's enactment. A mineral owner whose interest predates the statute misses the deadline and loses the interest. \textit{Sveen} relied on a long line of decisions upholding retroactive recording and filing requirements, including \textit{Texaco, Inc.\ v.\ Short}, 454 U.S.\ 516 (1982), which sustained a law of this kind. Because the owner could have preserved the right with minimal paperwork, the law does not substantially impair the contract.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Substantial Impairment): Retroactively Voiding Non-Competes and Guaranties.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Law Invalid): A State Repeals Its Own Bond Covenant.} To raise money for mass transit, a State repeals a statutory covenant, made to purchasers of its bonds, that limited the use of pledged toll revenues to protect bondholders' security. Bondholders sue. Unlike Minnesota's neutral default rule for private beneficiary designations, the repeal destroys a core term of the bargain, defeats bondholders' reasonable expectations, and cannot be undone by any action of theirs, so it substantially impairs the contract. And because the State is impairing its own financial obligation, the step-two inquiry is not deferential: under \textit{United States Trust Co.\ v.\ New Jersey}, 431 U.S.\ 1 (1977), which struck down a repeal of this kind, the impairment must be reasonable and necessary, and less drastic alternatives were available.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Revocation Rule That Is Hard to Undo.} A State's revocation-on-divorce statute applies retroactively to existing life-insurance policies but provides that a policyholder may re-designate a former spouse only by petitioning a court, paying a filing fee, and appearing at a hearing. The first two \textit{Sveen} factors still favor the State: the law reflects probable intent and does what a divorce court could have done. The third factor, however, now cuts the other way, because the policyholder can no longer undo the default ``with the stroke of a pen.'' \textit{Sveen} relied on the three features together and did not say whether the first two suffice alone, so whether this law substantially impairs the contract---and must face step-two review---is genuinely unclear.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Case Metadata: parallel S. Ct. citation, docket number, Eighth Circuit citation, constitutional-provision line, SupremeCourt.gov link.
- Legacy section in full (no Subsequent Developments subsection): introductory paragraphs; 'Pandemic Eviction Moratoria and Guaranty Laws' item and 'Post-Pandemic Deference Through 2026' item (lower-court and state-court decisions and scholarship, not Supreme Court decisions applying or narrowing Sveen).
- Exam Hypothetical 2 ('Opposite Side' Contrast with U.S. Trust and Spannaus) - a doctrinal contrast rather than a fact pattern; Example 4 now gives a U.S. Trust fact pattern.

**Notes:**

- Example 3 (Neon's Hypothetical 1) cites Melendez, a Second Circuit decision, as Neon wrote it.

## 219_smith_v_doe

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Alaska applied its sex offender registration and Internet notification law to offenders convicted before it was enacted; the Court held 6--3 that the law was a civil, nonpunitive regulation under the two-step intent--effects test, so its retroactive application did not violate the Ex Post Facto Clause (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/538/84/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} the Ninth Circuit by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued on November 13, 2002, and decided on March 5, 2003 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/538/84/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Kennedy delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, and Thomas.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Thomas filed a concurring opinion. Justice Souter filed an opinion concurring in the judgment.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Stevens filed a dissenting opinion. Justice Ginsburg filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Breyer.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Step One: Legislative Intent}

\subsubsection{Step Two: Punitive Effects (\textit{Mendoza-Martinez} Guideposts)}

Justice Thomas

Justice Souter

Justice Stevens

Justice Ginsburg, joined by Justice Breyer,
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Civil Regulation; No Ex Post Facto Violation): A Retroactive Financial-Fraud Registry.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Civil Regulation; No Ex Post Facto Violation): Civil Commitment of a Dangerous Offender.} As an inmate convicted of child sexual abuse nears release, the State petitions under a newly enacted statute to commit him to a secure treatment facility as a sexually violent predator, relying partly on conduct that predates the law. The statute is placed in the probate code, requires proof of a mental abnormality making the person dangerous, and provides for release once he is no longer dangerous. Under the intent--effects test that \textit{Smith} drew from \textit{Kansas v.\ Hendricks}, 521 U.S.\ 346 (1997), the legislature's civil purpose controls unless the clearest proof shows punitive effect, and \textit{Hendricks} upheld materially identical commitment against an Ex Post Facto challenge.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Punishment): Residency Bans Enforced by the Sentencing Court.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Punishment): Applying Harsher Sentencing Guidelines to an Earlier Crime.} A defendant commits bank fraud in 2005 and is sentenced in 2010 under amended federal Sentencing Guidelines that raise his recommended range well above the range in effect when he acted. No one disputes that a prison sentence is punishment, so there is no civil label for \textit{Smith}'s intent--effects inquiry to test; the only question is whether the change increases the punishment attached to his crime. In \textit{Peugh v.\ United States}, 569 U.S.\ 530 (2013), the Court held that sentencing a defendant under Guidelines promulgated after the offense, when they yield a higher range, violates the Ex Post Facto Clause, even though the Guidelines are advisory.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Lifetime GPS Monitoring Run by the Corrections Department.} A State requires every registrant convicted of an aggravated sex offense, including those convicted before the law took effect, to wear a GPS ankle monitor for life, with the corrections department tracking his location around the clock. The statute declares a public-safety purpose and sits in the civil code, so Step One favors the State. At Step Two, however, the device is a continuous physical burden supervised by corrections officers, resembling the probation and supervised release that \textit{Smith} distinguished because Alaska's registrants could move and work without supervision. Whether that restraint supplies the clearest proof of punishment, or is a reasonable means of protecting the public, has divided courts and is uncertain.
```

- Reasoning subsection headings re-created as \subsubsection headings (new text) so they nest under the majority subsection.

Kebodeaux, which merely assumed SORNA's validity); 'Federal Circuit Divergence on Expanded Registries' item (lower-court decisions, including November 2026 Sixth Circuit rulings); 'State Constitutional Pushback' item (state-law decisions). ## 220_moore_v_united_states

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Taxpayers who owned 13\% of an Indian company challenged the 2017 Mandatory Repatriation Tax as an unapportioned tax on unrealized income; the Court held 7--2 that Congress may attribute an entity's realized but undistributed income to its shareholders and tax them on it, while reserving whether the Constitution requires realization (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/602/22-800/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Ninth Circuit by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued December 5, 2023, and decided June 20, 2024 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/602/22-800/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/22-800_2024-06-20}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Kavanaugh delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Sotomayor, Kagan, and Jackson.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Jackson filed a concurring opinion. Justice Barrett filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justice Alito.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Thomas filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Gorsuch.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Constitutional Architecture of the Taxing Power}

\subsubsection{Entity Realization and Attribution Precedents}

\subsubsection{Longstanding Congressional Practice and Fiscal Consequences}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Attribution Valid): An Annual Tax on U.S. Shareholders of Foreign Subsidiaries.} Congress enacts a permanent annual tax requiring every U.S.\ person who owns at least 10\% of a controlled foreign corporation to report and pay tax on his pro rata share of the corporation's realized net business profits for that year, whether or not the profits are distributed. The corporation pays no U.S.\ tax on those profits. A 15\% shareholder argues that she has received nothing and so has no ``income.'' Under \textit{Moore}, the tax is valid without apportionment: the corporation has realized the income, and Congress may attribute that realized, undistributed income to its shareholders and tax them on their shares, just as the Mandatory Repatriation Tax and Subpart F do (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/602/22-800/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Attribution Valid): Partners Taxed on Profits a Partnership Retains.} A domestic real-estate partnership earns \$10 million in rental profits in a year and, as its agreement permits, distributes nothing to its partners. Congress requires each partner to pay income tax on his share of the partnership's profits, and a minority partner who cannot force a distribution objects that he is being taxed on money he never received. This is the paradigm case \textit{Moore} relied on. The partnership realized the income, the partners are taxed on their pro rata shares, and the entity itself is not taxed. The precedents the Court cited, especially \textit{Heiner v.\ Mellon}, 304 U.S.\ 271 (1938), upheld taxing partners on undistributed profits even when state law barred distribution, so the tax needs no apportionment.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (\textit{Moore} Does Not Apply): A Mark-to-Market Tax on Individual Holdings.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Arbitrary Attribution): Taxing a Corporation's Customers on Its Profits.} Congress provides that the undistributed profits of any foreign corporation shall be attributed to the U.S.\ persons who bought its products during the year, in proportion to their purchases, and taxed to them. A U.S.\ retailer that bought inventory from a foreign manufacturer, and owns none of its stock, is assessed tax on part of the manufacturer's earnings. \textit{Moore} permits Congress to attribute an entity's realized income to the entity's \emph{shareholders or partners}; it does not permit attribution to strangers to the entity. The Court also noted that Due Process bars arbitrary attribution, citing \textit{Burnet v.\ Wells}, 289 U.S.\ 670 (1933). Because the retailer has no ownership interest and no claim to the profits, the attribution is arbitrary and the tax cannot be sustained on \textit{Moore}'s reasoning.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (How Far Attribution Reaches): A Pass-Through Tax on Shareholders of Public Companies.}

Because the majority decided only the controlled-foreign-corporation tax before it, and four Justices doubted that corporate form may be disregarded for passive investors, the outcome is uncertain.
```

## 221_craig_v_missouri

### Memory Jogger

```latex
For a 4--3 Court, Chief Justice Marshall held that Missouri's interest-bearing loan-office certificates, issued on the faith of the state and designed to circulate as money, were unconstitutional ``bills of credit'' under Article I, Section 10, even though they were not legal tender, so a note given for them was void (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/29/410/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the Supreme Court of Missouri by a \textbf{4--3} vote. The case was argued on March 2--3, 1830, and decided on March 12, 1830 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/29/410/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Marshall delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Duvall, Story, and Baldwin.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justices Johnson, Thompson, and McLean each filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Johnson

Justice Thompson

Justice McLean

\item \textbf{Narrowed by \textit{Briscoe}:}

\item \textbf{Later Narrowing Decisions:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Unconstitutional): Faith-Backed Fiscal Liquidity Certificates.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Unconstitutional): Interest-Bearing Scrip Backed by a Dedicated Fund.} During a cash shortage, a State's comptroller pays state employees and contractors with interest-bearing scrip in \$1, \$5, and \$10 denominations, payable to bearer and receivable for all state taxes and fees. The statute pledges the revenue of the state lottery to redeem the scrip within three years. The State argues that interest makes the scrip's value fluctuate and that a dedicated fund, not bare state credit, stands behind it. Those were the arguments of Justices Johnson and Thompson in dissent, and the \textit{Craig} majority rejected them: Missouri's certificates also bore interest and rested on a pledged fund. Because the scrip is issued directly by state officers in small denominations designed to circulate as money, it is a prohibited bill of credit.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Constitutional): Ordinary State Bonds Sold to Investors.} To finance highway construction, a State sells \$800 million in twenty-year general-obligation bonds in \$5,000 denominations to banks, pension funds, and other investors, pledging its full faith and credit to repay them with interest. A taxpayer argues that paper issued on the faith of the State is a bill of credit under \textit{Craig}. The argument fails. Marshall explained that the Bills of Credit Clause does not bar ordinary state borrowing or contracts to pay for goods and services; it forbids paper intended to circulate through the community for its ordinary purposes as money. Large-denomination investment bonds are a loan to the State, not a medium of exchange, so they are not bills of credit even though they rest on the State's credit.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Constitutional Under \textit{Briscoe}): Notes of a State-Owned Corporate Bank.} A State charters a bank, owned entirely by the State and governed by directors chosen by the legislature, that can sue and be sued in its own name. The bank lends its own small-denomination notes to borrowers, and the notes circulate as currency; they are redeemable from the bank's own capital and are not issued on the faith of the State. Under \textit{Briscoe v.\ Bank of Commonwealth of Kentucky}, 36 U.S.\ (11 Pet.) 257 (1837), the notes are not bills of credit because a separately incorporated, suable entity issues them on its own corporate funds. Justice Story's \textit{Briscoe} dissent, invoking \textit{Craig}'s rule that the Constitution prohibits things rather than names, would treat them as an indirect emission, but \textit{Briscoe} remains the governing rule.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unsettled): A Reserve-Backed State Digital Token.}

The Court has never applied \textit{Craig} or \textit{Briscoe} to a digital instrument, so which line controls is uncertain.
```

## 222_poindexter_v_greenhow

### Memory Jogger

```latex
In the lead \textit{Virginia Coupon Case}, a 5--4 Court held that Virginia's tax-receivable bond coupons were valid contracts, not forbidden bills of credit; that the 1882 law refusing them impaired the obligation of contracts; and that a taxpayer could sue the collector who seized his desk because an officer enforcing a void statute is not the State (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/114/270/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on March 20 and 23--25, 1885, and decided on April 20, 1885 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/114/270/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/114/270}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Matthews delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Field, Harlan, Woods, and Blatchford.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Bradley filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Chief Justice Waite and Justices Miller and Gray, reported at \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/114/325/}{114 U.S. 330} following \textit{Marye v.\ Parsons}.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Bills of Credit:}

\item \textbf{The Officer-Suit Exception:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Officer Suit Allowed): Refusing Bond Coupons and Levying on a Company's Property.} A State issues twenty-year bonds whose interest coupons, by statute, are receivable for all state taxes at maturity. A later legislature forbids revenue officers to accept the coupons. A manufacturer tenders matured coupons for its franchise tax; the state revenue officer rejects them and seizes the company's delivery truck for nonpayment. The company sues the officer personally to recover the truck. Under \textit{Poindexter}, the receivability promise is a contract the later statute unconstitutionally impairs, the tender is equivalent to payment, and the void statute gives the officer no authority. He is therefore answerable as an individual wrongdoer, and the Eleventh Amendment does not bar the suit (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/114/270/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Not a Bill of Credit): Tax-Anticipation Warrants Receivable at Maturity.} To cover a seasonal cash shortfall, a State issues interest-bearing warrants in \$5{,}000 denominations, payable in one year and receivable for state taxes only once they mature. When the State later tries to disown the warrants by arguing that they were unconstitutional bills of credit, a holder sues. The argument fails under \textit{Poindexter}. The warrants are evidence of debt designed to be paid at maturity, not paper designed to circulate from hand to hand as money; their large denomination and maturity-only receivability confirm that the State did not intend them as currency. They are therefore valid obligations the State must honor.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Likely Bills of Credit): Small-Denomination Tax Credit Notes.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Suit Barred): Ordering the Treasurer to Pay Overdue Coupons.} After a State defaults on its bonds, a bondholder sues the state treasurer in federal court for an order directing him to pay the overdue interest coupons out of the state treasury. Unlike Poindexter, the bondholder does not complain that an officer has committed an affirmative wrong against his property; he seeks to compel the State itself to perform its contract with public funds. \textit{Poindexter} expressly distinguished such suits, which \textit{Louisiana v.\ Jumel}, 107 U.S.\ 711 (1883), held barred, and \textit{In re Ayers} confirmed that a suit seeking only to compel performance of a state contract is a suit against the State. The Eleventh Amendment bars it.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): An Injunction Ordering Collectors to Accept Coupons.} Instead of waiting for a seizure, a taxpayer holding matured coupons sues the state tax commissioner in federal court for an injunction requiring all collectors to accept the coupons for taxes in the future. \textit{Poindexter} supports the claim on the merits, and \textit{Ex parte Young}, which built on \textit{Poindexter}, permits prospective injunctions against officials enforcing unconstitutional laws. But the order would require officials to take affirmative action that compels the State to honor its contract, which looks like the specific performance \textit{Poindexter} and \textit{In re Ayers} treated as a suit against the State. Whether a court characterizes the relief as stopping enforcement of a void law or as enforcing the State's contract would likely decide the case.
```

## 223_fauntleroy_v_lum

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A Missouri judgment on an award arising from a Mississippi cotton-futures contract that was illegal in Mississippi had to be enforced in Mississippi: the Court held 5--4, per Justice Holmes, that full faith and credit bars collateral attack on a sister-state judgment for errors of law, leaving only want of jurisdiction as a defense (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/210/230/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the Supreme Court of Mississippi by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on April 27--28, 1908, and decided on May 18, 1908 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/210/230/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Holmes delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Fuller and Justices Brewer, Peckham, and Moody.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice White filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Harlan, McKenna, and Day.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Extension to Jurisdiction-Denying Statutes:}

\item \textbf{No Public-Policy Exception Reaffirmed:} In

\item \textbf{Applied to Adoption Decrees:} In
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Judgment Must Be Enforced): An Illegal Sports Wager.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Judgment Must Be Enforced): Damages for Breach of a Non-Compete.} State C's statutes declare employee non-compete agreements void as against public policy. An employer sues a former employee in State D, where the employee worked and where non-competes are enforceable, and obtains a final money judgment for breach of a non-compete covering State C. The employee moves home to State C, and the employer seeks to enforce the judgment there. State C's courts may not refuse on the ground that the underlying agreement offends State C's policy. State D had jurisdiction over the parties and the claim, so under \textit{Fauntleroy} its judgment is conclusive even if it misjudged which State's law should govern; the employee's remedy was appeal in State D (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/210/230/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Enforcement May Be Refused): A State Civil Penalty Against an Absent Defendant.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Decree Need Not Be Obeyed): An Injunction Against Testifying.}

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Private Bounty Judgment.} State E authorizes any private person to sue anyone who assists a specified activity and to recover at least \$10,000 per violation, without proof of personal injury. A State E court, with personal jurisdiction over the defendant, enters such a judgment, and the plaintiff seeks to enforce it in State F, where the activity is lawful. \textit{Fauntleroy} forbids State F from refusing merely because the judgment offends its public policy. State F's best argument is the penal-judgment exception of \textit{Huntington v. Attrill}: the award looks like a fine for a public wrong. But the plaintiff is a private party collecting a civil award, which resembles the private recovery enforced in \textit{Fauntleroy}. Which side of that line a bounty judgment falls on is unsettled.
```

## 224_baker_v_general_motors_corp

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A Michigan consent injunction barring a former GM engineer from testifying against GM could not stop him from testifying, under subpoena, in a Missouri wrongful-death suit brought by strangers to the Michigan case: sister-state judgments get exacting full faith and credit with no public-policy exception, but enforcement measures do not travel and a decree cannot control other courts' litigation (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/522/222/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{reversed and remanded} (9--0 in the judgment), though the Justices split 5--4 on the rationale. The case was argued on October 15, 1997, and decided on January 13, 1998 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/522/222/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/96-653}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Ginsburg delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices Stevens, Souter, and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Scalia concurred in the judgment. Justice Kennedy concurred in the judgment, joined by Justices O'Connor and Thomas.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Testimony Allowed): A Gagged Pharmaceutical Scientist.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Forum Law Controls Enforcement): Garnishment Under the Forum's Rules.} A creditor wins a valid money judgment in State~A, whose law allows garnishment of up to half of a debtor's wages, and registers it in State~B, where the debtor now lives and works. State~B caps wage garnishment at a much lower percentage and exempts the debtor's homestead. The creditor argues that full faith and credit requires State~B to apply State~A's garnishment rules. Under \textit{Baker}, State~B must recognize the judgment and treat the debt as conclusively established; it may not relitigate liability. But the Clause does not require a State to adopt another State's time, manner, and mechanisms for enforcing judgments. Enforcement measures do not travel with the judgment, so State~B's evenhanded garnishment limits and exemptions govern collection.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Full Faith and Credit Binds): A Money Judgment on a Confidentiality Settlement.} Suppose

State~B must enforce the judgment. Unlike the injunction in \textit{Baker}, the judgment does not try to control State~B's courts or witnesses; it simply fixes a debt between parties over whom State~A had jurisdiction. \textit{Baker} reaffirmed that there is no roving public-policy exception for judgments, and \textit{Fauntleroy v. Lum}, 210 U.S. 230 (1908), required enforcement of a judgment on a claim the forum State itself would not have enforced.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Preclusion Binds the Bakers): The Bakers Litigate and Lose in Michigan.}

The Bakers would then be parties bound by the Michigan judgment, and the Michigan court would have had adjudicatory authority over them. \textit{Baker}'s rule that a judgment rendered by a court with jurisdiction over the persons it governs qualifies for recognition throughout the land would require the Missouri federal court to give the privilege ruling the same issue-preclusive effect it has in Michigan, whatever Missouri's policy favoring disclosure.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unsettled): An Out-of-State Judgment Against a Shield-Law Physician.} A court in State~A, where abortion is banned, enters a civil money judgment against a physician licensed in State~B for prescribing abortion medication by telehealth to a State~A resident. State~B has enacted a shield law forbidding its officials to enforce such judgments, and a State~B court clerk refuses to file it. Under \textit{Baker}'s exacting rule, State~B's contrary policy cannot justify refusal, which suggests the judgment must be enforced. But the physician may argue that the judgment is penal and so falls outside full faith and credit under \textit{Huntington v. Attrill}, 146 U.S. 657 (1892), or that State~A lacked personal jurisdiction over a physician who never entered State~A. How those doctrines apply to telehealth remains unsettled.
```

- Case Metadata: docket number, chapter line, lower-court line, provisions list (Justia and Cornell links kept in Judicial Votes).
- Concurring and Dissenting intro sentence (covered by Judicial Votes); the bold item labels.
- Legacy section: introductory paragraph (restates the two rules); 'Records, Executive Officials, and Congress's Effects Power' item (Adar v. Smith, a lower-court decision; Respect for Marriage Act, legislation not applying Baker); 'Interstate Shield-Law Litigation (2025--2026)' item (ongoing lower-court litigation and commentary; its issue is reframed as the Fence-Sitter hypothetical without the post-2023 specifics). Kept: 'Post-Baker Supreme Court Reaffirmations' item (Hyatt I/III, Lance v. Dennis, V.L. v. E.L.).

## 225_puerto_rico_v_branstad

### Memory Jogger

```latex
When Iowa's Governor refused to extradite a man charged with murder in Puerto Rico, the Court unanimously overruled \textit{Kentucky v.\ Dennison} and held that federal courts may order a governor by mandamus to perform the mandatory duty to surrender a fugitive, and that Puerto Rico may enforce that duty under the federal Extradition Act (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/483/219/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the Eighth Circuit by a \textbf{9--0} vote on the judgment (6--3 as to Part II-B's constitutional holding). The case was argued on March 30, 1987, and decided on June 23, 1987 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/483/219/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Marshall delivered the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices Brennan, White, Blackmun, and Stevens; joined in Parts I, II-A, II-C, and III by Justices Powell and O'Connor; and joined in part by Justice Scalia.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice O'Connor, joined by Justice Powell, filed an opinion concurring in part and concurring in the judgment. Justice Scalia filed an opinion concurring in part and concurring in the judgment.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice O'Connor

Justice Scalia

\item \textbf{No Asylum-State Defenses:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Extradition Compelled): A Shield Law Cannot Protect an Actual Fugitive.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Extradition Compelled): Objection to the Demanding State's Punishment.} A man charged with capital murder in State X is arrested in State Y, which has abolished the death penalty. State X's extradition papers are in order, but State Y's Governor refuses to surrender him unless State X agrees not to seek a death sentence. Under \emph{Branstad}, the commands of the Extradition Clause are mandatory and leave the asylum State's executive no discretion, and \emph{Michigan v.\ Doran} limits the asylum State's review to whether the papers are in order and the person is the charged fugitive. The Governor's moral objection to the possible punishment, like Iowa's doubts about a fair trial in Puerto Rico, is no defense; State X may obtain a writ of mandamus, and the fugitive must raise his objections in State X's courts.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Mandatory Duty): A Non-Fugitive Telehealth Prescriber and a Commandeering Statute.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Mandatory Duty): A Demand From a Foreign Country.} A Canadian province charges a man with fraud and asks the Governor of a neighboring State, where he now lives, to surrender him. The Governor declines, and the province asks a federal court for mandamus under \emph{Branstad}. The request fails. \emph{Branstad} enforces a duty created by Article IV and the Extradition Act, and those provisions govern demands by States and, under \S~3182, by Territories; they say nothing about foreign nations. Extradition to another country runs through treaties and the national government, not through a governor's duty under Article IV, so no ministerial obligation exists for a federal court to compel.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unresolved): Puerto Rico's Demand After \emph{Printz}.} Puerto Rico again demands the extradition of a fugitive who fled to a State, and the State's Governor refuses. Relying on \emph{Branstad}'s statutory holding, Puerto Rico seeks mandamus under \S~3182. The Governor answers that Article IV's Extradition Clause speaks only of ``States,'' so the duty to surrender fugitives to a Territory rests on an Act of Congress alone, and \emph{Printz} forbids Congress to commandeer state executive officers. \emph{Branstad} answers the remedial question in Puerto Rico's favor, but Justice Scalia expressly noted that no party had questioned Congress's power to require extradition from a State to a Territory, and the Court has not addressed it since the modern anti-commandeering cases, so the result is uncertain.
```

- Case Metadata: docket number, Briefing Book Placement, provisions list, duplicate links.
- Legacy section: opening sentences of first paragraph; second paragraph (anti-commandeering reconciliation with New York, Printz, Murphy - commentary; Printz and Murphy still appear in Examples 3 and 5); third paragraph (2025-2026 shield-law conflicts, Louisiana/New York Carpenter dispute, August 2026 Texas/Minnesota dispute, Vladeck commentary). Kept: Ortiz item.

## 226_bivens_v_six_unknown_named_agents

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Federal narcotics agents raided Webster Bivens's apartment without a warrant, manacled him before his family, and strip-searched him; a 6--3 Court held that a victim of a Fourth Amendment violation by federal officers may sue them for money damages directly under the Constitution, absent special factors counseling hesitation or an equally effective congressional remedy (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/403/388/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} the Second Circuit by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued on January 12, 1971, and decided on June 21, 1971 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/403/388/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/403/388}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Brennan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Douglas, Stewart, White, and Marshall.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Harlan filed an opinion concurring in the judgment.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Chief Justice Burger, Justice Black, and Justice Blackmun each filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Extensions (\textit{Davis} and \textit{Carlson}):}

\item \textbf{Retrenchment (\textit{Abbasi} and \textit{Egbert}):}

\item \textbf{\textit{Goldey v.\ Fields} (2025):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (\textit{Bivens} Available): A DEA Home Raid.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (\textit{Bivens} Available): A Federal Prisoner Denied Emergency Medical Care.} A federal inmate suffering a severe asthma attack repeatedly asks Bureau of Prisons medical staff for help; they ignore him for hours, give him a medication they know is contraindicated, and he dies. His estate sues the officials for damages under the Eighth Amendment. The claim fits one of the three contexts in which the Court has recognized an implied damages remedy: \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/446/14/}{\textit{Carlson v.\ Green}, 446 U.S.\ 14 (1980)}, extended \textit{Bivens} to exactly this kind of deliberate indifference to a federal prisoner's serious medical needs and held that the Federal Tort Claims Act did not displace the remedy. Because the claim arises in an existing context, no new-context or special-factors analysis is needed.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (\textit{Bivens} Barred): A TSA Checkpoint Detention.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (\textit{Bivens} Barred): A Federal Employee Demoted for Public Criticism.} A federal agency engineer publicly criticizes his agency's safety program, and his supervisor demotes him in retaliation. The engineer sues the supervisor for damages under the First Amendment. Unlike the \textit{Bivens} plaintiff, he has access to an elaborate remedial scheme Congress created for federal personnel actions, including administrative appeals and back pay. In \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/462/367/}{\textit{Bush v.\ Lucas}, 462 U.S.\ 367 (1983)}, the Court refused to extend \textit{Bivens} on materially identical facts, deferring to Congress's comprehensive civil-service remedies; after \textit{Egbert}, the existence of such an alternative process forecloses a damages remedy even if it is less effective than damages.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (New Context?): An FBI Home Raid in a Fraud Investigation.} FBI agents investigating a bank-fraud scheme enter a suspect's home without a warrant or probable cause, handcuff him in front of his children, and strip-search him at the field office. He sues the agents for damages under the Fourth Amendment. The conduct mirrors \textit{Bivens} almost exactly, which favors the plaintiff. But \textit{Abbasi} treats differences in the agency involved and the legal mandate under which officers act as potentially meaningful, and \textit{Egbert} asks only whether there is any reason to think Congress better suited to create a remedy. Whether a different agency enforcing different statutes is a ``new context'' is unsettled, so the claim could survive or fail.
```

- 'Opposite Side' Contrast: second scenario (state officers and 42 U.S.C. 1983) - an exam tip about a different statute, not a Bivens application; the first scenario (DEA raid) is reused as Example 1.

```latex
, and travelers whose devices were searched sue the Secretary for damages (claim (b)).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No \textit{Bivens} Remedy): First Amendment Retaliation by a Federal Agent.} After a motel owner complains to an agent's supervisors about the agent's conduct on his property, the agent asks other agencies to investigate the owner's business. The owner sues the agent for damages, alleging retaliation for protected speech. The Court has never recognized a First Amendment \textit{Bivens} claim, so the case differs in a meaningful way from \textit{Bivens}, \textit{Davis}, and \textit{Carlson} and arises in a new context. These facts track \textit{Carlson v.\ Green} itself, one of the three cases in which the Court recognized a \textit{Bivens} remedy. ## 228_egbert_v_boule
```

### Memory Jogger

```latex
An innkeeper roughed up by a Border Patrol agent in his own driveway could not sue under \textit{Bivens}: the Court held 6--3 (Fourth Amendment) and 9--0 (First Amendment retaliation) that no implied damages remedy exists whenever there is any reason to think Congress is better equipped to create one (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/596/21-147/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the Ninth Circuit, by a \textbf{6--3} vote on the Fourth Amendment excessive-force claim and \textbf{9--0} on the First Amendment retaliation claim. The case was argued March 2, 2022, and decided June 8, 2022 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/596/21-147/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/21-147}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Thomas delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Alito, Kavanaugh, and Barrett.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Gorsuch filed an opinion concurring in the judgment. Justice Sotomayor, joined by Justices Breyer and Kagan, concurred in the judgment as to the First Amendment claim.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Sotomayor, joined by Justices Breyer and Kagan, dissented as to the Fourth Amendment claim.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No \textit{Bivens} Remedy): An FBI Raid Far From the Border.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No \textit{Bivens} Remedy): Retaliation by a Federal Inspector.} A restaurant owner publicly criticizes a federal food-safety agency. Days later, an agency inspector conducts an unannounced inspection and issues a string of citations that are later dismissed. The owner sues the inspector for damages under \textit{Bivens}, alleging First Amendment retaliation. \textit{Egbert} squarely forecloses the claim: all nine Justices agreed that no \textit{Bivens} action exists for First Amendment retaliation, because a plaintiff can easily allege a retaliatory motive for almost any adverse federal action, and permitting such suits would chill legitimate official conduct across the federal workforce. Whether to provide a damages remedy for retaliation by federal officers is for Congress (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/596/21-147/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Express Statutory Cause of Action): The Same Force by a State Trooper.} A state highway patrol trooper stops a driver, drags him from the car without provocation, and throws him to the pavement, breaking his collarbone. The driver sues the trooper for damages under 42 U.S.C.\ \S~1983. \textit{Egbert} poses no obstacle. Its reasoning rests on separation of powers: courts should not \emph{imply} a damages action that Congress has not created. Here Congress has expressly supplied the cause of action against persons acting under color of state law, so there is no question whether Congress or the courts should decide. The driver may proceed on his Fourth Amendment excessive-force claim, subject to ordinary defenses such as qualified immunity.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Prospective Relief): Enjoining an Ongoing Federal Practice.} Residents of a border town show that Border Patrol agents, under a written sector policy, routinely enter fenced private backyards without warrants or consent to look for migrants. The residents sue the agency head in her official capacity, seeking a declaratory judgment and an injunction against the practice; they seek no damages. \textit{Egbert} limits only implied retrospective money damages against individual officers. It does not withdraw the federal courts' power to grant prospective equitable relief against ongoing unconstitutional federal conduct, whether through traditional equity jurisdiction or under the Administrative Procedure Act's waiver of sovereign immunity for nonmonetary relief (5 U.S.C.\ \S~702). The suit may proceed to the merits.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (A Near-Replica of \textit{Bivens}): Narcotics Agents Search a Home.} Federal drug agents enter a New York apartment without a warrant, manacle the tenant in front of his family, threaten them, search the apartment, and strip-search the tenant at a federal facility. The tenant sues the agents for damages under the Fourth Amendment. The facts track \textit{Bivens} itself, which \textit{Egbert} declined to overrule, so a court could hold the claim arises in no new context. But \textit{Egbert} warned that ``parallel circumstances'' with \textit{Bivens} are not enough unless the claim also survives the Court's restrictive modern framework, and the agency's internal complaint process might count as an alternative remedial structure. Justice Gorsuch saw no principled way to distinguish such cases. Whether even a \textit{Bivens} replica survives is uncertain.
```

- Hypothetical 2 ('Opposite Side' Contrast): an exam tip rather than a fact pattern; its two points are turned into Examples 3 and 4.
- Hypothetical 1 'Analysis:' label.

## 229_united_states_steel_corp_v_multistate_tax_commission

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Applying the functional test of \textit{Virginia v. Tennessee}, the Court held 7--2 that the Multistate Tax Compact needed no congressional consent because it gave member states no power they lacked individually and did not enhance state power at the expense of federal supremacy (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/434/452/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the three-judge District Court by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued on October 11, 1977, and decided on February 21, 1978 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/434/452/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/434/452}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Powell delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justices Brennan, Stewart, Marshall, Rehnquist, and Stevens.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice White filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Blackmun.
\end{itemize}
```

### Holding

```latex
\textbf{Question Presented:}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Applied to Regional Banking Statutes:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Consent Needed): An Advisory Interstate AI Oversight Commission.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Consent Needed): Reciprocal Recognition of Teaching Licenses.} Twenty States enact identical statutes agreeing to recognize one another's teaching licenses and creating an advisory council of state education officials that recommends common certification standards. Each State keeps full control over whom it licenses, may ignore the council's recommendations, and may withdraw by repealing its statute. A teachers' union argues that the arrangement is an unconsented compact. Under \textit{U.S. Steel}, the relevant question is the agreement's potential impact on federal supremacy, and here there is none: every State could recognize out-of-state licenses on its own, the council exercises no sovereign power, and Congress remains free to legislate. The arrangement therefore requires no congressional consent, whether it is called a compact or reciprocal legislation.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Consent Required): A Binding Atlantic Maritime Security Compact.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Consent Required): Transferring a County Between States.} Two neighboring States agree that a populous county will be detached from the first State and annexed to the second, and each legislature approves the transfer. The second State would gain population, and with it potential seats in the House of Representatives and electoral votes. Under the functional test of \textit{Virginia v. Tennessee} that \textit{U.S. Steel} reaffirmed, an agreement requires consent when it tends to increase the political power of the States in a way that may encroach upon the just supremacy of the United States. Shifting a block of population and territory between States alters representation in the national government itself, so the agreement cannot take effect without Congress's consent.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unsettled): The National Popular Vote Interstate Compact.} States agree to award their presidential electors to the winner of the national popular vote, effective only once States holding a majority of electoral votes have joined. Supporters rely on \textit{U.S. Steel}: each State already has plenary power under Article II to choose how to appoint its electors, the agreement creates no commission with sovereign power, and members may withdraw. Critics answer with Justice White's point that the Clause must forbid some joint actions that individual States could take alone, and argue that a bloc controlling the outcome of presidential elections changes the national political structure and dilutes the influence of nonmember States. Because \textit{U.S. Steel} measured harm only against federal supremacy and the Court has never applied it to a compact affecting the selection of federal officers, the outcome is genuinely uncertain.
```

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Federal Law): An Advance-Consented Disaster Compact.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Federal Law): One State Tries to Rewrite a Bistate Bridge Commission's Duties.} Two States create a bistate bridge commission by a compact to which Congress has consented. One State later enacts a statute requiring the commission to follow that State's public-bidding rules, which the compact does not mention, and its courts enforce the statute against the commission. Under \textit{Cuyler}, congressional consent to a compact on a proper federal subject---here, interstate transportation and commerce---makes the compact federal law. Its meaning is therefore a federal question for the Supreme Court, not a matter settled by one State's courts, and a single State may not impose new obligations on the commission that the compact's terms do not authorize (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/449/433/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Not Federal Law): An Unconsented Occupational Licensing Compact.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Federal Habeas Relief): A Technical Violation of the 120-Day Trial Deadline.} A prisoner transferred under IAD Article IV is tried 130 days after arrival in the receiving State. He did not object to the trial date until after it had passed and shows no prejudice from the delay. After losing in the state courts, he seeks federal habeas corpus. \textit{Cuyler} makes the IAD federal law, but federal-law status does not mean that every violation supports collateral relief. In \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/512/339/}{\textit{Reed v.\ Farley}, 512 U.S.\ 339 (1994)}, the Court held that this kind of unobjected-to, harmless violation of the 120-day rule is not cognizable in federal habeas, so the petition fails.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unresolved): Is a Uniform Witness Act Federal Law?} Two States have each adopted the Uniform Act to Secure the Attendance of Witnesses, a reciprocal statute governing out-of-state subpoenas in criminal cases. A defendant argues that, like the IAD, the Uniform Act is a crime-control compact covered by Congress's 1934 advance consent, so its construction is a federal question. \textit{Cuyler}'s reasoning supports him: advance consent counted for the IAD, and cooperation in criminal law enforcement is a proper federal subject. But footnote 10 of \textit{Cuyler} expressly left this question open, and the Uniform Act may be ordinary reciprocal legislation rather than an agreement among States. Because the line between a compact and parallel state statutes is unsettled, the outcome is uncertain.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Case Metadata: full party caption, docket number, Briefing Book placement, Cornell Constitution Annotated link (Justia link kept in Judicial Votes).
- Separate opinions: 'Concurrences: None.' item (stated in Judicial Votes).
- Legacy section: opening paragraph (260 compacts, ABA Urban Lawyer citation, consent asymmetry framing); 'Through 2026' paragraph (State v. Torkelson, George Harms Constr., ABA 2025, Columbia HRLR scholarship) - lower-court applications and scholarship, not Supreme Court decisions applying or limiting Cuyler. Kept: compact-dispute reaffirmations and Carchman / Reed v. Farley.
- Exam Hypotheticals: subsection heading and hypothetical labels (both fact patterns and analyses reused in Examples 1 and 3).

## 231_united_states_v_belmont

### Memory Jogger

```latex
The Court unanimously reversed and let the United States collect a New York bank deposit assigned to it by the Soviet Union in the Litvinov Assignment; Justice Sutherland's majority held that a sole executive agreement made incident to recognition needs no Senate consent and overrides contrary state law and policy (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/301/324/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the Second Circuit, \textbf{9--0} in the judgment. The case was argued on March 4, 1937, and decided on May 3, 1937 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/301/324/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/301/324}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Sutherland delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Hughes and Justices Van Devanter, McReynolds, Butler, and Roberts.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Stone filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justices Brandeis and Cardozo.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Expansion in \textit{Pink}:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (State Law Preempted): Claims Settlement Incident to Recognition.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (State Policy Cannot Block Collection): An Assigned Foreign Bank Claim.} On recognizing a new government in Country Z, the President accepts, by an exchange of diplomatic notes, that government's assignment to the United States of claims owed to Z's nationalized central bank by American debtors, so that the United States can fund settlements of Americans' claims against Z. A company in State Q owes the central bank on a loan. When the United States sues to collect, State Q's courts refuse, citing the State's policy against giving effect to foreign confiscations. Under \textit{Belmont}, the assignment is a valid sole executive agreement incident to recognition, and ``the external powers of the United States are to be exercised without regard to state laws or policies''; the United States may collect (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/301/324/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Agreement Not Preemptive): Directing State Criminal Courts.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Statute Prevails): An Executive Agreement Contrary to a Federal Statute.} The President concludes a sole executive agreement with a trading partner promising to admit its steel free of the import duties that a federal statute requires, and directs customs officials to stop collecting the duties. An importer that competes with the foreign mills sues to compel collection. \textit{Belmont} held only that a valid sole executive agreement overrides \textit{state} law and policy; it did not suggest that the President, acting alone, may displace an Act of Congress. Because the agreement conflicts with a valid federal statute enacted under Congress's power over foreign commerce, the statute controls and the duties must be collected.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): Ending State Tort Suits Without a Compensation Fund.} The President, acting without Senate consent or new legislation, signs an agreement with a long-recognized foreign government that terminates all pending state-court suits by Americans against that government's state-owned chemical company over a factory disaster, in exchange for trade concessions but no compensation fund. The executive can invoke \textit{Belmont}, \textit{Pink}, and \textit{Dames \& Moore}: settling nationals' claims is a long-standing presidential practice, and state law must yield. But the agreement is not incident to recognition, provides no substitute remedy, and rests on whatever congressional acquiescence can be shown, which \textit{Medell\'in} said confines these precedents to a ``narrow set of circumstances.'' Whether it preempts the suits is uncertain.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Case Metadata: full caption, Chapter line, provisions line, duplicate source links (Justia and Cornell links kept in Judicial Votes).
- Concurring and Dissenting: lead-in sentence and the bold label before Justice Stone's concurrence (restated in Judicial Votes and the subsection heading).
- Legacy section: opening framing sentence; 'cabin-checked' lead-in; 'Modern Legacy Through 2026' item (Case-Zablocki, 2026 WHO-withdrawal commentary - not a decision applying Belmont). Kept: Pink, Dames & Moore, Garamendi, Medellin / Zivotofsky items.
- Exam Hypotheticals: headings and 'Analysis' labels.

**Notes:**

- Four Subsequent Developments items kept (Pink, Dames & Moore, Garamendi, Medellin) because each applied or narrowed Belmont's holding directly.

## 232_united_states_v_pink

### Memory Jogger

```latex
The Litvinov Assignment, an executive agreement the President made without the Senate when recognizing the Soviet Union, gave the United States title to a nationalized Russian insurer's New York assets; the Court held 5--2 that the agreement overrode New York's contrary law and policy just as a treaty would (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/315/203/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{5--2} vote. The case was argued on December 15, 1941, and decided on February 2, 1942 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/315/203/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/315/203}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Douglas delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Black, Frankfurter, Murphy, and Byrnes.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Frankfurter filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Chief Justice Stone filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Roberts.
    \item \textbf{Not Participating:} Justices Reed and Jackson did not participate.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Applied (\textit{Dames \& Moore}):}

\item \textbf{Applied (\textit{Garamendi}):}

\item \textbf{Narrowed (\textit{Medell\'in}):} In
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Agreement Preempts State Law): Nation Z's Airline Assets.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Agreement Preempts State Law): Ending State-Court Attachments After Recognition.} When the United States recognizes a new government in Nation R, the President agrees by exchange of letters that all claims of American nationals against Nation R will be resolved by a binding international claims commission and that pending attachments of Nation R's property in American courts will be released. A creditor who obtained a state-court attachment of Nation R's bank deposits in Illinois argues that the President cannot, without the Senate, override Illinois attachment law. Under \textit{Pink}, settling claims to remove obstacles to recognition is a modest implied power of the President, and the resulting agreement has the same supremacy over state law and policy that a treaty would. The Illinois court must release the attachment; the creditor's remedy lies before the claims commission.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Agreement Does Not Preempt): Nation Z's Statute-of-Limitations Waiver.} Return to the Nation Z agreement.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Agreement Likely Ineffective): Overriding a Federal Statute.} Congress enacts a statute imposing trade sanctions on companies owned by Nation W and directs the Treasury to enforce them. Seeking better relations, the President signs an executive agreement with Nation W promising that its companies will be exempt, and a company prosecuted for violating the sanctions invokes the agreement as a defense. \textit{Pink} does not help it. \textit{Pink} held that a sole executive agreement incident to recognition prevails over conflicting \textit{state} law because foreign affairs belong to the national government; it did not hold that the President may displace an Act of Congress. Here the President acts against Congress's expressed will, where executive power is at its lowest under Justice Jackson's framework in \textit{Youngstown Sheet \& Tube Co. v. Sawyer}, 343 U.S. 579 (1952), and \textit{Medell\'in} confined \textit{Pink} to a narrow set of circumstances. The statute likely controls.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Open Question): A Claims Settlement That Wipes Out American Creditors' Liens.} As part of recognizing a new government in Nation V, the President agrees to accept a lump-sum payment in full settlement of all American claims and to transfer to Nation V the frozen assets that American creditors had already attached in state courts. The creditors, all U.S. nationals, claim a taking of their property without just compensation. \textit{Pink} supports the agreement's power to displace state law, and its due process holding allowed the United States to prefer itself and its nationals over foreign creditors. But \textit{Pink} did not involve extinguishing American creditors' vested state-law liens, and in \textit{Dames \& Moore v. Regan}, 453 U.S. 654 (1981), the Court upheld a similar suspension of claims while declining to decide whether the creditors were entitled to compensation. Whether the agreement effects a compensable taking remains uncertain.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Case Metadata: parallel reporter citations, court and posture line, Briefing Book placement line, provisions list (Justia and Cornell links kept in Judicial Votes).
- Concurring and Dissenting: the bold labels 'Concurrence (Justice Frankfurter):' and 'Dissent (Chief Justice Stone, joined by Justice Roberts):' (replaced by subsection headings).
- Legacy section: first paragraph (high-water-mark characterization, Bricker Amendment); the opening framing sentence of the second paragraph; Zivotofsky v. Kerry sentence (recognition power generally, with its own brief) and the closing 'Thus, Pink remains binding law' sentence. Kept: Dames & Moore, Garamendi, Medellin.
- Pedagogical section: 'Opposite Side' Contrast with Medellin v. Texas - a comparison of two real cases rather than a hypothetical; Medellin is kept under Subsequent Developments. Exam Hypothetical: 'Analysis:' label; its two provisions are reused as Examples 1 and 3.

**Notes:**

- Examples 1 and 3 both draw on Neon's single Nation Z hypothetical (provision 1 and provision 2 respectively).

## 233_tennessee_wine_and_spirits_retailers_assn_v_thomas

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Tennessee required retail liquor-store license applicants to have lived in the State for two years; the Court held 7--2 that the rule violated the dormant Commerce Clause and was not saved by \S~2 of the Twenty-first Amendment, which does not shield protectionist alcohol laws lacking evidence-backed health or safety justifications (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/588/18-96/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Sixth Circuit by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued on January 16, 2019, and decided on June 26, 2019 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/588/18-96/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/18-96}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Alito delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Ginsburg, Breyer, Sotomayor, Kagan, and Kavanaugh.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Gorsuch filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Thomas.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Law Invalid): A Property-Ownership Requirement for Craft-Beer Licenses.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Law Invalid): An All-Resident Ownership Rule for Corporate Retailers.} A State allows a corporation to hold a retail liquor license only if every one of its officers, directors, and shareholders is a state resident, which bars any publicly traded chain from opening a store. The State says local ownership keeps retailers accountable to the community. Applying \textit{Tennessee Wine}, the rule discriminates against nonresidents on its face, and the State must show with concrete evidence that its predominant effect is protecting health or safety rather than protecting in-state owners from competition. Accountability can be secured through registered agents, background checks, inspections, and license revocation, as the Court explained in rejecting Tennessee's rationales, so the ownership rule falls outside \S~2's protection.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Law Likely Valid): Neutral Density Caps, Price Floors, and Storefront Rules.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Law Likely Valid): A State-Run Retail Monopoly.} A State repeals its private retail licensing system and provides that all packaged liquor will be sold only in stores owned and staffed by a state agency. A national retail chain that hoped to enter the market challenges the law. Unlike Tennessee's residency rule, the monopoly does not favor residents over nonresidents: it excludes every private seller, in-state or out-of-state, equally. \textit{Tennessee Wine} condemns laws that discriminate against nonresidents and whose predominant effect is protectionism. Because the monopoly does not discriminate, that inquiry is never triggered, and the State's choice falls within the leeway \S~2 gives each State in choosing alcohol-related public health and safety measures.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unresolved): Direct Shipping by Out-of-State Retailers.} A State lets its licensed wine retailers ship wine directly to consumers' homes but forbids out-of-state retailers to do so unless they open a licensed store in the State. An out-of-state retailer argues that the law discriminates against it just as Tennessee's residency rule did. The State answers that requiring an in-state premises is part of its three-tier system, which \textit{Granholm} called unquestionably legitimate, and that in-state stores can be inspected and audited. As the Subsequent Developments above show, lower courts have divided over whether courts may treat physical presence as an essential feature of the three-tier system or must demand concrete evidence under the predominant-effect test, and the Supreme Court has not resolved the question.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Case Metadata: docket number, lower-court citations, provisions list, slip-opinion link (kept in Key Quotations).
- Legacy section: first paragraph (settled the circuit split - restates the holding; National Pork Producers v. Ross - narrowed other dormant Commerce Clause branches, tangential); final paragraph on May 18, 2026 cert denials in Day v. Henry and Chicago Wine Co. v. Braun. Kept: the retailer-shipping split paragraph lead-in and the two circuit-method items (Day v. Henry; Block v. Canepa).
- Exam Hypothetical: 'Analysis:' label and headings (texts reused in Examples 1 and 3).

**Notes:**

- Kept Subsequent Developments items cite Day v. Henry (9th Cir. 2025, amended Sept. 2025) and a June 2026 blog post; verify in audit.
- Subsequent Developments is lower-court material only (no later Supreme Court decision applies Tennessee Wine); kept because the open retailer-shipping question is what students need to state current law.

## 234_balzac_v_porto_rico

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A Puerto Rican newspaper editor convicted of criminal libel without a jury argued that the 1917 grant of U.S. citizenship carried the Sixth Amendment with it; a unanimous Court held that Puerto Rico remained an unincorporated territory, where only fundamental rights apply of their own force and the jury right does not, because locality rather than citizenship controls (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/258/298/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{affirmed} the Supreme Court of Porto Rico, \textbf{9--0}. The case was argued on March 20, 1922, and decided on April 10, 1922 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/258/298/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Taft delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices McKenna, Day, Van Devanter, Pitney, McReynolds, Brandeis, and Clarke.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Holmes concurred in the result without a separate opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Right Applies): Citizenship Without Grand-Jury Indictment.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Locality Controls): A Mainland-Born Citizen Tried in Porto Rico.} In 1920 a citizen born and raised in Ohio moves to San Juan to run a business. Charged under the insular penal code with a misdemeanor, he demands a jury, pointing out that he was a citizen of the United States long before the Jones Act and would have had a jury in Ohio. Under \textit{Balzac}, his birthplace makes no difference: it is the locality, not the status of the person, that determines whether the Constitution's jury guarantees apply in matters of judicial procedure. Because Porto Rico is unincorporated and the jury right is not among the fundamental rights that apply there of their own force, he may be tried without a jury, as Taft expressly noted (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/258/298/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Rights Apply): An Incorporated Territory, or a Fundamental Right.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (\textit{Balzac} Not Extended): A Civilian Tried by Court-Martial Abroad.} During peacetime, the civilian wife of a U.S. soldier stationed overseas is charged with killing her husband on a military base and is tried by court-martial, without a jury or grand-jury indictment, under military law. The government invokes the \textit{Insular Cases} to argue that the jury guarantees do not follow citizens abroad. In \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/354/1/}{\textit{Reid v.\ Covert}, 354 U.S.\ 1 (1957)}, the Court rejected that argument on these facts and held that the civilian was entitled to the protections of Article~III and the Fifth and Sixth Amendments. The plurality declined to extend the \textit{Insular Cases}' territorial reasoning beyond its context of governing unincorporated territories.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Is \textit{Balzac} Still Good Law?): A Felony Bench Trial in an Unincorporated Territory Today.} A U.S. national living in an unincorporated Pacific territory is tried for a serious felony by a territorial court without a jury, as local law permits. She argues that the jury right is now recognized as fundamental. \textit{Balzac} squarely held that the jury right does not apply of its own force in unincorporated territories, and the Court has never overruled it. But \textit{Duncan v.\ Louisiana} later described the jury right as fundamental to the American scheme of justice, Justice Gorsuch in \textit{Vaello Madero} urged overruling the \textit{Insular Cases}, and the Court in \textit{Reid v.\ Covert} refused to extend them. A lower court would likely follow \textit{Balzac}, but the Supreme Court's answer is genuinely uncertain.
```

**Dropped from the original:**

- Case Metadata: Chapter line, lower-court citation, provisions line, Wikisource link.
- Legacy section: introductory paragraph; 'Status Through 2026' item (2024 Civil Rights Commission memorandum, Fitisemanu cert. denial). Kept: 'Piecemeal Rights Extension' item (Reid, Flores de Otero, Torres, Harris v. Rosario) and 'Aurelius and Vaello Madero' item.

**Notes:**

- Neon's 'Opposite Side' Contrast contains two genuine fact patterns (express incorporation; warrantless searches in an unincorporated territory) and is reused whole as Example 3.
- Key Quotations retains Neon's fourth quotation, which is from Justice Gorsuch's Vaello Madero concurrence rather than from Balzac itself.

```latex
The SSA sued a disabled U.S. citizen to recover SSI payments made after he moved to Puerto Rico; the Court held 8--1 that the Fifth Amendment does not require Congress to extend SSI to Puerto Rico, because the territory's general exemption from federal income taxes supplies a rational basis under the Territory Clause (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/596/20-303/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes
