```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the First Circuit by an \textbf{8--1} vote. The case was argued on November 9, 2021, and decided on April 21, 2022 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/596/20-303/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/20-303}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Kavanaugh delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Thomas, Breyer, Alito, Kagan, Gorsuch, and Barrett.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Thomas filed a concurring opinion. Justice Gorsuch filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Sotomayor filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Thomas

Justice Gorsuch

Justice Sotomayor
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Territorial Exclusion Upheld): A Federal Heating and Cooling Credit.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Territorial Asymmetry Upheld): A Cap on Federal Medicaid Funds for the Territories.} Congress funds Medicaid in the States through an open-ended federal match but gives Puerto Rico and the other territories a fixed annual block of federal money, so that once the cap is reached the territory must pay the full cost. Low-income residents of Puerto Rico argue that the disparity denies them equal protection. Under \textit{Vaello Madero}, Congress may weigh the general balance of benefits to and burdens on territorial residents, including their exemption from most federal income taxes, the cost of greater reimbursement, and the risk of disrupting the territory's economy. \textit{Harris v.\ Rosario} upheld lower AFDC reimbursements to Puerto Rico on similar reasoning, and the cap survives rational-basis review.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Exclusion Invalid): Excluding Residents of a State.} Now suppose Congress

. \textit{Vaello Madero}'s rationale does not carry over:

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Strict Scrutiny): An Ancestry-Based Exclusion in Puerto Rico.} Alternatively, suppose Congress

. Here

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Excluding Puerto Rico From a Payroll-Tax-Funded Benefit.} Congress creates a federal paid family-leave benefit financed entirely by a new payroll tax that is collected from workers and employers in Puerto Rico just as in the States, but it pays benefits only to residents of the States. A worker in Puerto Rico who paid the tax is denied benefits. \textit{Vaello Madero} rested on Puerto Rico's exemption from federal income taxes, a rationale that is missing when residents pay the very tax that funds the program, much as Justice Sotomayor stressed that Puerto Ricans pay payroll taxes. Yet rational-basis review accepts any conceivable justification and the majority looked to the general balance of benefits and burdens rather than any one tax, so a court could uphold the exclusion. The outcome is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 236_ex_parte_milligan

### Memory Jogger

```latex
An Indiana civilian sentenced to hang by a Civil War military commission won his release: the Court held unanimously that the commission lacked jurisdiction, and 5--4 that neither the President nor Congress may subject civilians to military trial where the civil courts are open, even while habeas corpus is suspended (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/71/2/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court answered the certified questions in Milligan's favor, \textbf{9--0} on the judgment that the military commission lacked jurisdiction and Milligan was entitled to discharge, and \textbf{5--4} on whether Congress could have authorized such a commission. The case was argued March 5--9 and 12--13, 1866, and decided April 3, 1866, with full opinions delivered in December 1866 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/71/2/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/71/2}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Davis delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Nelson, Grier, Clifford, and Field.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Chief Justice Chase filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justices Wayne, Swayne, and Miller.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Holding

```latex
\textbf{Question Presented:}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Unlawful Belligerents (\textit{Ex parte Quirin}):}

\item \textbf{Military Detention of Citizens (\textit{Hamdi v. Rumsfeld}):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Military Trial Invalid): Congress Authorizes Commissions for Domestic Saboteurs.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Military Trial Invalid): Presidential Martial Law After Riots.} After several nights of rioting in a major city, the President, acting without any statute, declares martial law in the city and orders that persons arrested for arson be tried by military tribunals. A college student who has no connection to any armed force is arrested for setting a police car on fire and convicted by a tribunal, although the federal and state courts in the city never closed. The conviction cannot stand. Under Justice Davis's majority, martial rule ``can never exist where the courts are open,'' and the student is a civilian entitled to indictment and jury trial. Even Chief Justice Chase's concurrence would not save the tribunal, because Chase located the power to authorize military commissions in Congress, not the President acting alone (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/71/2/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Military Jurisdiction Permitted): A Citizen Saboteur Enlisted With the Enemy.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Military Detention Permitted): A Citizen Captured on a Foreign Battlefield.}

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Open but Compromised Courts): A Militia Supporter in an Insurrection.} During an armed insurrection in one State, a militia seizes several county seats, and its members threaten jurors and witnesses in the federal courthouse, which stays open but cannot seat a jury in militia cases. Congress authorizes military commissions in that State for persons who knowingly supply the militia with weapons. A citizen who sold rifles to the militia, but never joined it, is tried by a commission. Under Davis's bright line, the courts are open, so the trial is invalid; but Chase warned that courts may be technically open yet ``wholly incompetent,'' and here Congress has acted. \textit{Quirin} also complicates matters, since the defendant's knowing support for an armed force may or may not make him a belligerent rather than a \textit{Milligan}-type civilian. The outcome is uncertain.
```

- Legacy section: intro paragraph; Hamdan v. Rumsfeld and Boumediene v. Bush sentences (they rest on statutory authorization and the Suspension Clause, not Milligan's holding); 'Through 2026' summary paragraph (CONAN, Vladeck). Kept: Quirin paragraph and Hamdi sentence.
- Exam hypothetical heading/'Analysis:' label and the 'Opposite Side' Contrast lead-in sentence; the contrast's two fact patterns are reused as Examples 3 and 4.

## 237_boumediene_v_bush

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Holding 5--4 that the Suspension Clause has full effect at Guantanamo Bay, the Court struck down \S~7 of the Military Commissions Act because the Detainee Treatment Act's review of CSRT determinations was not an adequate and effective substitute for habeas corpus (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/553/723/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} the D.C. Circuit by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on December 5, 2007, and decided on June 12, 2008 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/553/723/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/06-1195.ZO.html}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Kennedy delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Souter filed a concurring opinion, joined by Justices Ginsburg and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Chief Justice Roberts filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Scalia, Thomas, and Alito. Justice Scalia filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Thomas and Alito.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Suspension Clause Applies): A Leased Atoll Outside Any War Zone.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Inadequate Substitute): Judicial Review Without the Power to Order Release.} After \textit{Boumediene}, Congress again strips district courts of habeas jurisdiction over Guantanamo detainees and substitutes review in the D.C. Circuit. The new statute lets detainees have counsel, see the evidence against them, and present newly discovered exculpatory evidence, and it authorizes the court to declare a detention unlawful. But it provides that the court may only recommend release to the Secretary of Defense, who retains final authority. \textit{Boumediene} held that an adequate substitute for habeas must include, at a minimum, the power to order the conditional release of a person unlawfully detained. Because the reviewing court cannot order release, the substitute remains inadequate and the statute operates as an unconstitutional suspension of the writ.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Suspension Clause Does Not Apply): A Forward Operating Base in an Active Combat Zone.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Outside the Core of the Writ): Expedited Removal at the Border.}

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): A Detention Facility on an Allied Nation's Military Base.} Under a long-term status-of-forces agreement, the United States operates a detention facility on a military base in an allied country far from any active hostilities, holding noncitizens captured in a distant conflict; the host nation retains sovereignty and its law formally applies on the base, though U.S. forces run the facility. Congress bars habeas review and provides only military status hearings. Two \textit{Boumediene} factors favor the detainees: the process for determining their status is weak, and there is no war-zone friction. But the site factor points the other way, because unlike Guantanamo, the United States lacks complete and indefinite control, and a court order might create friction with the host government. How courts weigh that mix is uncertain under \textit{Boumediene}'s functional test.
```

## 238_dhs_v_thuraissigiam

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A Sri Lankan asylum seeker caught 25 yards inside the border could not use habeas to challenge his negative credible-fear finding: the Court held 7--2 that the Suspension Clause protects release from unlawful custody, not further review of a claim to stay, and that an alien at the threshold of entry has only the procedural rights Congress grants (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/591/19-161/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded with instructions to dismiss} by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued on March 2, 2020, and decided on June 25, 2020 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/591/19-161/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/19-161}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Alito delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Thomas, Gorsuch, and Kavanaugh.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Thomas filed a concurring opinion. Justice Breyer filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justice Ginsburg.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Sotomayor filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Kagan.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Thomas

Justice Breyer

Justice Sotomayor
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Habeas Review): A Border-Adjacent Credible-Fear Denial.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Habeas Review): An Asylum Seeker at a Port of Entry.} A noncitizen without valid documents presents herself at a land port of entry and asks for asylum. She is placed in expedited removal, and an asylum officer and an immigration judge find no credible fear. In a habeas petition she argues that her interview was conducted through an interpreter who spoke a different dialect, and she asks for a new interview. Like Thuraissigiam, she seeks another chance at admission rather than simple release, so the Suspension Clause does not require review. And because she has never entered the United States, she has only the procedural rights Congress has provided by statute; the Court stressed that a clandestine entrant should not receive more process than such a port-of-entry applicant (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/591/19-161/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Habeas Available): Core Release From Interior Detention.}

; and

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Habeas Available): A Claim of Citizenship.} A man apprehended near the border is placed in expedited removal and ordered removed after a negative credible-fear finding. In a habeas petition he asserts that he is a United States citizen, born in Texas, and offers a birth certificate. Section 1252(e)(2) itself permits habeas review of whether the petitioner is an alien and whether he is a lawful permanent resident, refugee, or asylee, so the district court has jurisdiction to decide his citizenship claim. Nothing in \textit{Thuraissigiam} forecloses that inquiry: a citizen wrongly subjected to expedited removal is not an alien seeking initial admission, and his challenge is to the Executive's power to hold and remove him at all.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unresolved): Expedited Removal Deep in the Interior.} DHS arrests a noncitizen in Chicago who entered without inspection and has lived there for eighteen months, places him in expedited removal under the statute's two-year presence limit, and denies his credible-fear claim. He files a habeas petition arguing that the asylum officer applied the wrong legal standard and that he is entitled to full due process. The Suspension Clause holding cuts against him because he seeks further review rather than release. But the due process holding rested on Thuraissigiam's status at the threshold of initial entry, and the Court did not say when presence inside the country matures into an entry carrying fuller constitutional protection (see \textit{Zadvydas v.\ Davis}, 533 U.S.\ 678 (2001)). Whether eighteen months of residence is enough remains open.
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Statute Controls): Revoking a Visa-Waiver Treaty.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Later Statute Controls): A Tax Statute Overriding a Treaty.} A self-executing tax treaty between the United States and Country Y exempts Y's residents from U.S. tax on certain interest income. Congress later enacts a statute taxing that income for all foreign recipients, without mentioning the treaty. A Y resident sues for a refund, arguing that the treaty guarantees the exemption. Under the later-in-time rule that \textit{Chae Chan Ping} applied, a treaty and a federal statute stand on equal footing as the supreme law of the land, so ``the last expression of the sovereign will must control.'' The statute governs in U.S. courts; whether the United States has breached its international obligations is a matter for the political branches and diplomacy, not the courts (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/130/581/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Plenary Power Does Not Shield the Statute): Imprisonment at Hard Labor Without a Jury.} Suppose Congress

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Deference to the Executive): A Proclamation Contrary to Statute.} Suppose

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Statute Excluding Immigrants of One Religion.} Congress enacts a statute barring the admission of any immigrant who belongs to a named religion. A U.S. citizen whose spouse is denied a visa under the statute sues, invoking the Establishment and Equal Protection principles. \textit{Chae Chan Ping} treated Congress's exclusion judgments, even explicitly racial ones, as ``conclusive upon the judiciary,'' and \textit{Mandel} and \textit{Trump v. Hawaii} apply highly deferential review to entry decisions. But \textit{Trump v. Hawaii} emphasized that the proclamation there was neutral on its face, leaving open whether a facially religious exclusion would receive the same deference. Whether the plenary power tolerates an express religious exclusion has never been decided.
```

## 240_trump_v_hawaii

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Upholding Proclamation No.~9645, President Trump's third entry-restriction order, the Court held 5--4 that \S~1182(f) gives the President broad power to suspend entry and that, under deferential rational-basis review, the facially neutral proclamation survived an Establishment Clause challenge; it also repudiated \textit{Korematsu} (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/585/17-965/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on April 25, 2018, and decided on June 26, 2018 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/585/17-965/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/17-965}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Roberts delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Kennedy, Thomas, Alito, and Gorsuch.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Kennedy and Justice Thomas each filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Breyer filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Kagan. Justice Sotomayor filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Ginsburg.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Kennedy

Justice Thomas

Justice Breyer

Justice Sotomayor

\item \textbf{Applied (\textit{Department of State v. Mu\~noz}):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Proclamation Upheld): Suspending Tourist Visas After a Vetting Review.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Proclamation Upheld): An Open-Ended Suspension to Pressure a Foreign Government.} The President finds that a foreign government's refusal to accept the return of its nationals ordered removed from the United States harms U.S. interests, and under \S~1182(f) suspends the entry of that country's nationals as immigrants until the government begins cooperating. Plaintiffs argue that \S~1182(f) authorizes only temporary emergency measures and that the suspension discriminates by nationality in immigrant-visa issuance, contrary to \S~1152(a)(1)(A). Both arguments failed in \textit{Trump v. Hawaii}. The Court read \S~1182(f) to leave to the President whether, whose, for how long, and on what conditions entry is suspended, noting past nationality-based suspensions without fixed end dates, and held that \S~1152 governs visa issuance, not the threshold question of admissibility. The suspension is lawful.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Order Invalid): A Domestic Policy Targeting Buddhist-Studies Programs.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Likely Invalid): An Entry Ban Defined by Religion on Its Face.} The President issues a proclamation under \S~1182(f) suspending the entry of all foreign nationals who adhere to a named religion, citing general terrorism concerns but no country-specific vetting review. U.S. citizens separated from relatives abroad sue. \textit{Trump v. Hawaii} does not save the proclamation, because the Court's analysis depended on what the policy did not do: Proclamation No.~9645 was neutral on its face, rested on a multi-agency review of foreign governments' practices, and excluded most of the world's Muslims. A proclamation that classifies by religion on its face announces the very ground the Establishment Clause forbids, so it cannot reasonably be understood to rest on a justification independent of unconstitutional grounds, even under rational-basis review.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): Waivers That Exist Only on Paper.} A facially neutral \S~1182(f) proclamation restricts entry from several Muslim-majority countries and promises case-by-case waivers. In litigation, plaintiffs produce evidence that consular officers were told not to grant waivers, that almost none were granted, and that the agency review the proclamation cites had recommended lifting restrictions on two of the countries. Under the majority's approach, a court asks only whether the policy can reasonably be understood to rest on a legitimate national-security justification, and a neutral text plus an agency process ordinarily suffices. But Justice Breyer's dissent argued that waivers operating as window dressing would show the policy's real basis was religious animus. How much evidence of pretext can overcome the deference \textit{Trump v. Hawaii} prescribes remains unsettled.
```

## 241_hayburns_case

### Memory Jogger

```latex
When Congress told federal circuit courts to decide veterans' pension claims subject to revision by the Secretary of War and Congress, five Justices on circuit refused, writing that Congress may not assign Article III courts non-judicial duties or make their decisions subject to executive or legislative revision; the Supreme Court itself never reached the merits (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/2/409/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court issued \textbf{no merits judgment}. It divided \textbf{3--3} and denied Attorney General Randolph's \textit{ex officio} mandamus motion, then held his renewed motion on Hayburn's behalf under advisement until Congress's Act of February 28, 1793, mooted it (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/2/409/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Opinion of the Court:} None; the report is a procedural entry by reporter Alexander Dallas, with a footnote reprinting the circuit-court letters to President Washington (2 U.S.\ at 410--414 n.*).
    \item \textbf{Views on the Merits:} Five of the six sitting Justices---Chief Justice Jay and Justices Cushing, Wilson, Blair, and Iredell---stated their constitutional objections in circuit-court letters: the New York Circuit (Jay, Cushing, and District Judge Duane), the Pennsylvania Circuit (Wilson, Blair, and District Judge Peters), and the North Carolina Circuit (Iredell and District Judge Sitgreaves).
    \item \textbf{Concurring and Dissenting Opinions:} None in the modern sense.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Unconstitutional): Executive Veto of Court Awards.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Unconstitutional): Advisory Rulings on Pending Bills.} To reduce litigation over new laws, Congress directs the D.C. Circuit to review any bill passed by one House, at the request of a committee chair, and to issue a ruling on whether it would be constitutional if enacted. No party is injured, no one is sued, and the ruling binds no one. The duty is not ``properly judicial'' or performed ``in a judicial manner,'' in the words of the New York circuit judges: it asks a court to advise the legislature rather than decide a case between adverse parties. Under the advisory-opinion bar that \textit{Hayburn's Case} founded and \textit{Muskrat v.\ United States} applied, Congress cannot impose this assignment on an Article III court.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Constitutional): Terminating Prospective Injunctions After a Change in Law.} Suppose Congress enacts

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Constitutional): Judges Serving Voluntarily on a Commission.} Suppose Congress enacts

. Under

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Judgments Payable Only if Congress Appropriates.} Congress allows veterans to sue in federal district court for wrongly denied disability benefits, but provides that any judgment may be paid only from funds Congress appropriates each year for that purpose. After a court enters a final judgment for a veteran, Congress declines to fund that award. On one view, the judgment itself remains final and binding, and Congress has merely exercised its power of the purse, which \textit{Hayburn's Case} did not address. On another, a judgment that Congress can decline to honor case by case is effectively ``liable to a reversion, or even suspension, by the Legislature itself,'' which the North Carolina judges said the Constitution forbids. Where the line falls is unsettled.
```

Jarkesy, 2026 litigation) - tangential, and its quoted phrase is not from Hayburn's Case. Kept: Plaut item; Muskrat/California v. Texas/Mistretta item.

## 242_flast_v_cohen

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Federal taxpayers challenged federal education grants that funded instruction and textbooks in religious schools; an 8--1 Court, narrowing \textit{Frothingham v.\ Mellon}, held that taxpayers have standing when they attack an exercise of Congress's taxing and spending power as exceeding a specific constitutional limit on that power---here, the Establishment Clause (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/392/83/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed} the three-judge District Court by an \textbf{8--1} vote. The case was argued on March 12, 1968, and decided on June 10, 1968 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/392/83/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Warren delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Black, Douglas, Brennan, Stewart, White, Fortas, and Marshall.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Douglas, Justice Stewart, and Justice Fortas each filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Harlan filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Reinterpreting Article III Standing and \textit{Frothingham}}

\subsubsection{The Double-Nexus Test}

Justice Douglas

Justice Stewart

Justice Fortas

Justice Harlan
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Standing Exists): Express Appropriation for Sanctuary Repairs.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Standing Exists): An As-Applied Challenge to a Grant Program Open to Religious Groups.} Congress enacts a spending statute authorizing the Secretary of Health and Human Services to award grants to public and private organizations, expressly including religious organizations, to provide counseling on adolescent health. Taxpayers allege that some religious grantees are using the federal money to teach religious doctrine and sue under the Establishment Clause. Although an agency selects the grantees, the challenge is still to the operation of a congressional spending program under Article I, \S\ 8, and it invokes the Establishment Clause, so both \textit{Flast} nexuses are satisfied. \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/487/589/}{\textit{Bowen v.\ Kendrick}, 487 U.S.\ 589 (1988)}, upheld taxpayer standing for exactly this kind of as-applied challenge (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/392/83/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Standing): Outside \textit{Flast}'s Window.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Standing): Donating a Surplus Federal Building to a Seminary.} Acting under a statute that lets agencies dispose of surplus federal property, the General Services Administration conveys a closed federal office building, without charge, to a religious seminary. A taxpayers' group sues, claiming the gift violates the Establishment Clause. The claim fails \textit{Flast}'s first nexus twice over: the conveyance is an executive decision rather than a congressional spending enactment, and the authority for it rests on the Property Clause of Article IV, not the Taxing and Spending Clause of Article I, \S\ 8. \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/454/464/}{\textit{Valley Forge Christian College}, 454 U.S.\ 464 (1982)}, denied taxpayer standing on materially identical facts.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Congressional Mandate or Executive Discretion?): A Recovery-Services Appropriation.} Congress appropriates \$20 million for grants to community organizations, including faith-based organizations, to provide addiction-recovery services; the statute does not require that any grantee be religious. The agency awards most of the money to churches whose programs incorporate worship, and taxpayers sue. If the grants are viewed as carrying out Congress's own spending program, \textit{Bowen} supports standing. If they are viewed as the agency's discretionary choices under a general appropriation, the plurality in \textit{Hein} would deny it. And with several Justices having urged that \textit{Flast} be overruled, the result is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 243_friends_of_the_earth_v_laidlaw

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Environmental groups sued a polluter under the Clean Water Act for civil penalties; the Court held 7--2 that members who curtailed use of a river had standing even without proof of environmental harm, that penalties deterring ongoing violations provide redress, and that voluntary cessation moots a case only if the defendant shows it is absolutely clear the violations cannot reasonably be expected to recur (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/528/167/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} the Fourth Circuit by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued on October 12, 1999, and decided on January 12, 2000 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/528/167/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/528/167}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Ginsburg delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Stevens filed a concurring opinion. Justice Kennedy filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Scalia filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Thomas.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Tension With \textit{TransUnion} on Concrete Injury:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Live Case; Not Moot): Post-Filing Compliance by a Chemical Plant.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Standing Exists): Birdwatchers Who Stay Away From a Marsh.} A paper mill upstream of a coastal marsh repeatedly exceeds the dioxin limits in its discharge permit. Members of a birding club submit affidavits that they used to visit the marsh monthly but have stopped because they fear the illegal discharges, and one says her lakeside cabin has become harder to rent. The mill shows that scientists have found no measurable harm to the marsh's wildlife. Under \textit{Laidlaw}, the relevant injury is to the plaintiffs, not the environment: a reasonable concern about ongoing unlawful pollution that leads members to give up specific, regular uses of an identified place is concrete injury in fact, unlike the vague some-day intentions in \textit{Lujan}.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Standing): Violations Ended Before Suit.} Suppose

. Standing is measured when the complaint is filed, and at that moment nothing remained to deter. Civil penalties payable to the Treasury for wholly past violations cannot redress the members' injury, which is why \textit{Steel Co.\ v.\ Citizens for a Better Environment} found no standing. The voluntary-cessation doctrine never comes into play, because it applies only to a case that was live when filed and is later claimed to be moot.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Standing): An Undisclosed Credit-File Error.} Alternatively, suppose

. The consumer has a statutory cause of action, but under \textit{TransUnion} a bare statutory violation is not enough: the inaccurate file, never sent to anyone, causes no concrete harm with a close historical analogue such as defamation. Unlike the \textit{Laidlaw} members, who changed how they used a real place because of ongoing pollution, the consumer has suffered nothing Article III recognizes, so the suit must be dismissed.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Closing the Plant but Keeping the Permit.} After a citizen suit is filed over ongoing permit violations, the defendant shuts down its facility, lays off its workers, and lists the property for sale, but keeps its discharge permit in force and says it has no present plans to reopen. That is essentially Laidlaw's own posture, which the Court remanded without deciding. The defendant argues that closure makes recurrence impossible; the plaintiffs answer that a retained permit leaves the defendant free to resume discharges at will, and \textit{Fikre} held that a bare assurance of no present intent does not carry the formidable burden. Whether the closure is truly permanent is a factual question that could go either way.
```

## 244_trump_v_casa

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Challengers to the 2025 birthright-citizenship executive order won nationwide injunctions in three district courts; without reaching the order's legality, the Court held 6--3 that universal injunctions likely exceed federal courts' equitable authority under the Judiciary Act of 1789, limiting relief to what is needed to give each plaintiff with standing complete relief (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/606/24a884/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
By a \textbf{6--3} vote, the Supreme Court \textbf{granted} the Government's applications for \textbf{partial stays} of the three universal preliminary injunctions. The applications were argued May 15, 2025, and decided June 27, 2025 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/606/24a884/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/24A884}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Barrett delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Thomas, Alito, Gorsuch, and Kavanaugh.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Thomas filed a concurring opinion, joined by Justice Gorsuch. Justice Alito filed a concurring opinion, joined by Justice Thomas. Justice Kavanaugh filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Sotomayor filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Kagan and Jackson. Justice Jackson filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Holding

```latex
\textbf{Question Presented:}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{The Judiciary Act of 1789 and Historical Equity}

\subsubsection{Rejection of the Bill-of-Peace Analogy and Rule 23}

\subsubsection{Complete Relief vs.\ Universal Relief and Stay Factors}

Justice Thomas

Justice Alito

Justice Kavanaugh

Justice Sotomayor

Justice Jackson
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Universal Scope Stayed): Two Plaintiffs Challenge a Wire-Transfer Surcharge.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Relief Limited to the Plaintiff): A Contractor Challenges a Speech Condition.} An executive order bars all federal contractors from making public statements about a pending trade negotiation. One software contractor sues, alleging a First Amendment violation, and the district court, finding the order likely unconstitutional, enjoins its enforcement against every federal contractor in the country. Under \textit{CASA}, the injunction is broader than necessary: an order barring the Government from enforcing the condition against the plaintiff fully redresses \emph{its} injury, and nothing about the relief is indivisible. The injunction must be narrowed to the plaintiff. Other contractors may file their own suits or seek certification of a Rule 23(b)(2) class (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/606/24a884/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Pathways \textit{CASA} Leaves Open): APA Vacatur and a Certified Class.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Indivisible Relief): Noise From a Federal Firing Range.} A homeowner sues the Defense Department, alleging that a new nighttime live-fire training schedule at a neighboring base creates a nuisance and violates federal law. The district court enjoins nighttime firing altogether. The injunction benefits every neighbor of the base, not just the plaintiff, but \textit{CASA} does not require it to be narrowed. Justice Barrett explained that a court may award relief that incidentally benefits nonparties when indivisible relief is needed to redress the plaintiff's own injury, and she used a neighbor's nuisance as her example. There is no way to stop the noise at one house while letting it continue next door, so the scope of the injunction is consistent with the complete-relief principle.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (State Plaintiffs): A Benefits Rule and Cross-Border Students.} A State sues to block an executive order that would bar federal student-aid eligibility for children of certain noncitizens. It argues that an injunction limited to its own residents would not give it complete relief, because students constantly move into the State from elsewhere, and its universities would have to verify each student's eligibility under a patchwork of rules. \textit{CASA} acknowledged that the inquiry for state plaintiffs is ``more complicated'' and remanded for the lower courts to decide whether narrower relief would suffice. Justice Thomas, by contrast, warned that complete relief is a ``ceiling'' and that nonparty-benefiting remedies must remain rare, and Justice Alito flagged lax state standing as a loophole. Whether the State can justify relief extending beyond its borders is genuinely uncertain.
```

Barbara merits ruling, Tincher v. Noem, Ninth and First Circuit state-plaintiff remand rulings, Louisiana v. FDA / City of Columbus APA-vacatur rulings). ## 245_medellin_v_texas

### Memory Jogger

```latex
The Court held 6--3 that neither the ICJ's \textit{Avena} judgment nor President Bush's memorandum directing state courts to give it effect was binding domestic law: a non-self-executing treaty obligation becomes enforceable in U.S. courts only through congressional implementing legislation (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/552/491/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Texas Court of Criminal Appeals by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued on October 10, 2007, and decided on March 25, 2008 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/552/491/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/06-984.ZS.html}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Roberts delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Alito.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Stevens filed an opinion concurring in the judgment.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Breyer filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Souter and Ginsburg.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Aftermath:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Not Domestic Law): A Presidential Directive Enforcing an International Tribunal's Ruling.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Not Domestic Law): A Human-Rights Treaty Ratified With a Non-Self-Executing Declaration.} The Senate consents to a human-rights treaty guaranteeing fair criminal procedures, but attaches a declaration that the treaty's substantive provisions are not self-executing, and Congress passes no implementing statute. A state prisoner petitions a state court to vacate his conviction, arguing that a state evidentiary rule violates the treaty and is preempted under the Supremacy Clause. Under \textit{Medell\'{i}n}, a treaty is domestic law only if Congress implements it or the treaty itself conveys an intention that it be self-executing and is ratified on those terms. Here the ratifying Senate expressly said the opposite. The treaty binds the United States internationally, but it gives the prisoner no rule of decision that state courts must apply.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Binding Domestic Law): Implementing Legislation or Explicit Self-Executing Text.} Return to the environmental-tribunal hypothetical, but change the legal backdrop.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Valid Executive Action): A Claims-Settlement Executive Agreement.} Alternatively,

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unresolved): A Damages Suit for Failure to Give Consular Notice.} Police arrest a foreign national and never tell him of his right under Article 36 of the Vienna Convention to have his consulate notified. After his release, he sues the officers for damages under 42 U.S.C.\ \S~1983, claiming they violated rights secured by federal law. Unlike Medell\'{i}n's claim, this suit does not depend on an ICJ judgment or a presidential memorandum; it rests on the Convention itself, which Justices Stevens and Breyer viewed as self-executing. Whether Article 36 also confers individual rights enforceable in a private damages action is a separate question: the Court in \textit{Sanchez-Llamas v.\ Oregon}, 548 U.S.\ 331 (2006), assumed without deciding that it does, and \textit{Medell\'{i}n} did not resolve the point. The outcome therefore remains uncertain.
```

Trump paragraph (tangential separation-of-powers commentary). Kept: the aftermath sentence (no implementing legislation; Medellin executed).

## 246_nlrb_v_noel_canning

### Memory Jogger

```latex
The Recess Appointments Clause reaches intra-session as well as inter-session recesses and vacancies that arose before the recess, but a break of fewer than 10 days is presumptively too short, and the Senate is in session when it says it is; so President Obama's January 2012 NLRB appointments, made during a three-day gap between pro forma sessions, were invalid (9--0 in judgment) (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/573/513/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{affirmed} the D.C. Circuit's judgment (9--0), though it divided 5--4 on the scope of the Recess Appointments Clause. The case was argued on January 13, 2014, and decided on June 26, 2014 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/573/513/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/12-1281}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Breyer delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Kennedy, Ginsburg, Sotomayor, and Kagan.
    \item \textbf{Concurring in the Judgment:} Justice Scalia filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Thomas and Alito.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Scalia
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Appointment Invalid): Pro Forma Sessions and a Short Holiday Break.}

During an August break,

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Agency Order Void): A Quorum That Depends on an Invalid Appointee.} During a five-day break in the middle of a session, with no pro forma sessions, the President recess-appoints a member to a three-member federal commission that needs two members for a quorum. The new member and one Senate-confirmed member then issue an order fining a shipping company. The company petitions for review, arguing that the commission lacked a quorum. Under \textit{Noel Canning}, a recess of fewer than ten days is presumptively too short, and nothing unusual justified the appointment. The appointee therefore never lawfully held office, the commission acted without a quorum, and---as with the NLRB order against Noel Canning---the order must be set aside (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/573/513/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Appointment Valid): An 18-Day Summer Recess.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Appointment Valid): The Break Between Sessions.} The Senate's first session ends on December 20, and the second session is scheduled to begin on January 5; the Senate schedules no pro forma sessions. On December 28, the President recess-appoints an ambassador to a post that has been vacant for eight months because the nomination stalled in committee. Justice Scalia's view that the vacancy must first arise during the recess would doom the appointment, but the majority's holdings sustain it: the Clause covers vacancies that already exist during the recess, and an inter-session recess of more than ten days is long enough to trigger the President's power. The appointee may serve until the end of the Senate's next session.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Catastrophe During a Nine-Day Recess.} During a nine-day intra-session recess, a terrorist attack kills the Secretary of Homeland Security and her deputy, and the Senate cannot promptly reconvene. The President recess-appoints a new Secretary. A nine-day break is presumptively too short under \textit{Noel Canning}, but the Court left room for very unusual circumstances such as a national catastrophe, while making clear that Senate political opposition does not qualify. A court would have to decide whether the emergency truly prevented the Senate from acting and whether temporary acting officials could have filled the gap. Because the Court has never applied the exception, the outcome is uncertain.
```

- Separate opinions: 'Dissents: None.' item (stated in Judicial Votes).
- Legacy section in full: FVRA / NLRB v. SW General (statutory vacancies case that distinguished Noel Canning, not an application of its holding); post-2024 recess-appointment commentary (Volokh Conspiracy, Center for Renewing America); methodological 'liquidation' paragraph (Zivotofsky, Mazars, CFPB, Seila Law, Trump v. Slaughter) - these cite Noel Canning's historical-practice method rather than applying or limiting its Recess Appointments holding, and Trump v. Slaughter (2026) could not be verified. No Subsequent Developments subsection.

## 247_loper_bright_enterprises_v_raimondo

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Overruling \textit{Chevron}, the Court held 6--3 (6--2 in \textit{Loper Bright}) in a challenge to a rule making herring fishermen pay for federal observers that the APA requires courts to exercise independent judgment on whether an agency acted within its statutory authority, rather than deferring to an agency's reading of an ambiguous statute (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/603/22-451/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{vacated and remanded} both judgments, by a \textbf{6--3} vote in \textit{Relentless} and a \textbf{6--2} vote in \textit{Loper Bright} (Justice Jackson recused). The cases were argued on January 17, 2024, and decided on June 28, 2024 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/603/22-451/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/22-451}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Roberts delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Thomas, Alito, Gorsuch, Kavanaugh, and Barrett.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Thomas filed a concurring opinion. Justice Gorsuch filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Kagan filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Sotomayor, and by Justice Jackson as to \textit{Relentless}.
\end{itemize}
```

### Holding

```latex
\textbf{Question Presented:}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Thomas

Justice Gorsuch

Justice Kagan
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Deference): Defining a Statutory Term.} Suppose

EPA

. Whether a chemical in use for seventy years is ``emerging'' is a question about the scope of EPA's statutory authority, not a choice within it. Under \textit{Loper Bright}, the court reviews the definition

, and if the best reading of ``emerging'' excludes legacy chemicals, EPA's definition falls even though the term is ambiguous.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Deference): Making Regulated Parties Pay for Inspectors.} A statute authorizes an agency to require inspectors aboard cargo barges and expressly allows the agency to charge barge operators for inspectors in two specified programs, but says nothing about charging operators in other programs. The agency issues a rule requiring all operators to pay for inspectors. Before \textit{Loper Bright}, a court might have found the silence ambiguous and upheld the rule as a permissible reading. Now the court must decide for itself the ``single, best meaning'' of the statute, and Congress's express authorization of fees in only two programs strongly suggests that the agency has no such power elsewhere. The agency's view receives at most \textit{Skidmore} respect (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/603/22-451/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Deference Survives): A Choice Within an Express Delegation.} Under the same emerging-compounds statute, EPA

. Here

. \textit{Loper Bright} expressly preserved respect for statutes that delegate discretionary authority to an agency; the court polices the boundary of the delegation but does not substitute its own judgment about the right number.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Prior Holding Stands): Relitigating a Rule Upheld Under \textit{Chevron}.} In 2010 the Supreme Court upheld a specific agency rule, applying \textit{Chevron} and deferring to the agency's reading of an ambiguous statute. After \textit{Loper Bright}, a company subject to the same rule sues, arguing that the rule must now be vacated because the precedent upholding it rested on the overruled deference framework. The challenge fails. \textit{Loper Bright} stated that it does not call into question prior cases that relied on \textit{Chevron}: their holdings that specific agency actions are lawful remain subject to statutory \textit{stare decisis}, and mere reliance on \textit{Chevron} is not a ``special justification'' for overruling them.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): An Agency Reverses Course.}

The 2012 decision held only that the old reading was permissible, not that it was the best reading, so it may not settle whether the opposite reading is lawful. If the court concludes the old reading was best, the reversal fails; if the new reading is best, the prior precedent may not stand in its way. \textit{Loper Bright} did not say how such a precedent binds a later, contrary interpretation.
```

## 248_united_states_v_ec_knight_co

### Memory Jogger

```latex
The Sugar Trust's purchase of four Philadelphia refineries gave it control of about 98\% of American sugar refining, yet the Court held 8--1 that the Sherman Act could not reach it because manufacturing is not commerce and affects interstate commerce only indirectly; Justice Harlan dissented alone, and the manufacture/commerce line was later repudiated (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/156/1/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the dismissal of the government's bill by an \textbf{8--1} vote. The case was argued on October 24, 1894, and decided on January 21, 1895 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/156/1/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/156/1}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Fuller delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Field, Gray, Brewer, Brown, Shiras, Jackson, and White.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Harlan dissented alone.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Beyond the Commerce Power): Local Possession of a Homemade Tool.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Beyond the Commerce Power): Compelling Purchases of Domestic Products.} To revive domestic manufacturing, Congress requires every adult who does not already own one to buy an American-made electric bicycle by a set date, reasoning that the combined decisions of millions of nonbuyers substantially affect the interstate market. \textit{E.C. Knight}'s manufacture/commerce line is long gone, so the challenge cannot rest on the fact that the bicycles are manufactured. But the Court's modern effort to define enforceable outer limits supplies one: in \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/567/519/}{\textit{NFIB v. Sebelius}, 567 U.S. 519 (2012)}, a majority of Justices concluded that the Commerce Clause lets Congress regulate existing commercial activity but not compel individuals to enter commerce. The mandate would likely exceed the commerce power, though Congress might pursue the same goal through its taxing or spending powers.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Federal Power Reaches It): A Chip-Fabrication Monopoly.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Federal Power Reaches It): Price-Fixing at a Local Grain Elevator.} Grain-elevator operators in a single Kansas county agree on the prices they will pay farmers for wheat, nearly all of which the elevators then ship by rail to mills in other States. The operators argue that their agreement concerns only local purchases completed before any interstate shipment, the same kind of point that defeated the government in \textit{E.C. Knight}. The argument fails. Even before the New Deal, \href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/196/375/}{\textit{Swift \& Co. v. United States}, 196 U.S. 375 (1905)}, upheld a Sherman Act injunction against meatpackers' price-fixing at local stockyards because the livestock moved in a current of interstate commerce. Under modern doctrine the case is easier still: price-fixing is economic activity whose effect on the interstate wheat market is substantial, so Congress may reach it.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Homemade Firearms Within a National Market Scheme.} Congress enacts a comprehensive statute regulating the interstate market in firearm parts and kits, and, to keep that market closed to untraceable weapons, also bans the possession of any homemade firearm lacking a serial number, even one built entirely from materials found within a single State and never sold. A hobbyist charged with possessing such a gun argues that, as in \textit{Lopez}, mere possession is noneconomic. The government relies on \textit{Gonzales v. Raich}, 545 U.S. 1 (2005), which upheld a ban on home-grown marijuana as an essential part of a comprehensive scheme regulating an interstate market. Because the possession is noneconomic yet the statute is part of a broad market regulation, the case falls between \textit{Lopez} and \textit{Raich}, and the outcome is uncertain.
```

## 249_united_states_v_comstock

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Federal prisoners nearing release challenged a statute allowing their indefinite civil commitment as sexually dangerous; the Court held 7--2 that the Necessary and Proper Clause authorized it as a rational, narrow incident of Congress's power to run federal prisons and act as a responsible custodian (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/560/126/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued on January 12, 2010, and decided on May 17, 2010 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/560/126/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/08-1224}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Breyer delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Stevens, Ginsburg, and Sotomayor.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Kennedy filed an opinion concurring in the judgment. Justice Alito filed an opinion concurring in the judgment.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Thomas filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justice Scalia in all but Part III--A--1--b.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{\textit{Comstock} Distinguished (\textit{NFIB v.\ Sebelius}):}

\item \textbf{\textit{Comstock} Applied (\textit{United States v.\ Kebodeaux}):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Within Congress's Power): Commitment of Violently Dangerous Federal Prisoners.} Congress amends federal law to allow civil commitment, at the end of a federal sentence, of any prisoner in Bureau of Prisons custody who suffers from a serious mental illness and, as a result, would pose a serious danger of violent conduct if released. As in \S~4248, the government must prove dangerousness by clear and convincing evidence, and the Attorney General must transfer custody to any willing State. Every \textit{Comstock} consideration points toward validity: the statute rationally serves the federal power to imprison and care for federal prisoners, extends a long tradition of federal commitment of dangerous mentally ill prisoners, reflects the government's role as a responsible custodian, accommodates state interests, and is confined to people already in federal custody.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Within Congress's Power): Holding a Contagious Prisoner Past the Release Date.} A federal inmate due for release next week develops a highly contagious, dangerous infectious disease. A federal statute allows the Bureau of Prisons, after a hearing, to keep such an inmate in medical isolation beyond the end of his sentence until he is no longer contagious, unless the health authorities of his home State agree to take custody. Under \textit{Comstock}, Congress may act as a responsible custodian of those it lawfully holds, protecting the communities into which a prisoner would be released. The measure is narrow, limited to people already in federal custody, temporary, and deferential to state health authorities, so it is a necessary and proper incident of running federal prisons rather than a general police power.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Beyond Congress's Power): Commitment of State Felons via a Firearm Nexus.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Beyond Congress's Power): Commitment of People Never Charged.} Congress authorizes the Attorney General to seek federal civil commitment of any person whom the FBI investigated for a federal sex offense but decided not to charge, if a court finds the person sexually dangerous. The people covered were never convicted, never held by the Bureau of Prisons, and are not subject to any continuing federal obligation. \textit{Comstock} stressed that \S~4248 reached only a small class already in federal custody, which kept its link to enumerated powers from becoming too attenuated. Without that custodial link, the statute does not carry any enumerated power into execution; civil commitment of dangerous persons at large is the kind of general police power the Court said remains with the States.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): New Registration Duties for Long-Released Federal Offenders.} Congress requires every person ever convicted of a federal sex offense to register with federal authorities, including people who completed their sentences decades ago and were released without conditions, before any federal registration law existed. In \textit{Kebodeaux}, the Court upheld applying registration rules to a former serviceman because he remained subject to a preexisting federal registration obligation when released. Here, no such continuing obligation exists, so the link to the power that supported the original conviction is weaker. On the other hand, the statute addresses the consequences of federal convictions, is less intrusive than commitment, and builds on a federal registration tradition. Whether the link is close enough is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 250_us_term_limits_v_thornton

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Arkansas barred three-term Representatives and two-term Senators from its general-election ballot; a 5--4 Court held that the Constitution's qualifications for Congress are fixed and exclusive, so neither Congress nor a State may add term limits, whether directly or disguised as ballot-access rules---only an Article V amendment can (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/779/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Supreme Court of Arkansas by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on November 29, 1994, and decided on May 22, 1995 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/779/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/514/779}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Stevens delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Kennedy filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Thomas filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices O'Connor and Scalia.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Reaffirming \textit{Powell} and Rejecting Reserved State Power}

\subsubsection{Ballot-Access Disguise and the Elections Clause}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Unconstitutional): District Residency and a Tax-Return Ballot Label.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Unconstitutional): Congress Limits Its Own Members' Terms by Statute.} Responding to public pressure, Congress passes a statute providing that no person who has served six terms in the House or two terms in the Senate is eligible for election to that chamber again. A long-serving Representative sues to stay on the ballot. \textit{Thornton} reaffirmed \textit{Powell v.\ McCormack}'s holding that the Qualifications Clauses are exclusive and that Congress itself cannot add to them, and Stevens's opinion explained that any change to those qualifications must come through Article V. A statutory term limit is an added qualification for congressional service, so it is unconstitutional no matter which body enacts it (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/779/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Constitutional): State Term Limits, Resign-to-Run, and Neutral Ballot Rules.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Constitutional): A State Applies for an Article V Convention.} Frustrated by \textit{Thornton}, a state legislature adopts a resolution applying to Congress, under Article V, to call a convention for proposing a constitutional amendment imposing congressional term limits, and urges its congressional delegation to support such an amendment. Opponents argue that the State is again trying to impose term limits it may not adopt. Unlike Arkansas's Amendment 73, the resolution adds no qualification, keeps no candidate off the ballot, and does not handicap any class of candidates. It pursues precisely the route \textit{Thornton} identified as the only legitimate path to congressional term limits: amendment of the Constitution under Article V, in which the Constitution gives state legislatures an express role.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Neutral Information or a Handicap?): A Ballot Notation of Terms Served.} A State requires its ballots to list, beside each congressional incumbent's name, the number of terms the incumbent has served (for example, noting that a Representative is completing a sixth term). The notation is accurate and applies to every incumbent, and the State says it simply informs voters. But the measure was adopted by an initiative whose sponsors openly sought to promote congressional term limits, and the label may be designed to disadvantage long-serving incumbents. Under \textit{Thornton} and \textit{Cook v.\ Gralike}, a ballot rule is invalid if it is meant to handicap a class of candidates and dictate electoral outcomes, but neutral, factual ballot information is a familiar Elections Clause regulation. Whether this notation is the former or the latter is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 251_trump_v_anderson

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Colorado's Supreme Court removed Donald Trump from the 2024 presidential primary ballot under Section~3 of the Fourteenth Amendment; the Court unanimously reversed, holding that States may disqualify state officers and candidates but have no power to enforce Section~3 against federal offices, which a five-Justice majority assigned to Congress (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/601/23-719/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{reversed} the Colorado Supreme Court, \textbf{9--0} in the judgment, dividing \textbf{5--4} on whether congressional legislation under Section~5 is the exclusive federal mechanism for enforcing Section~3. The case was argued on February 8, 2024, and decided on March 4, 2024 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/601/23-719/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/23-719}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Per Curiam Opinion:} Joined in full by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Thomas, Alito, Gorsuch, and Kavanaugh.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Barrett filed an opinion concurring in part (joining Parts I and II--B) and concurring in the judgment. Justices Sotomayor, Kagan, and Jackson filed a joint opinion concurring in the judgment.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Barrett

Justices Sotomayor, Kagan, and Jackson
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (State Lacks Power): Excluding a U.S.\ Senate Candidate.}

Consider first the Senate candidate.

\item \textbf{Same-Side (State Lacks Power): A Voter Challenge to a Sitting Representative.} Voters in a congressional district petition a state court under the state election code to remove their incumbent U.S.\ Representative from the general-election ballot, presenting evidence that he helped plan an armed attack on a federal building. The state court finds that he engaged in insurrection and orders him off the ballot. Under \textit{Anderson}, the finding does not matter: a seat in the House is a federal office, and States have no power to enforce Section~3 with respect to federal offices. Any disqualification must come through legislation enacted by Congress or the House's own power to judge its Members' qualifications, so the order must be reversed.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (State Power Preserved): Barring a Former Sheriff From the Governor's Race.} In the same hypothetical, the State also acts against the former sheriff.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Likely Permissible): Excluding an Underage Presidential Candidate.} A 29-year-old files to appear on a State's presidential primary ballot, and the Secretary of State refuses because Article~II requires the President to be at least 35. The candidate invokes \textit{Anderson}'s holding that States may not enforce Section~3 against presidential candidates. \textit{Anderson} addressed only Section~3, a disability that requires fact-intensive findings and that Congress may remove by a two-thirds vote; an age requirement is objective, categorical, and cannot be waived by Congress, so state enforcement creates no risk of a conflicting state-by-state patchwork. Lower courts have allowed States to keep constitutionally ineligible candidates off the ballot, as in \textit{Hassan v.\ Colorado} (a naturalized citizen), so the exclusion is likely valid.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Enforcing the Two-Term Limit.} A former President who has already been elected twice files to run again, and a State keeps him off its presidential ballot under the Twenty-Second Amendment. Like the age requirement, the two-term limit is objective and turns on public records rather than disputed findings about conduct, and Congress has no power to waive it, which suggests that \textit{Anderson}'s concerns do not apply. On the other hand, \textit{Anderson} stressed the uniquely important national interest in presidential elections and the chaos of state-by-state ballot decisions, reasoning that could be read to bar any single State from deciding a presidential candidate's eligibility. Because the Court has never addressed the question, the outcome is uncertain.
```

- Entire Legacy section (state-office enforcement example, Maine/Illinois aftermath, Trump v. United States and 2024 election backdrop, absence of new Section 5 legislation, Baude/Paulsen and Somin scholarship, pending certiorari petition): no later decision overruling, narrowing, extending, or applying the holding, so no Subsequent Developments subsection.
- 'Opposite Side' Contrast (Chiafalo v. Washington and Hassan v. Colorado) - an exam tip comparing real cases; its themes inform new Examples 4 and 5.

## 252_deshaney_v_winnebago_county

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A Wisconsin county social-services agency repeatedly documented signs that four-year-old Joshua DeShaney was being abused by his father but never removed him; the Court held 6--3 that the Due Process Clause imposes no duty to protect against private violence unless the State has taken the person into custody or created the danger (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/489/189/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Seventh Circuit by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued November 2, 1988, and decided February 22, 1989 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/489/189/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/489/189}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Rehnquist delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices White, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, and Kennedy.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Brennan filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Marshall and Blackmun. Justice Blackmun filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Holding

```latex
\textbf{Question Presented:}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\subsubsection{Text, History, and the Negative-Liberties Baseline}

\subsubsection{Narrowing the Custodial ``Special Relationship'' Exception}

\subsubsection{State Tort Law and the Social-Worker Dilemma}

\item \textbf{Reinforced and Extended (\textit{Collins}; \textit{Castle Rock}):}

\item \textbf{State-Created Danger in the Circuits:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (State Prevails): Slow Response to Protective-Order Violations.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (State Prevails): Broken Promises of Extra Patrols.} A woman tells her local police department that a former boyfriend has threatened to kill her. An officer promises that patrol cars will drive past her house every hour that week, but none do, and on the third night the former boyfriend breaks in and injures her. She sues the officer and the city under \S~1983, arguing that the promise created a special relationship. Under \textit{DeShaney}, the claim fails. The State did not restrain her liberty, and the danger came from a private actor. The Court made clear that an affirmative duty arises not from the State's knowledge of a person's predicament or its ``expressions of intent to help him,'' but only from limits it has imposed on the person's freedom to act on her own behalf. Any remedy lies in state tort law (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/489/189/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Plaintiff Prevails): A Child Stranded on the Highway.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Custody Triggers a Duty): A Prisoner Beaten by a Cellmate.} A state prisoner tells prison officers that his cellmate has threatened to kill him and asks to be moved. The officers laugh off the request, and that night the cellmate beats him severely. Unlike Joshua, the prisoner is in state custody: the State has locked him in a cell with his attacker and taken away his ability to protect himself or seek help. \textit{DeShaney} itself explained that when the State so restrains a person's liberty, as in \textit{Estelle v.\ Gamble} and \textit{Youngberg v.\ Romeo}, it assumes a duty to provide for his basic needs, including reasonable safety. The prisoner can state a constitutional claim if he shows the officers knew of and disregarded a substantial risk to his safety, the Eighth Amendment standard the Court applied to prisoners attacked by other inmates in \textit{Farmer v.\ Brennan}, 511 U.S.\ 825 (1994).

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Foster Care): Abuse in a State-Selected Foster Home.} A county child-welfare agency removes a six-year-old from her parents and places her with foster parents it has licensed and supervises. Caseworkers receive two reports that the foster father is hitting her but do not investigate, and she is seriously injured. The agency relies on \textit{DeShaney}: the abuser was a private person, not a state employee. The child answers that the State took her from her family and chose where she would live, a restraint on her liberty much closer to institutionalization than Joshua's situation. \textit{DeShaney} expressly left open whether a child in state-arranged foster care stands in a custodial relationship with the State (489 U.S.\ at 201 n.9), and the Supreme Court has not resolved the question, so the outcome is uncertain.
```

## 253_penn_central_transportation_co_v_nyc

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Upholding New York City's refusal to allow an office tower above Grand Central Terminal, the Court held 6--3 that no taking occurred and set the default test for regulatory takings: an ad hoc weighing of economic impact, interference with investment-backed expectations, and the character of the government action, measured against the parcel as a whole (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/438/104/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the New York Court of Appeals by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued on April 17, 1978, and decided on June 26, 1978 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/438/104/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Brennan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Stewart, White, Marshall, Blackmun, and Powell.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Rehnquist filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justice Stevens.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Taking): Denial of a Tower Atop a Landmark Warehouse.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (No Taking): A Wetlands Buffer Covering Half a Lot.} An owner holds a two-acre residential lot with a house on the front acre. A county wetlands ordinance, part of a countywide program protecting watersheds, forbids any construction on the rear acre, which borders a protected marsh. The owner claims that the county has taken the rear acre outright because it can no longer be built on. Under \textit{Penn Central}, the owner cannot sever the rear acre and treat it as a separate parcel that has lost all value; the court measures the burden against the parcel as a whole. Viewed that way, the owner keeps the house and the full residential use of the property, the regulation is part of a general program adjusting benefits and burdens, and the economic impact, though real, is partial. No taking occurred.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Per Se Taking): A Public Easement and an Unconnected Permit Fee.} Modify the Harborview example:

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Per Se Taking): A Total Ban on Building on Beachfront Lots.} An owner buys two vacant beachfront lots zoned for single-family homes. Two years later, the State enacts a coastal setback law that forbids any permanent habitable structure on either lot, leaving them with no economically beneficial use. The State argues that \textit{Penn Central} balancing applies and that protecting the shoreline is a weighty public purpose. Under \textit{Lucas v.\ South Carolina Coastal Council}, 505 U.S.\ 1003 (1992), however, a regulation that deprives land of all economically beneficial use is a categorical taking, and \textit{Penn Central}'s ad hoc factors do not apply. The State can avoid paying compensation only by showing that background principles of its property or nuisance law already prohibited building homes on the lots.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Individual Landmarking of a Small Building Without TDRs.} A developer buys a modest commercial building planning to replace it with a mid-rise apartment complex permitted by existing zoning. Shortly after the purchase, the city designates the building alone as a landmark and bars demolition. The building can still be leased, but its value falls by about 75 percent, and the city's program offers no transferable development rights at the site. The owner has strong arguments on economic impact and on frustrated investment-backed expectations, and Justice Rehnquist's concern about singling out a few owners resonates. The city answers that the building retains an economically viable use, that the designation belongs to a comprehensive preservation program like the one upheld in \textit{Penn Central}, and that diminution in value alone does not establish a taking. With the factors pulling in opposite directions and no fixed weights, the outcome is unpredictable.
```

