## 254_dolan_v_city_of_tigard

### Memory Jogger

```latex
When Tigard conditioned Florence Dolan's store-expansion permit on dedicating a public floodplain greenway and a bike path, the Court held 5--4 that, beyond \textit{Nollan}'s essential nexus, the city must make an individualized determination of rough proportionality between the dedication and the development's impact, and it had not (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/512/374/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on March 23, 1994, and decided on June 24, 1994 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/512/374/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Rehnquist delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Stevens filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Blackmun and Ginsburg. Justice Souter filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Stevens

Justice Souter
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Conditions Invalid): A Boardwalk Easement and an Arts Fee for a Warehouse.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Fee Invalid): A Traffic Fee Far Exceeding the Project's Impact.} A county requires every applicant for a permit to build a backyard cottage to pay a \$60{,}000 traffic-mitigation fee, although the county's own engineering study estimates that a cottage adds about \$3{,}000 worth of traffic burden to county roads. An owner pays under protest and sues. After \textit{Koontz}, a demand for money is an exaction subject to \textit{Nollan} and \textit{Dolan}, and after \textit{Sheetz}, the fee is not exempt because the legislature set it. Traffic mitigation has an essential nexus to new housing, but a fee twenty times the projected impact is not roughly proportional in extent, and the county cannot carry its burden of justifying it (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/512/374/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Conditions Valid): Denial, or a Private Buffer and a Calibrated Fee.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Dedication Valid): A Turn Lane Supported by a Traffic Study.} A developer seeks approval for a 300-home subdivision whose only entrance will be on a two-lane county road. A traffic study commissioned by the county shows that the subdivision will generate most of the peak-hour traffic at the entrance, and the county conditions approval on dedicating a 12-foot strip along the frontage for a turn lane. The condition serves the legitimate goal of traffic safety (\textit{Nollan}'s nexus), and the county has made the individualized, quantified determination \textit{Dolan} requires: the strip is related in nature and extent to the traffic the project will create. No precise calculation is required, so the dedication stands.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unresolved): A Citywide School-Impact Fee Schedule.} A city adopts an ordinance charging every new home a school-impact fee of \$4 per square foot, based on a study of the average number of students generated by new housing citywide. A builder of a retirement community restricted to residents aged 55 and older argues that its homes will add almost no students. After \textit{Sheetz}, the legislative fee is subject to \textit{Nollan/Dolan}, but the Court left open whether a fee schedule based on averages for a class of developments must be tailored as specifically as a parcel-by-parcel exaction. The city will rely on its study and the impracticality of individualized fees; the builder will invoke \textit{Dolan}'s demand for an individualized determination. The outcome is uncertain.
```

## 255_kelo_v_city_of_new_london

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Upholding 5--4 New London's condemnation of unblighted homes for a comprehensive economic-development plan, the Court held that promoting economic development is a ``public purpose'' satisfying the Takings Clause's public-use requirement under deferential review, while leaving States free to impose stricter limits (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/545/469/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the Connecticut Supreme Court by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on February 22, 2005, and decided on June 23, 2005 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/545/469/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Stevens delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Kennedy filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice O'Connor filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices Scalia and Thomas. Justice Thomas filed a separate dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Holding

```latex
\textbf{Question Presented:}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Taking Valid): A Regional Industrial-Park Plan.} After its largest factory closes, a county adopts, following two years of studies and public hearings, a comprehensive plan to redevelop 200 acres into an industrial park. Developers and tenants will be chosen later through competitive bidding. Most owners sell, but several owners of well-kept, unblighted farmhouses refuse, and the county condemns their land. Under \textit{Kelo}, job creation and an expanded tax base are a legitimate public purpose, the plan is judged as a whole rather than lot by lot, and no particular private beneficiary was identified in advance. Courts will not second-guess whether the projected benefits will materialize, so the takings satisfy the Public Use Clause (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/545/469/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Taking Valid): Land for a Common-Carrier Rail Line.} A State authorizes a privately owned railroad, regulated as a common carrier that must serve all shippers on equal terms, to condemn a strip of farmland for a new freight line. The landowner objects that the property is being transferred to a private company. \textit{Kelo} confirms this is an easy case: even before reaching the broader ``public purpose'' test, the Court treated transfers to a private common carrier whose services are open to the public as a settled example of public use. With just compensation paid, the taking is valid.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Taking Likely Invalid): A One-Parcel Transfer to a Disappointed Buyer.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Taking Barred by State Law): A State Constitutional Amendment.} A city adopts a plan just like New London's and condemns unblighted homes for private office and retail development. The State's constitution, however, has been amended to forbid takings for transfer to private entities for the primary purpose of economic development or increased tax revenue. The owners sue in state court under the state constitution. The taking would satisfy the federal Public Use Clause under \textit{Kelo}, but \textit{Kelo} sets only a floor: the Court stressed that ``nothing in our opinion precludes any State from placing further restrictions on its exercise of the takings power.'' The state-law bar controls, and the condemnation fails.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Uncertain): A Downtown Arena for a Named Team.} A city negotiates with a professional sports team that wants a new arena, then adopts a 20-acre downtown redevelopment plan, after public hearings, that includes the arena, a public plaza, and housing, and condemns several unblighted businesses on the site. The city can invoke \textit{Kelo}: a formal plan, public deliberation, and genuine economic-development goals. But the plan was prompted by, and is built around, a single identified private beneficiary, which is the scenario Justice Kennedy flagged as possibly warranting ``a more stringent standard of review.'' Whether a court finds a public purpose or a pretextual private transfer depends on how it weighs the planning process against the team's central role.
```

## 256_cedar_point_nursery_v_hassid

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A California rule letting union organizers enter farms for up to three hours a day, 120 days a year, was held 6--3 to be a \emph{per se} physical taking: a regulation that appropriates the owner's right to exclude for third parties requires just compensation, however intermittent the access, subject to limited exceptions (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/594/20-107/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} the Ninth Circuit's decision (923 F.3d 524) by a \textbf{6--3} vote. The case was argued on March 22, 2021, and decided on June 23, 2021 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/594/20-107/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/20-107}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Chief Justice Roberts delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Thomas, Alito, Gorsuch, Kavanaugh, and Barrett.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Kavanaugh filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Breyer filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Sotomayor and Kagan.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Kavanaugh

Justice Breyer
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Per Se Taking): Wildlife Surveys on Fenced Timberland.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Per Se Taking): Canvassers in a Gated Community.} A city ordinance requires the owners' association of every private, gated residential community to admit registered political canvassers two weekends a month during election season so they can go door to door, and fines associations that refuse. The streets are private and closed to the public. Under \textit{Cedar Point}, the ordinance does not regulate how owners use their land; it grants third parties a right to physically enter it, appropriating the owners' right to exclude. That the access is limited to a few weekends affects only the amount of compensation. \textit{PruneYard} does not help the city, because \textit{Cedar Point} confined it to property generally open to the public, and no exception applies: canvassing is not a background common-law privilege, and the access is not a condition on a government benefit.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Per Se Taking): Licensing Inspections and a Zoning Setback.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Per Se Taking): A Highway Crew's One-Time Intrusion.} While repaving a state highway, a state road crew mistakenly parks its trucks and stores gravel on an adjoining farmer's field for two days, damaging part of a crop, and then leaves. No statute or regulation gives the State any right of access to the field. The farmer claims a \emph{per se} physical taking under \textit{Cedar Point}. The claim fails under the Court's first limiting boundary: an isolated physical invasion not undertaken under a granted right of access is ordinarily a trespass, remedied in tort, not an appropriation of the right to exclude. If the intrusion recurred or became part of a deliberate government program, the farmer could argue for a taking, and courts would weigh factors like those in \textit{Arkansas Game and Fish Comm'n v. United States}, 568 U.S. 23 (2012).

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unsettled): Union Organizers at a Supermarket.} A state statute gives union organizers a right to stand on the private sidewalk and parking lot of any large supermarket, for limited hours, to speak with employees and customers. The store is open to the shopping public every day, but its owner bars all solicitation. The owner argues that \textit{Cedar Point} makes the statute a \emph{per se} taking because it grants third parties a right of access the owner never consented to. The State responds that \textit{Cedar Point} itself distinguished \textit{PruneYard}, which allowed access to a shopping center generally open to the public. Yet organizers are not customers using the property as invited, and the \textit{Cedar Point} majority treated the right to exclude as a fundamental element of property that cannot be balanced away. How far \textit{PruneYard} survives is uncertain.
```


### Memory Jogger

```latex
Congress cut off food stamps to any household containing an unrelated member, aiming at ``hippie communes''; the Court held 7--2 that the classification failed even rational-basis review, because a bare congressional desire to harm a politically unpopular group is not a legitimate governmental interest (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/413/528/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{affirmed} the three-judge District Court by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued on April 23, 1973, and decided on June 25, 1973 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/413/528/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/413/528}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Brennan delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Douglas, Stewart, White, Marshall, Blackmun, and Powell.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinion:} Justice Douglas filed a concurring opinion (413 U.S.\ at 538--545).
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinion:} Justice Rehnquist filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Chief Justice Burger (413 U.S.\ at 545--547).
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
\item \textbf{Retrenchment (\textit{Trump v.\ Hawaii}):}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Invalid Animus): Excluding Protest Misdemeanants From Housing Vouchers.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Invalid Animus): Excluding Households With Tattoo Artists From Utility Relief.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Valid Under Deferential Review): A Neutral Unrelated-Occupant Zoning Limit.}

Unlike \textit{Moreno}, the ordinance

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Valid Under Deferential Review): A Work Requirement for Food Assistance.} Congress provides that able-bodied adults without dependents may receive food assistance for only three months in any three-year period unless they work or join a training program at least twenty hours a week. Challengers argue that the rule harms poor, unemployed adults. Unlike the 1971 amendment in \textit{Moreno}, the rule is tied directly to the program's purposes of helping those in need while encouraging self-sufficiency, the record shows no aim of harming an unpopular group, and the line it draws---between those who can work and those who cannot---bears a plausible relation to that goal. Under the deferential review of \textit{Dandridge v.\ Williams}, the rule survives even if it is a blunt instrument.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Mixed Motives): A Ban on Living in Vehicles.} A city bans sleeping in parked vehicles on public streets overnight, citing sanitation complaints and traffic safety. During debate, several council members describe the people affected in disparaging terms and say the city should make them leave. Under \textit{Moreno}, a bare desire to harm a politically unpopular group is not a legitimate interest, and the remarks resemble the anti-hippie statements in the Food Stamp Act's history. But sanitation and street safety are plausible, independent justifications, and \textit{Trump v.\ Hawaii} suggests that a policy survives unless it is inexplicable by anything but animus. Whether a court treats the hostile remarks as revealing the law's real purpose or as stray comments is genuinely uncertain.
```

## 258_shaw_v_reno

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Voters challenged North Carolina's snake-like, majority-Black District 12, drawn after a Justice Department objection; a 5--4 Court held that a redistricting plan so bizarre that it is unexplainable on grounds other than race states an Equal Protection claim triggering strict scrutiny, even without any dilution of a group's voting strength (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/509/630/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{5--4} vote. The case was argued on April 20, 1993, and decided on June 28, 1993 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/509/630/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice O'Connor delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justices Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice White filed a dissenting opinion, joined by Justices Blackmun and Stevens. Justices Blackmun, Stevens, and Souter each filed a separate dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice White

Justice Blackmun

Justice Stevens

Justice Souter
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Strict Scrutiny; District Invalid): A Barbell-Shaped Hispanic-Majority District.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Strict Scrutiny Triggered): A Mechanical Racial Target in a Regular-Looking District.} A legislature redraws a congressional district that had elected the minority community's preferred candidates by comfortable margins for twenty years. Mapmakers testify that they were instructed to raise the district's Black voting-age population above 50 percent, and to do so they move tens of thousands of Black voters in and out of the district, splitting several precincts, although the district's outline is not especially strange. \textit{Shaw}'s logic does not depend on a bizarre shape alone: once race predominates over traditional principles, strict scrutiny applies. In \textit{Cooper v.\ Harris}, 581 U.S.\ 285 (2017), the Court struck down a North Carolina district drawn to a 50-percent racial target where the State lacked a strong basis in evidence that the VRA required it (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/509/630/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (\textit{Shaw} Claim Fails): Partisan Line-Drawing.}

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (No Strict Scrutiny): Awareness of Race in a Compact District.} A State's redistricting commission draws a compact, contiguous district that follows county and city lines and keeps a recognized metropolitan area together. The district happens to be 55 percent Black, and the commission's records show that members knew its racial makeup and discussed it, but every boundary choice is explained by population equality, compactness, and respect for political subdivisions. A voter alleges a racial gerrymander. \textit{Shaw} acknowledged that legislatures are always aware of race when they redistrict; what triggers strict scrutiny is a plan that subordinates traditional districting principles to race. Because traditional criteria, not race, explain these lines, the district is reviewed deferentially and stands.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Compelling Interest Uncertain): A Remedial District Ordered After a VRA Finding.} After a federal court finds that a State's map violates \S~2 of the Voting Rights Act by diluting Black votes, the legislature draws a new, somewhat irregular majority-Black district, consciously using race to meet the court's remedy. White and Black voters inside the district bring a \textit{Shaw} challenge. Race plainly predominated, so strict scrutiny applies. \textit{Shaw} left open whether VRA compliance can be a compelling interest; later cases assumed it could when the State has a strong basis in evidence, and a prior judicial finding of a violation is strong evidence. But \textit{Callais}, as described above, narrowed that interest to \S~2 applied to intentional discrimination. Whether this remedial district survives depends on whether the earlier \S~2 finding rested on proof that meets that narrower standard and on whether the district is narrowly tailored---a genuinely open question.
```

## 259_chaplinsky_v_new_hampshire

### Memory Jogger

```latex
A Jehovah's Witness called a city marshal a ``damned racketeer'' and ``damned Fascist'' on a public street; a unanimous Court upheld his conviction, holding that ``fighting words,'' face-to-face epithets likely to provoke an average person to immediate violence, belong to narrow classes of speech the First Amendment does not protect (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/315/568/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{affirmed} the Supreme Court of New Hampshire, \textbf{9--0}. The case was argued on February~5, 1942, and decided on March~9, 1942 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/315/568/}{Justia}):

\item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Murphy delivered the opinion of the Court

\item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Fighting Words; Conviction Upheld): A Face-to-Face Challenge at a Protest.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Fighting Words; Conviction Upheld): Slurs Hurled at a Neighbor's Door.} After a dispute over a property line, a man walks onto his neighbor's porch, stands inches from her husband when he opens the door, calls him a string of personal slurs, and shoves a finger in his face while daring him to step outside and fight. He is convicted under the same narrowly construed breach-of-the-peace statute. The words are aimed at one listener, delivered face to face, and accompanied by an invitation to brawl, so they are the kind of personally abusive epithets that an average addressee would be likely to answer with violence. Because the statute reaches only such words and applies regardless of their subject, \textit{Chaplinsky} supports the conviction.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Protected Speech): Insults Through a Bullhorn and Online.} Suppose

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Ordinance Invalid): Banning Only Bias-Motivated Fighting Words.} A city makes it a crime to use fighting words that insult or provoke others on the basis of race, religion, or gender, but leaves untouched fighting words based on other topics, such as political affiliation or union membership. A defendant who directed a racial epithet at a neighbor in a face-to-face confrontation challenges the ordinance. Even if his words fit \textit{Chaplinsky}'s category, the city may not pick out a subset of fighting words for punishment because of the ideas they express. Under \textit{R.A.V.\ v.\ City of St.\ Paul}, the ordinance is unconstitutional content and viewpoint discrimination, although the city could instead ban all fighting words evenhandedly.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): Chaplinsky's Own Words Spoken Today.} During an arrest on a public street, a man faces the arresting officer and calls him a damned racketeer and a fascist, but makes no threat and takes no step toward him. He is convicted under a narrowly construed fighting-words statute. The words are personal, face to face, and identical to those \textit{Chaplinsky} said were plainly likely to provoke retaliation. Yet they are also political criticism of a public official, and Justice Powell's concurrence in \textit{Lewis} suggested that police officers are expected to show more restraint than ordinary citizens. Because \textit{Chaplinsky} has never been overruled but the Court has not upheld a fighting-words conviction since, the result is uncertain.
```

## 260_naacp_v_alabama

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Alabama tried to oust the NAACP and held it in contempt for refusing to hand over its rank-and-file membership lists; a unanimous Court held that compelled disclosure of members' identities, exposing them to reprisal without a compelling, closely related state interest, violates the freedom of association protected by the Fourteenth Amendment (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/357/449/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{reversed and remanded}, 9--0. The case was argued January 15--16, 1958, and decided June 30, 1958 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/357/449/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Harlan delivered the opinion of a unanimous Court, joined by Chief Justice Warren and Justices Black, Frankfurter, Douglas, Burton, Clark, Brennan, and Whittaker.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} None.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Disclosure Invalid): A Legislative Demand for an Advocacy Group's Members.} A state legislative committee investigating whether a small LGBTQ advocacy group in a rural county has lobbied without registering subpoenas the group's complete membership list. The group has already registered, produced its officers' names and its budget, and offered sworn testimony about its lobbying. It submits affidavits showing that members whose affiliation became known locally lost jobs and received threats. As in \textit{NAACP v.\ Alabama}, compelled disclosure would expose members to private reprisal made possible by state compulsion, and the rank-and-file names have no substantial bearing on the committee's stated purpose, which the group's other disclosures already serve. The subpoena cannot be enforced (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/357/449/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Disclosure Invalid): Teachers Required to List Every Association.} A State requires every public-school teacher, as a condition of employment, to file an affidavit listing every organization to which the teacher has belonged or contributed in the past five years. The State says it needs the information to evaluate teachers' fitness. Applying \textit{NAACP v.\ Alabama}, the Court struck down a nearly identical Arkansas law in \textit{Shelton v.\ Tucker}, 364 U.S.\ 479 (1960). The State may inquire into teachers' competence, but a demand for every association, with no limit to those relevant to fitness, reaches far beyond that interest and deters teachers from joining unpopular groups. Because disclosure is not closely tailored to the State's legitimate need, the requirement violates the freedom of association.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Disclosure Valid): Contributions to a Federal Candidate.} A federal statute requires every candidate's campaign committee to report the name and address of anyone who contributes more than \$200, and the reports are made public. A donors' group challenges the law on its face, invoking \textit{NAACP v.\ Alabama}, but offers no evidence that its supporters have faced threats or harassment. Under \textit{Buckley v.\ Valeo}, 424 U.S.\ 1 (1976), disclosure of contributions to candidates survives exacting scrutiny because it informs voters, deters corruption, and supports enforcement of contribution limits. \textit{NAACP v.\ Alabama} still matters at the margin: a minor party or group that shows a reasonable probability that disclosure will bring threats or reprisals can seek an as-applied exemption.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Disclosure Valid): Signers of a Referendum Petition.} Voters sign a petition to put a contested ballot measure before the electorate, and state law makes the signed petitions available to the public on request. Sponsors of the petition sue to block release of the signers' names, arguing that disclosure will chill participation. Under \textit{Doe v.\ Reed}, 561 U.S.\ 186 (2010), a facial challenge fails: signing a referendum petition is a public act in the lawmaking process, and disclosure serves the State's interest in detecting fraud and preserving the integrity of the election. Unlike the NAACP's members, the signers have not shown that disclosure will expose them to reprisal; if a particular group can make that showing, an as-applied challenge remains available.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Confidential Donor Lists and Foreign Influence): The Civic Transparency Act.}

For the group:

For the State:

Because the law is aimed at a weighty interest but sweeps in every small domestic donor, its fate turns on how strictly a court applies \textit{Bonta}'s tailoring requirement.
```

- Case Metadata: docket number, Chapter/Topic line, Cornell CONAN link.
- Concurring and Dissenting Opinions section ('None. ...'): restated in Judicial Votes; its commentary sentence on institutional solidarity dropped.
- Legacy section: introductory sentence (including the NAACP v. Alabama ex rel. Flowers parenthetical). Kept: Civil Rights Progeny item, Bonta item, First Choice Women's Resource Centers v. Davenport item.

## 261_tinker_v_des_moines

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Holding 7--2 that students do not ``shed their constitutional rights to freedom of speech or expression at the schoolhouse gate,'' the Court ruled that a school may not punish passive political expression, such as black armbands protesting the Vietnam War, unless it reasonably forecasts material and substantial disruption (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/393/503/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court \textbf{reversed and remanded} by a \textbf{7--2} vote. The case was argued on November 12, 1968, and decided on February 24, 1969 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/393/503/}{Justia}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Fortas delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Warren and Justices Douglas, Brennan, White, and Marshall.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Stewart and Justice White each filed a concurring opinion.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} Justice Black and Justice Harlan each filed a dissenting opinion.
\end{itemize}
```

### Holding

```latex
\textbf{Question Presented:}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Stewart

Justice White

Justice Black

Justice Harlan
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Student Wins): A ``Green Tyranny'' T-Shirt.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Student Wins): Lunchtime Leaflets About a City Council Meeting.} During the lunch period, a high school student hands classmates leaflets urging them to attend a city council meeting on local police policy. The leaflets are not vulgar, are not school-sponsored, and promote no illegal activity. A few students toss them aside; no class is interrupted. The principal suspends the student under a rule forbidding distribution of political material on campus, explaining that police policy is a divisive topic that could upset some families. Under \textit{Tinker}, the school must point to facts reasonably forecasting material and substantial interference with schoolwork or discipline, and a wish to avoid the discomfort of an unpopular viewpoint is not enough. Peaceful communication among students during lunch is the kind of intercommunication \textit{Tinker} treated as part of education, so the suspension violates the First Amendment.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (School Prevails): Documented Disruption and a Viewpoint-Neutral Ban.} Change two facts in the T-shirt example:

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (School Prevails): Editing the Journalism Class Newspaper.} Students in a high school journalism class write an article for the class's newspaper, which is produced with school funds under a teacher's supervision, describing named classmates' experiences with family divorce. The principal removes the article before publication, concerned that the families were not given a chance to respond and that the material is inappropriate for younger readers. \textit{Tinker}'s disruption test does not govern. Under \textit{Hazelwood School District v.\ Kuhlmeier}, 484 U.S.\ 260 (1988), a school may control the content of school-sponsored expressive activities, such as a curricular newspaper, when its actions are reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns. Because readers would attribute the newspaper to the school, the principal may edit it without proving any forecast of disruption.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Unsettled): A Weekend Post Mocking a Teacher.} On a Saturday, from home, a student posts on social media an edited photo of a teacher with a crude, demeaning caption, which spreads quickly among classmates. On Monday, students giggle and pass the image around during the teacher's class, and the teacher reports feeling humiliated. The school suspends the student. Under \textit{Mahanoy}, off-campus speech receives strong protection because the school's \textit{in loco parentis} role is diminished there, yet the Court left room for schools to address serious bullying or harassment of particular people. The school can point to some in-class distraction and to \textit{Tinker}'s undefined concern for the rights of others; the student can answer that the post was off-campus mockery that caused discomfort rather than material and substantial disruption. How courts strike that balance remains uncertain.
```

## 262_shurtleff_v_city_of_boston

### Memory Jogger

```latex
Boston had approved 284 straight private requests to fly flags on a City Hall flagpole but refused Camp Constitution's Christian flag; the Court held 9--0 that, because Boston did not actively control the flags' messages, the program was private speech, and the refusal was unconstitutional viewpoint discrimination (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/596/20-1800/}{Justia}).
```

### Judicial Votes

```latex
The Supreme Court unanimously \textbf{reversed and remanded} (9--0). The case was argued on January 18, 2022, and decided on May 2, 2022 (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/596/20-1800/}{Justia}; \href{https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/20-1800}{Cornell LII}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Majority Opinion:} Justice Breyer delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Sotomayor, Kagan, Kavanaugh, and Barrett.
    \item \textbf{Concurring Opinions:} Justice Kavanaugh filed a concurring opinion. Justice Alito filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justices Thomas and Gorsuch. Justice Gorsuch filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Justice Thomas.
    \item \textbf{Dissenting Opinions:} None.
\end{itemize}
```

### Analysis of Opinions

```latex
Justice Kavanaugh

Justice Alito

Justice Gorsuch
```

### Examples: Future Applications

```latex
\item \textbf{Same-Side (Private Speech; Exclusion Invalid): The Library's Community Heritage Wall.}

\item \textbf{Same-Side (Private Speech; Exclusion Invalid): Holiday Displays in the Town Green.} For fifteen years a town has issued a permit to any resident group that asks to place a seasonal display on its town green in December, without reviewing designs; it has approved nativity scenes, menorahs, and a local scout troop's snowman. When an atheist association applies to erect a sign celebrating the winter solstice and questioning religious belief, the town refuses, saying the sign is offensive. As in \textit{Shurtleff}, history alone cannot carry the day when the town has exercised no meaningful control over the displays' messages, so the green hosts private speech. Rejecting one group's display because of its views on religion is viewpoint discrimination that the Free Speech Clause forbids (\href{https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/596/20-1800/}{Justia}).

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Government Speech): A Closed-List Flag Policy.} After \textit{Shurtleff}, a city adopts a written policy that its third flagpole will fly only the city flag or flags chosen by city council resolution to express the city's own messages, and it stops accepting applications from private groups. A religious group later asks to raise its flag and is refused. The policy is the kind of change Justice Breyer said nothing prevented Boston from making: the city now actively selects and controls every flag, the public has no access to the pole, and the history of flags at seats of government already favors the city. The flags are government speech, so the Free Speech Clause does not require the city to accept the group's flag.

\item \textbf{Opposite-Side (Government Speech): Actively Reviewed Specialty License Plates.} A state motor-vehicle board reviews every proposed specialty license-plate design, routinely rejects designs it finds inconsistent with state messaging, and owns the designs it approves. The board rejects a proposed plate bearing an advocacy group's political slogan. Under \textit{Walker v.\ Texas Div., Sons of Confederate Veterans, Inc.}, 576 U.S.\ 200 (2015), which \textit{Shurtleff} distinguished, plates are traditionally used to convey state messages, observers associate them with the State, and the board actively controls their content. All three factors point to government speech, so the board may reject the design without satisfying viewpoint neutrality.

\item \textbf{Fence-Sitter (Close Call): A Juried Public Mural Program.} A city invites artists to propose murals for the walls of city-owned buildings. A city arts commission reviews each proposal for artistic quality and site compatibility and has approved about 90 percent of them, but it has no written guidelines on content. It rejects a proposal featuring religious imagery. The commission's review is more active than Boston's rubber stamp, and murals on public buildings may be read as the city's own message, which points toward government speech under \textit{Summum}. Yet the high approval rate, the absence of content standards, and the program's open invitation resemble \textit{Shurtleff}'s come-one-come-all forum. Because \textit{Shurtleff}'s holistic inquiry gives no fixed weight to each factor, the result could go either way.
```
